LVTD/Jev Benchmark

29 / 29 answers · 406 / 406 pairs · Judge: jev-1.13.0

The prompt

Let X_1, X_2, ... be independent Uniform(0,1) random variables, S_n = X_1 + ... + X_n, and N = min{n >= 1 : S_n > 2}. Derive exact closed forms for E[N] and Var(N), without simulation or numerical integration. First derive P(S_n <= 2) from a simplex- volume argument using inclusion-exclusion, carefully treating n=0, n=1 and boundary cases. Then justify the tail-sum identities for the first and second moments and evaluate the resulting infinite sums. Show the algebra, establish convergence, and give numerical sanity checks of the final constants. Finally generalize the expression for E[N_t] to an arbitrary real threshold t > 0, where N_t = min{n >= 1 : S_n > t}; a finite sum involving floor(t) and exponentials is acceptable. Keep the solution self-contained within 2,000 words and explicitly explain why strict versus non-strict threshold events do or do not matter.
Judging rubric

Prioritize a valid inclusion-exclusion derivation, correct low-n and boundary cases, justified moment identities and convergence, exact expectation and variance algebra, and a correct arbitrary- threshold generalization. Check numerical sanity and distinguish continuity for positive n from the deterministic n=0 case. Presentation alone cannot compensate for mathematical mistakes.

GLM 5.3 Prime

Requested model: z-ai/glm-5.3-prime · Output budget: 65536 tokens · Requested reasoning: provider default · Sep 28, 2026, 01:30 UTC

Reported answer cost: $0.5326900000 USD. Excludes retry and judging costs.

# Setup and statement of results

Let $S_0\equiv 0$, $p_n:=P(S_n\le 2)$, $N=\min\{n\ge 1:S_n>2\}$. The answers derived below are

$$E[N]=e^2-e\approx 4.6707742705,\qquad E[N^2]=5(e^2-e)\approx 23.3538713524,$$
$$\operatorname{Var}(N)=(e^2-e)(5-e^2+e)\approx 1.5377390667,\qquad 
E[N_t]=\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}(-1)^k\frac{e^{t-k}(t-k)^k}{k!}.$$

## 1. Reduction to tail probabilities

Each $X_i\ge 0$ a.s., so $(S_n)$ is a.s. nondecreasing. Hence, exactly,
$$\{N>n\}=\{S_1\le2,\dots,S_n\le2\}=\{S_n\le 2\}\qquad(n\ge0),$$
the last step by monotonicity (for $n=0$ both sides are $\Omega$). Also $N\ge3$ a.s., since $S_2\le 2$ a.s.

Since $(X_1,\dots,X_n)$ is uniform on $[0,1]^n$ (density $1$), $p_n$ is the $n$-dimensional Lebesgue volume of $R_n=\{x\in[0,1]^n:\sum_i x_i\le 2\}$.

## 2. $P(S_n\le 2)$: simplex volumes and inclusion–exclusion

**Simplex volume.** For $s\ge0$, $V_n(s):=\mathrm{vol}\{x\in[0,\infty)^n:\sum x_i\le s\}=s^n/n!$ by induction: $V_1(s)=s$, and slicing the last coordinate, $V_n(s)=\int_0^s V_{n-1}(s-u)\,du=s^n/n!$.

**Inclusion–exclusion.** $R_n=T_n(2)\setminus\bigcup_{i=1}^n E_i$, where $T_n(2)=\{x\ge0:\sum x_i\le 2\}$ and $E_i=\{x\ge0:\sum x_i\le2,\ x_i>1\}$. For $J\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}$ with $|J|=k$, translating $x_j\mapsto x_j-1$ ($j\in J$) gives
$$\mathrm{vol}\Big(\bigcap_{j\in J}E_j\Big)=V_n(2-k)=\tfrac{(2-k)^n}{n!}\ \text{if }2-k>0,\qquad 0\ \text{if }2-k\le 0 .$$

**Boundary cases.** For $k=2$ the intersection is $\{x_1=x_2=1,\ x_i=0\notin J\}$ — a single point, of $n$-volume $0$ for every $n\ge1$; hence the term $\binom n2 0^n$ vanishes (convention $0^n=0$ for $n\ge1$). For $k\ge3$ the intersection is empty. Changing "$x_i>1$" to "$x_i\ge1$", or "$\sum\le 2$" to "$\sum<2$", alters only hyperplane slices of volume $0$. Inclusion–exclusion (valid for any measure) yields, for $n\ge1$:
$$p_n=\sum_{k=0}^{2}(-1)^k\binom nk V_n(2-k)=\frac{2^n-n\cdot1^n+\binom n2\cdot 0^n}{n!}=\frac{2^n-n}{n!},$$
with $\binom nk=0$ for $k>n$.

**Small $n$ and checks.** $n=0$: $S_0\equiv0$, so $p_0=1$. $n=1$: $(2-1)/1!=1$ — correct, since $X_1<1<2$ a.s. (geometrically $T_1(2)=[0,2]$, $E_1=(1,2]$, difference $[0,1]$). $n=2$: $(4-2)/2!=1$ — correct, since $X_1+X_2\le 2$ a.s. (two strips of area $\tfrac12$, intersection a point). $n=3$: $(8-3)/3!=5/6$ — correct: $\{S_3>2\}$ becomes $\{y\ge0,\sum y_i<1\}$ under $y_i=1-x_i$, of volume $1/6$. Consistently, the finite-difference identity $\sum_{k=0}^n(-1)^k\binom nk(x-k)^n=n!$ shows the formula returns $1$ whenever $n\le 2$, as it must since $S_n\le n\le 2$ a.s.

## 3. Tail-sum identities and convergence

For integer-valued $N\ge1$,
$$N=\sum_{n\ge0}\mathbf 1\{N>n\},\qquad N^2=\sum_{n\ge0}(2n+1)\mathbf 1\{N>n\},$$
since the first sum counts $n=0,\dots,N-1$, and $\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}(2n+1)=N^2$. The partial sums increase pointwise to the left sides, so monotone convergence gives
$$E[N]=\sum_{n\ge0}p_n,\qquad E[N^2]=\sum_{n\ge0}(2n+1)p_n.\tag{1}$$

**Convergence.** $0\le p_n=(2^n-n)/n!\le 2^n/n!$, so $\sum p_n\le e^2<\infty$; likewise $\sum(2n+1)p_n\le\sum(2n+1)2^n/n!<\infty$ by the ratio test (its value $5e^2-1$ appears in §5). Hence both series converge absolutely, $N$ has finite moments, and every rearrangement below is justified. Moreover $p_n\to0$ and $\{N=\infty\}=\bigcap_n\{S_n\le2\}$, so $P(N<\infty)=1$.

## 4. $E[N]$

$$E[N]=1+\sum_{n=1}^\infty\Big(\frac{2^n}{n!}-\frac{n}{n!}\Big)=1+\big(e^2-1\big)-\big(e\big),$$
using $\sum_{n\ge1}x^n/n!=e^x-1$ and $\sum_{n\ge1}n/n!=\sum_{m\ge0}1/m!=e$. Hence
$$\boxed{E[N]=e^2-e=e(e-1)\approx 4.6707742705}.$$

## 5. $E[N^2]$ and $\operatorname{Var}(N)$

From (1), $E[N^2]=1+A-B$ with $A=\sum_{n\ge1}(2n+1)2^n/n!$, $B=\sum_{n\ge1}(2n+1)n/n!$. Since $\sum_{n\ge1}nx^n/n!=xe^x$ (shift $m=n-1$):
$$A=2\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{n2^n}{n!}+\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{2^n}{n!}=2(2e^2)+(e^2-1)=5e^2-1,$$
$$B=\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{2n+1}{(n-1)!}=\sum_{m\ge0}\frac{2m+3}{m!}=2e+3e=5e.$$
Therefore
$$\boxed{E[N^2]=1+(5e^2-1)-5e=5(e^2-e)=5E[N]\approx 23.3538713524},$$
and with $\mu:=e^2-e\approx4.6707742705$,
$$\operatorname{Var}(N)=5\mu-\mu^2=\mu(5-\mu)=e(e-1)(5+e-e^2)\approx 1.5377390667,\quad \sigma_N\approx1.24006 .$$

## 6. Numerical sanity checks (no simulation)

- Partial sums of $\sum p_n$: $1,2,3,3.8333,4.3333,4.5583,4.6389,4.6629,4.6690,4.6704,4.67071,4.67076,4.67077\to4.6707743$ (tail beyond $n=12$ is $<2\cdot10^{-6}$). Partial sums of $\sum(2n+1)p_n$: $1,4,9,14.8333,19.3333,21.8083,22.8556,23.2157,23.3203,23.3466,23.3525,23.3536,23.3538\to23.3538714$.
- Law of $N$: $P(N=n)=p_{n-1}-p_n$ gives $1/6,\,1/3,\,11/40,\,13/90,\dots$ — nonnegative and telescoping to $p_2=1$. Also $N\ge3$ a.s. and $E[N]\approx4.67>3$; $E[N^2]=23.3539>(4.67077)^2=21.8161$, so $\operatorname{Var}>0$.
- Benchmarks: at $t=1$ the same argument gives $E[N_1]=e\approx2.71828$ (the classical constant). Renewal asymptotics $E[N_t]\approx t/\bar\mu+E[X^2]/(2\bar\mu^2)=2t+\tfrac23$ (with $\bar\mu=\tfrac12$, $E[X^2]=\tfrac13$) predicts $4.6667$ at $t=2$ (exact: $4.6708$) and $6.66667$ at $t=3$ (exact: $6.66657$, see §8) — the correct trend.

## 7. Why strict vs. non-strict does not matter here

(i) The volumes in §2 are insensitive to $<$ vs. $\le$: all boundaries are hyperplane slices of measure zero, and the degenerate $k=2$ intersections are exactly such null sets. (ii) For $n\ge1$, $S_n$ is a continuous random variable (convolving the uniform law of $X_1$ with any distribution yields a law with a density), so $P(S_n=c)=0$ for every $c$; hence $P(S_n\le2)=P(S_n<2)$. (iii) Let $N'=\min\{n:S_n\ge2\}$. Then $\{N\neq N'\}\subseteq\bigcup_{n\ge1}\{S_n=2\}$, and by countable subadditivity (no independence needed) $P\big(\bigcup_n\{S_n=2\}\big)\le\sum_n P(S_n=2)=0$. Thus $N=N'$ a.s. and all moments agree. Likewise $\{N>n\}=\{S_n\le2\}$ exactly for the strict $N$, while $\{N'>n\}=\{S_n<2\}$, equal a.s. to $\{S_n\le2\}$.

So strictness is immaterial **only because of continuity**; with atomic increments (e.g. Bernoulli) the two definitions differ, and the vanishing boundary term $\binom n2 0^n$ would no longer vanish.

## 8. General threshold $t>0$

The same inclusion–exclusion (intersections of $k$ constraints have volume $V_n(t-k)$, nonempty only for $k<t$) gives the Irwin–Hall CDF for $n\ge1$:
$$F_n(t):=P(S_n\le t)=\frac1{n!}\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}(-1)^k\binom nk (t-k)^n,\tag{2}$$
with the same conventions ($\binom nk=0$ for $k>n$; the $k=\lfloor t\rfloor$ term is $0$ when $t\in\mathbb Z$). For $0\le t\le1$ this is the plain simplex $t^n/n!$; for $t\ge n$ the finite-difference identity returns $1$.

Since $\{N_t>n\}=\{S_n\le t\}$,
$$E[N_t]=\sum_{n=0}^\infty F_n(t)=1+\sum_{n=1}^\infty\frac1{n!}\sum_{k=0}^{m}(-1)^k\binom nk (t-k)^n,\qquad m:=\lfloor t\rfloor.\tag{3}$$

**Interchange.** For $k\le m$: $|\binom nk(t-k)^n/n!|\le n^m t^n/n!$ (using $\binom nk\le n^k/k!\le n^m$ and $0\le t-k\le t$), and $\sum_n n^m t^n/n!$ converges by the ratio test; the same holds with a factor $(2n+1)$. So (3) may be summed $k$-first.

**Key series.** Since $\binom nk/n!=1/(k!(n-k)!)$,
$$\sum_{n=1}^\infty\binom nk\frac{x^n}{n!}=\sum_{n=k}^\infty\frac{x^n}{k!(n-k)!}=\frac{x^k}{k!}e^x-\delta_{k0},$$
the subtraction deleting the $n=0$ term $\binom 0k\,x^0=\delta_{k0}$. Substituting $x=t-k$ into (3):
$$E[N_t]=1+\big(e^t-1\big)+\sum_{k=1}^{m}(-1)^k\frac{e^{t-k}(t-k)^k}{k!},$$
that is, the finite closed form
$$\boxed{\,E[N_t]=\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}(-1)^k\,\frac{e^{t-k}(t-k)^k}{k!}\,}.\tag{4}$$

**Checks.** $0<t\le1$: $E[N_t]=e^t=\sum_n t^n/n!$ (classical; $t=1$ gives $e$). $t=2$: $e^2-e\cdot1+e^0\cdot0^2/2!=e^2-e$ ✓ (§4). $t=3$: $e^3-2e^2+e/2\approx6.666566$, vs. $2t+\tfrac23=6.666667$ ✓. $t=2.5$: $e^{2.5}-\tfrac32e^{1.5}+\tfrac18e^{0.5}\approx5.666051$, between the $t=2,3$ values ✓. $E[N_t]$ is nondecreasing in $t$ and continuous across integers — as $t\to m^+$ the new $k=m$ term $e^{t-m}(t-m)^m/m!\to0$ — again the null boundary at work.

**Bonus (second moment).** Using $\sum_{n\ge1}n\binom nkx^n/n!=e^xx^k(x+k)/k!$ (split $n=(n-k)+k$), one gets $\sum_{n\ge1}(2n+1)\binom nkx^n/n!=e^xx^k(2x+2k+1)/k!-\delta_{k0}$; with $x=t-k$ the factor $2x+2k+1=2t+1$ is independent of $k$, so
$$E[N_t^2]=(2t+1)E[N_t],\qquad \operatorname{Var}(N_t)=E[N_t]\bigl(2t+1-E[N_t]\bigr),$$
recovering $E[N^2]=5\mu$ at $t=2$ (hence $\operatorname{Var}(N)=\mu(5-\mu)$, §5) and $3e$ at $t=1$.

**Summary.** $E[N]=e^2-e\approx4.6707742705$; $\operatorname{Var}(N)=(e^2-e)(5-e^2+e)\approx1.5377390667$; and for any real $t>0$, $E[N_t]=\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}(-1)^k e^{t-k}(t-k)^k/k!$, with $E[N_t^2]=(2t+1)E[N_t]$.
MiMo-V2.6-Flash

Requested model: xiaomi/mimo-v2.6-flash · Output budget: 65536 tokens · Requested reasoning: provider default · Sep 28, 2026, 08:33 UTC

Reported answer cost: $0.0060076800 USD. Excludes retry and judging costs.

# Setting

Let $X_1,X_2,\dots \stackrel{iid}{\sim}\mathrm{Unif}(0,1)$, $S_n=X_1+\dots+S_n$'s partial sums, $S_0=0$, and
$$N=\min\{n\ge 1: S_n>2\}.$$

---

## 1. The tail probabilities $F_n:=P(S_n\le 2)$

**Step 1 (truncated simplex volume).** For $n\ge1$, $t\ge0$,
$$P(S_n\le t)=\int_{[0,1]^n}\mathbf 1\{\textstyle\sum_i x_i\le t\}\,dx
=\int_{[0,\infty)^n}\mathbf 1\{\textstyle\sum_i x_i\le t\}\prod_{i=1}^n\mathbf 1\{x_i\le1\}\,dx .$$
Since $\mathbf 1\{x_i\le1\}=1-\mathbf 1\{x_i>1\}$ on $[0,\infty)$, expanding the product gives
$$\prod_i(1-\mathbf 1\{x_i>1\})=\sum_{K\subseteq[n]}(-1)^{|K|}\mathbf 1\{x_i>1\ \forall i\in K\}.$$
For $|K|=k$ substitute $y_i=x_i-1\ (i\in K)$, $y_i=x_i$ otherwise: the region becomes the full simplex $\{y\ge0,\ \sum y_i\le t-k\}$, of volume $(t-k)_+^n/n!$ (standard simplex volume $1/n!$, scaled by $(t-k)_+^n$). It is **empty** when $k>t$. There are $\binom nk$ such $K$. Hence the inclusion–exclusion identity
$$\boxed{P(S_n\le t)=\frac1{n!}\sum_{k=0}^{\min(n,\lfloor t\rfloor)}(-1)^k\binom nk (t-k)^n }\tag{1}$$
For $k>n$ we have $\binom nk=0$, so (1) also automatically yields $1$ when $t\ge n$ (whole cube inside). Note: the *closed* simplex was integrated; the hyperplane $\sum x_i=t$ has Lebesgue measure $0$, so open vs. closed is immaterial here.

**Step 2 (boundary cases).** Take $t=2$, so $\lfloor t\rfloor=2$:

* $n=0$: $S_0=0$, define $F_0:=P(S_0\le2)=1$ (empty sum).
* $n=1$: (1) gives $\tfrac1{1!}\big[2^1-1\cdot1^1\big]=1$ — correct, $X_1<1\le2$.
* $n=2$: $\tfrac1{2!}\big[2^2-2\cdot1^2+(2-2)^2\big]=1$ — correct, $S_2<2$ a.s.
* $n\ge3$: the $k=2$ term contains $0^n=0$, so
$$\boxed{F_n=P(S_n\le2)=\frac{2^n-n}{n!}\ (n\ge1),\qquad F_0=1.}\tag{2}$$

Checks: $F_3=\frac{8-3}{6}=\frac56$ (Irwin–Hall: $P(S_3>2)=\frac{(3-2)^3}{6}=\frac16$ ✓); $F_4=\frac{16-4}{24}=\frac12$ (symmetry of the $n=4$ Irwin–Hall density about its mean $2$ ✓); $F_5=\frac{32-5}{120}=0.225$; $F_6=\frac{58}{720}=0.08056$.

---

## 2. Why strict vs. non-strict does not change numbers (but must be written correctly)

By monotonicity of $S_\cdot$,
$$\{N>n\}\iff S_j\le2\ \forall j\le n\iff S_n\le 2,\qquad \{N\le n\}\iff S_n>2 .\tag{3}$$
The first equivalence is an **exact set identity using $\le$**; writing $\{N>n\}=\{S_n<2\}$ is only true modulo a null set.

*Why null:* for every $n\ge1$, $S_n$ is the sum of $n$ uniforms, so it has the Irwin–Hall density $f_1*\dots*f_1$, a bounded piecewise-polynomial (hence absolutely continuous) function. Therefore $P(S_n=2)=0$ for **all** $n\ge1$, and
$$P(S_n>2)=P(S_n\ge2),\qquad P(S_n\le2)=P(S_n<2).$$
So strict/non-strict choices change no probability; they *would* matter if the law of $S_n$ had an atom at $2$ (e.g. lattice variables), or at $n=0$, where $S_0=0$ is a genuine atom: we must use $F_0=P(S_0\le2)=1$, and indeed $P(S_0<2)=1$ too, so even there both agree — but only because $0<2$.

Also from (3): $\{N\le2\}=\{S_2>2\}$ has probability $0$, so **$N\ge3$ a.s.**

---

## 3. Tail-sum identities and convergence

$N$ is a.s. finite: $F_n\le 2^n/n!\to0$, so $P(N=\infty)=\lim_n P(N>n)=0$; and by (3), $P(N>n)=F_n$.

For an a.s. finite positive integer r.v. with $\sum_n P(N>n)<\infty$:
$$E[N]=\sum_{n\ge0}P(N>n),\qquad
N^2=\sum_{k=1}^{N}(2k-1)\Rightarrow E[N^2]=\sum_{k\ge1}(2k-1)P(N\ge k)=\sum_{n\ge0}(2n+1)P(N>n),$$
all sums of nonnegative terms, so Tonelli justifies term-by-term summation. **Absolute convergence:** by (2), $\sum_{n\ge0}F_n\le\sum_n 2^n/n!=e^2<\infty$ and $\sum_n (2n+1)F_n\le\sum_n(2n+1)2^n/n!<\infty$; also $F_n\sim 2^n/n!$ has ratio $2/(n+1)<1$ for $n\ge2$, so the tails are geometric-like.

---

## 4. Evaluating the sums

**Mean.**
$$E[N]=F_0+\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{2^n-n}{n!}
=1+\underbrace{\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{2^n}{n!}}_{e^2-1}-\underbrace{\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{n}{n!}}_{\sum_{m\ge0}1/m!=e}=e^2-e .$$

**Second moment.**
$$E[N^2]=1+\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{(2n+1)(2^n-n)}{n!}
=1+\underbrace{\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{2n\,2^n}{n!}}_{2\sum_{m\ge0}2^{m+1}/m!=4e^2}+\underbrace{\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{2^n}{n!}}_{e^2-1}-\underbrace{\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{2n^2+n}{n!}}_{2\sum(m+1)/m!+\sum 1/m!=4e+e=5e}$$
$$E[N^2]=1+4e^2+e^2-1-5e=5(e^2-e)=5\,E[N].$$

**Variance.**
$$\operatorname{Var}(N)=5(e^2-e)-(e^2-e)^2=e(e-1)\big(5+e-e^2\big).$$

---

## 5. Numerical sanity checks

$e=2.7182818,\ e^2=7.3890561$:

$$E[N]=e^2-e=4.6707743,\qquad \operatorname{Var}(N)=1.5377389,\qquad \mathrm{SD}(N)=1.2400558 .$$

Partial sums $\sum_{n\le N}F_n$: $3,\ 3.8333,\ 4.3333,\ 4.5583,\ 4.6389,\ 4.6629,\ 4.6690,\ 4.6704,\ 4.67069\ \to 4.6707743$ ✓
Partial sums $\sum_{n\le N}(2n+1)F_n$: $1,4,9,14.833,19.333,21.808,22.856,23.216,23.320,23.347,23.353,23.3539\to23.3538715=5(e^2-e)$ ✓

Laws $P(N=n)=F_{n-1}-F_n$: $0,\,0,\,\tfrac16,\,\tfrac13,\,0.275,\,0.1444,\,0.0565,\,0.0179,\,0.0048,\dots$ — these sum to $1$ and re-give $4.6708$; $N\ge3$ a.s. as predicted; $E[N^2]=5E[N]$ implies $E[N(N-1)]=4E[N]=18.683$.

---

## 6. General threshold $t>0$

Let $N_t=\min\{n\ge1:S_n>t\}$, $m=\lfloor t\rfloor$. Exactly as in §2, $\{N_t>n\}=\{S_n\le t\}$, and $P(S_n=t)=0$ for $n\ge1$ (same density argument), so $\le$ vs $<$ is immaterial numerically. With (1) and $P(S_0\le t)=1$:
$$E[N_t]=1+\sum_{n\ge1}\frac1{n!}\sum_{k=0}^{m}(-1)^k\binom nk (t-k)^n
=1+\sum_{k=0}^{m}\frac{(-1)^k}{k!}\sum_{n\ge k}(t-k)^n\frac{1}{(n-k)!}.$$
With $a_k=t-k>0$ and $j=n-k$:
$$\sum_{n\ge k}\frac{a_k^n}{(n-k)!}=\frac{a_k^k}{1}\sum_{j\ge0}\frac{a_k^{j}}{j!}=a_k^k e^{a_k}\quad(k\ge1),\qquad \sum_{n\ge1}\frac{t^n}{n!}=e^{t}-1\ (k=0).$$
The $+1$ cancels the $-1$ of the $k=0$ term:
$$\boxed{\;E[N_t]=\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}(-1)^k\,\frac{e^{\,t-k}(t-k)^k}{k!}\;}\tag{4}$$
(with $0^0=1$, and the top term $k=m$ vanishing when $t\in\mathbb Z$ since $(t-k)^k=0$).

*Checks:* $t=2$: $e^2-e+0=4.67077$ ✓. $t=1$: $e-e^0\cdot0= e=\sum 1/n!$ ✓. $t\in(0,1)$: $E[N_t]=e^t$; e.g. $t=0.5$: $1+0.5+0.125+0.02083+\dots=1.648721=\sqrt e$ ✓. $t=1.5$: $e^{1.5}-\tfrac12e^{0.5}=3.65733$, matching the partial sum $1+1+0.875+0.5+0.2005+0.06198+\dots=3.65733$ ✓. $t=3$: $e^3-2e^2+\tfrac12e=6.666566$, matching $4+0.9583+0.775+0.5+0.2603+\dots=6.66656$ ✓.

**Bonus (same machinery):** $\sum_{n\ge0}(2n+1)\frac{(t-k)^n}{k!(n-k)!}=\frac{e^{a_k}a_k^k}{k!}(2a_k+2k+1)=\frac{e^{a_k}a_k^k}{k!}(2t+1)$, hence
$$E[N_t^2]=(2t+1)E[N_t],\qquad \operatorname{Var}(N_t)=E[N_t]\big(2t+1-E[N_t]\big),$$
consistent with $t=2$: $5(e^2-e)=23.3539$ and $4.6708(5-4.6708)=1.5377$.

---

## Final answer

$$E[N]=e^{2}-e\approx 4.670774,\qquad \operatorname{Var}(N)=5(e^2-e)-(e^2-e)^2=e(e-1)(5+e-e^2)\approx 1.537739\ \ (\mathrm{SD}\approx1.2401),$$

with $P(N>n)=\dfrac{2^n-n}{n!}$ for $n\ge1$ ($P(N>0)=P(N>1)=P(N>2)=1$), and in general
$$E[N_t]=\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}(-1)^k\frac{e^{t-k}(t-k)^k}{k!}.$$

Requested model: meta/muse-spark-1.3-contributor · Output budget: 65536 tokens · Requested reasoning: provider default · Sep 28, 2026, 09:20 UTC

Reported answer cost: $0.0034759000 USD. Excludes retry and judging costs.

Let $X_{i}$ be i.i.d. $U(0,1)$, $S_{n}=X_{1}+\cdots+X_{n}$,
$S_{0}=0$, and for $t>0$
$$N_{t}=\min\{n\ge 1:S_{n}>t\}.$$
We study $t=2$, $N=N_{2}$. $X_{i}\ge 0$ surely, $>0$ a.s.,
so $S_{n}$ is nondecreasing surely, strictly increasing a.s.

Strict vs non-strict: $\{N_{t}>n\}=\cap_{k=1}^{n}\{S_{k}\le t\}
=\{S_{n}\le t\}$ surely, since $\max_{k\le n}S_{k}=S_{n}$.
Indeed $N_{t}>n$ means no $k\le n$ has $S_{k}>t$.
For $n=0$, $\{N_{t}>0\}$ is the whole space, and $S_{0}=0\le t$.
Hence, exactly,
$$P(N_{t}>n)=P(S_{n}\le t).$$
If $N_{t}'=\min\{n:S_{n}\ge t\}$, then $P(N_{t}'>n)=P(S_{n}<t)$.
For $n\ge 1$, $S_{n}$ is continuous, so $P(S_{n}=t)=0$.
Hence $P(S_{n}\le t)=P(S_{n}<t)$.
The only possible atom is $n=0$, $S_{0}=0$ deterministic.
For $t>0$, $P(S_{0}\le t)=P(S_{0}<t)=1$.
Thus for $t>0$ strict/non-strict thresholds give the same
tail probabilities and same moments. Moreover
$P(\cup_{n}\{S_{n}=t\})=0$, so $N_{t}=N_{t}'$ a.s.

### 1. Distribution of $S_{n}$

For $n\ge 1$, $(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})$ has density $1$ on $[0,1]^{n}$.
Hence for $x$ real,
$$F_{n}(x):=P(S_{n}\le x)=\text{Vol}_{n}\{y\in[0,1]^{n}:\sum y_{i}\le x\}.$$
If $x<0$, $0$; if $x\ge n$, $1$, since $\sum y_{i}\le n$ a.s.

Assume $n\ge 1$, $x\ge 0$. First the positive orthant simplex:
$$V_{n}(x)=\text{Vol}\{y_{i}\ge 0,\sum y_{i}\le x\}=x^{n}/n!.$$
Indeed $V_{1}(x)=x$, and
$V_{n}(x)=\int_{0}^{x}V_{n-1}(x-y)dy$, giving induction
$x^{n}/n!$.

We need additionally $y_{i}\le 1$. Let $A_{i}=\{y\ge0,
\sum y\le x, y_{i}>1\}$. Desired volume is $V_{n}(x)-{\rm Vol}(\cup_{i}A_{i})$.
By inclusion-exclusion for finite measures,
$${\rm Vol}(\cup A_{i})=\sum_{k\ge1}(-1)^{k+1}\sum_{|K|=k}
{\rm Vol}(\cap_{i\in K}A_{i}).$$
By symmetry only $k=|K|$ matters, with $\binom nk$ sets.
Fix $K$, $|K|=k$. If $y_{i}>1$ for $i\in K$, write $z_{i}=y_{i}-1\ge0$
for $i\in K$, $z_{i}=y_{i}$ else. Then $\sum z_{i}\le x-k$.
If $x-k<0$ this is empty, volume $0$. If $x-k\ge0$, volume
$(x-k)^{n}/n!$ by simplex formula (the strict $>0$ vs $\ge0$
is a null boundary). Hence
$${\rm Vol}(\cap_{K}A)=\frac{(x-k)_{+}^{n}}{n!},$$
where $a_{+}=\max(a,0)$. Therefore
$$F_{n}(x)=\frac1{n!}\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^{k}\binom nk (x-k)_{+}^{n},
\quad n\ge1.$$
Since $(x-k)_{+}=0$ for $k>x$, sum can stop at $\lfloor x\rfloor$
if $0\le x<n$. This is Irwin-Hall. It is continuous in $x$,
piecewise polynomial, so $P(S_{n}=x)=0$.

Treat $n=0$ separately: $S_{0}=0$, $F_{0}(x)={\bf1}_{\{x\ge0\}}$.
The formula with $n=0$ would involve $0^{0}$; avoid it.

Take $x=2$. Let $p_{n}=P(S_{n}\le2)$.
$p_{0}=1$. For $n=1$: $F_{1}(2)=P(X_{1}\le2)=1$ a.s.
since $X_{1}\le1$. Formula gives $2-1=1$.
For $n=2$: $S_{2}\le2$ a.s., so $p_{2}=1$.
Formula: $\frac12[2^{2}-2\cdot1^{2}]=1$.
For $n\ge3$: $\lfloor2\rfloor=2$, so
$$p_{n}=\frac1{n!}\Bigl[2^{n}-n\cdot1^{n}+\frac{n(n-1)}2 0^{n}\Bigr].$$
For $n\ge1$, $0^{n}=0$. Hence
$$p_{n}=\frac{2^{n}-n}{n!},\quad n\ge3.$$
Check: $n=3$: $(8-3)/6=5/6$; $n=4$: $(16-4)/24=1/2$;
$n=5$: $27/120=0.225$. Decays factorially.

### 2. Tail-sum moments

For integer $N\ge0$, $m\ge1$,
$$m=\sum_{n=0}^{m-1}1,\qquad m^{2}=\sum_{n=0}^{m-1}(2n+1).$$
Hence $N=\sum_{n\ge0}{\bf1}_{\{N>n\}}$,
$N^{2}=\sum_{n\ge0}(2n+1){\bf1}_{\{N>n\}}$.
By Tonelli (nonnegative terms),
$$E[N]=\sum_{n\ge0}P(N>n),\quad E[N^{2}]=\sum_{n\ge0}(2n+1)P(N>n),$$
allowing $+\infty$. Convergence will follow from factorial tails.

Here $P(N>n)=p_{n}$. Thus, if summable,
$$E[N]=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}p_{n},\quad
E[N^{2}]=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(2n+1)p_{n}.$$

Series $\sum a^{n}/n!$ converges absolutely for any $a$
(ratio $\to0$) to $e^{a}$. Also $\sum n/n!$, $\sum n^{2}/n!$
converge. So all below converge absolutely.

Compute $E[N]$:
$$E[N]=1+1+1+\sum_{n=3}^{\infty}\frac{2^{n}-n}{n!}.$$
$\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}2^{n}/n!=e^{2}$, so
$\sum_{n\ge3}2^{n}/n!=e^{2}-1-2-2=e^{2}-5$.
$\sum_{n\ge3}n/n!=\sum_{n\ge3}1/(n-1)!
=\sum_{m\ge2}1/m!=e-2$.
Hence
$$E[N]=3+(e^{2}-5)-(e-2)=e^{2}-e=e(e-1).$$
Numerically $e\approx2.71828$, $E[N]\approx4.67077$.

Compute $E[N^{2}]$. Terms $n=0,1,2$: $(2n+1)p_{n}=1,3,5$,
sum $9$. For $n\ge3$:
$$E[N^{2}]=9+\sum_{n\ge3}(2n+1)\frac{2^{n}}{n!}
-\sum_{n\ge3}(2n+1)\frac{n}{n!}:=9+S_{1}-S_{2}.$$

$S_{1}$: $\sum_{n\ge0}(2n+1)2^{n}/n!
=2\sum n2^{n}/n!+\sum2^{n}/n!$.
$\sum_{n\ge1}n2^{n}/n!=\sum_{n\ge1}2^{n}/(n-1)!
=2e^{2}$. So total $=4e^{2}+e^{2}=5e^{2}$.
Remove $n=0,1,2$: $1$, $3\cdot2=6$, $5\cdot4/2=10$,
total $17$. So $S_{1}=5e^{2}-17$.

$S_{2}$: $(2n+1)n/n!=(2n+1)/(n-1)!$.
Write $2n+1=2(n-1)+3$. So for $n\ge3$,
$$S_{2}=2\sum_{n\ge3}\frac1{(n-2)!}+3\sum_{n\ge3}\frac1{(n-1)!}
=2\sum_{m\ge1}\frac1{m!}+3\sum_{m\ge2}\frac1{m!}$$
$$=2(e-1)+3(e-2)=5e-8.$$
Thus
$$E[N^{2}]=9+5e^{2}-17-(5e-8)=5e^{2}-5e=5e(e-1).$$
$\approx23.3539$.

Hence
$${\rm Var}(N)=5e^{2}-5e-(e^{2}-e)^{2}
=e(e-1)[5-e(e-1)].$$
Numerically $\approx1.5379$, sd $\approx1.24$.

Sanity: $N=1,2$ have probability $0$ ($S_{2}\le2$ a.s.).
$P(N=3)=1-5/6=1/6$, $P(N=4)=5/6-1/2=1/3$,
$P(N=5)=1/2-27/120=0.275$, $P(N=6)\approx0.144$,
summing $\approx0.92$, mean contribution $\approx4.08$ plus
tail $\approx0.59$, matching $4.67$.
Also $E[S_{N}]=E[N]/2\approx2.335$ by Wald, so mean overshoot
$\approx0.335$, very close to asymptotic $E[X^{2}]/2E[X]=1/3$
for $U(0,1)$. This strongly supports the constant.

### 3. General threshold $t>0$

Let $m=\lfloor t\rfloor$, $F_{n}(t)=P(S_{n}\le t)$.
For $n\le t$ (i.e. $n\le m$), $F_{n}=1$ since $S_{n}\le n\le t$.
Indeed $F_{0}=1$. So
$$E[N_{t}]=\sum_{n\ge0}F_{n}=(m+1)+\sum_{n=m+1}^{\infty}F_{n}.$$
For $n>m$,
$$F_{n}=\frac1{n!}\sum_{k=0}^{m}(-1)^{k}\binom nk(t-k)^{n}.$$
Write $\binom nk/n!=1/[k!(n-k)!]$, $y_{k}=t-k$:
$$F_{n}=\sum_{k=0}^{m}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}\,y_{k}^{n}\frac1{(n-k)!}.$$
Factor $y_{k}^{n}=y_{k}^{k}y_{k}^{\,n-k}$. Let $j=n-k$:
$$\sum_{n\ge m+1}y_{k}^{n}/(n-k)!
=y_{k}^{k}\sum_{j\ge m+1-k}y_{k}^{j}/j!
=y_{k}^{k}\Bigl(e^{y_{k}}-\sum_{j=0}^{m-k}y_{k}^{j}/j!\Bigr).$$
Interchange of finite $k$-sum and convergent $n$-series is justified.
Hence
$$E[N_{t}]=m+1+\sum_{k=0}^{m}\frac{(-1)^{k}y_{k}^{k}}{k!}e^{y_{k}}-B,$$
$$B=\sum_{k=0}^{m}\frac{(-1)^{k}y_{k}^{k}}{k!}\sum_{j=0}^{m-k}\frac{y_{k}^{j}}{j!}.$$

Claim $B=m+1$, leaving a pure exponential-polynomial sum.
Indeed put $n=j+k$. Then $0\le n\le m$, $0\le k\le n$:
$$B=\sum_{n=0}^{m}\frac1{n!}\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^{k}\binom nk(t-k)^{n}.$$
Lemma: for any $t$ and $n\ge0$,
$\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^{k}\binom nk(t-k)^{n}=n!$.
Then each $n$-term is $1$, $B=m+1$.

Lemma proof: expand $(t-k)^{n}=\sum_{i=0}^{n}\binom ni t^{n-i}(-k)^{i}$.
Need $S_{n,i}:=\sum_{k}(-1)^{k}\binom nk k^{i}$.
Consider $(1-e^{s})^{n}=\sum_{k}\binom nk(-1)^{k}e^{ks}
=\sum_{i}S_{n,i}s^{i}/i!$. But $1-e^{s}=-s+O(s^{2})$,
so $(1-e^{s})^{n}=(-1)^{n}s^{n}+O(s^{n+1})$.
Comparing coefficients: $S_{n,i}=0$ for $i<n$,
$=(-1)^{n}n!$ for $i=n$. Then only $i=n$ survives in the
expansion, giving $(-1)^{n}(-1)^{n}n!=n!$.

Therefore, for $t>0$, $m=\lfloor t\rfloor$,
$$E[N_{t}]=\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}(t-k)^{k}e^{\,t-k}.$$
This is finite, involving only $\lfloor t\rfloor$ and exponentials.
For $0<t\le1$, $m=0$, $E=e^{t}$. For $1<t\le2$,
$E=e^{t}-(t-1)e^{t-1}$. At $t=2$ this is $e^{2}-e$.
For $2<t\le3$, add $(t-2)^{2}e^{t-2}/2$, etc.
When $t-k=0$, $k>0$, interpret $0^{k}=0$; continuity across
integers holds since that term vanishes. ∎
MiMo V2.6 Pro

Requested model: xiaomi/mimo-v2.6-pro · Output budget: 65536 tokens · Requested reasoning: provider default · Sep 28, 2026, 01:19 UTC

Reported answer cost: $0.0205032900 USD. Excludes retry and judging costs.

# Setup and notation

Let $X_1,X_2,\dots$ be i.i.d. $\mathrm{U}(0,1)$, $S_0:=0$, $S_n=\sum_{i=1}^nX_i$, and
$$N=\min\{n\ge 1:\ S_n>2\}.$$
Since $S_n/n\to \tfrac12$ a.s. (SLLN), $S_n\uparrow\infty$ a.s., so $N<\infty$ a.s. Moreover $X_i>0$ a.s., so $n\mapsto S_n$ is strictly increasing; hence for $n\ge 1$
$$\{N>n\}=\{S_1\le 2,\dots,S_n\le 2\}=\{S_n\le 2\},\qquad \{N>0\}=\Omega. \tag{1}$$

---

# 1. The tail probability $P(S_n\le t)$ by simplex volume + inclusion–exclusion

For $t>0$ put $V_n(t)=\mathrm{vol}\{x\in\mathbb R^n:\ x_i\ge0,\ \sum x_i\le t,\ x_i\le 1\}$. Because the joint density of $(X_1,\dots,X_n)$ is $\mathbf 1_{[0,1]^n}$, $P(S_n\le t)=V_n(t)$.

The unrestricted simplex $\{x\ge0,\sum x_i\le t\}$ has volume $t^n/n!$. Impose $x_i\le 1$ by inclusion–exclusion over $A_i=\{x_i>1\}$: for a set $S$ with $|S|=k$, substituting $y_i=x_i-1$ ($i\in S$) gives
$$\mathrm{vol}\{x\ge0,\ \sum x_i\le t,\ x_i\ge 1\ (i\in S)\}=\frac{\big((t-k)^+\big)^n}{n!}.$$
Hence, with $C(n,k)=0$ for $k>n$ and $(t-k)^n=0$ for $t<k$,
$$\boxed{\,P(S_n\le t)=\frac1{n!}\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}(-1)^k\binom nk (t-k)^n\,}\tag{2}$$
(the Irwin–Hall CDF).

**Boundary/convention checks.**
* $n=0$: $S_0=0$, so $P(S_0\le t)=1$; formula (2) with $0^0=1$ also gives $1$. We nevertheless treat $n=0$ separately (below $0^n=0$ is used only for $n\ge1$).
* $t\ge n$: the whole cube is admissible and (2) must return $1$ (identity $\sum_k(-1)^k\binom nk(t-k)^n=n!$ for $t\ge n$). E.g. $n=1$: $\min(t,1)$; $n=2,t=2$: $\frac12(4-2+0)=1$.
* $n=1,2$ with $t=2$ give $1$, as they must, since $S_1<1<2$, $S_2<2$ a.s.

**Case $t=2$.** For $n\ge1$ only $k=0,1,2$ contribute, and the $k=2$ term contains $(2-2)^n=0$:
$$P(S_n\le 2)=\frac{2^n-n\cdot 1^n+\binom n2 0^n}{n!}=\frac{2^n-n}{n!},\qquad n\ge 1,\tag{3}$$
and $P(S_0\le2)=1$ (consistent with $(2^0-0)/0!=1$). Checks: $n=1:\frac{2-1}{1}=1$; $n=2:\frac{4-2}{2}=1$; $n=3:\frac56$ (indeed $P(S_3>2)=P(S_3<1)=\frac16$ by symmetry); $n=4:\frac12$.

By (1): $P(N>n)=\frac{2^n-n}{n!}$ for $n\ge1$, $P(N>0)=1$, and
$$P(N=n)=P(N>n-1)-P(N>n)=\frac{(n-2)\big(2^{n-1}-n\big)}{n!},\qquad n\ge3,$$
$P(N=n)=0$ for $n\le 2$ (as it must: $S_2<2$ a.s. $\Rightarrow N\ge3$).

---

# 2. Tail-sum identities

For integer-valued $N\ge1$, $N=\sum_{n\ge0}\mathbf 1_{\{N>n\}}$ and $N^2=\sum_{k=1}^{N}(2k-1)=\sum_{n\ge0}(2n+1)\mathbf 1_{\{N>n\}}$. All summands are nonnegative, so Tonelli justifies exchanging expectation and summation:
$$E[N]=\sum_{n\ge0}P(N>n),\qquad E[N^2]=\sum_{n\ge0}(2n+1)P(N>n).\tag{4}$$

**Convergence.** $P(N>n)=\frac{2^n-n}{n!}\le \frac{2^n}{n!}$, and the ratio of consecutive terms tends to $\frac{2}{n+1}\to0$; likewise $\frac{(2(n+1)+1)(2^{n+1}-\cdots)/(n+1)!}{(2n+1)(2^n-n)/n!}\sim\frac{2}{n+1}\to0$. So both series in (4) converge absolutely (indeed super-geometrically).

---

# 3. Evaluating the sums

We need, with $e^z=\sum_{n\ge0}z^n/n!$:
$$\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{2^n}{n!}=e^2-1,\quad \sum_{n\ge1}\frac{n\,2^n}{n!}=\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{2^n}{(n-1)!}=2e^2,\quad \sum_{n\ge1}\frac{n}{n!}=e,\quad \sum_{n\ge1}\frac{n^2}{n!}=\sum_{m\ge0}\frac{m+1}{m!}=2e .$$

**First moment.**
$$E[N]=1+\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{2^n-n}{n!}=1+(e^2-1)-e=\boxed{e^2-e=e(e-1)\approx 4.6707742705 }.$$

**Second moment.**
$$E[N^2]=1+\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{(2n+1)(2^n-n)}{n!}=1+\Big[2(2e^2)+(e^2-1)\Big]-\Big[2(2e)+e\Big]=5e^2-5e=5e(e-1)\approx 23.35387135 .$$

**Variance.**
$$\mathrm{Var}(N)=5e(e-1)-\big(e(e-1)\big)^2=e(e-1)\Big(5-e(e-1)\Big)\approx 1.53773907,\qquad \mathrm{sd}(N)\approx1.240056 .$$
Equivalently $\mathrm{Var}(N)=E[N]\big(5-E[N]\big)$.

---

# 4. Sanity checks

*Truncations.* $\sum_{n=0}^{N}P(N>n)$: $3,3.8333,4.3333,4.5583,4.6389,4.6629,4.6690,4.67043,4.67071,4.67076,\dots\to4.670774$.
For $E[N^2]$: $9,14.8333,19.3333,21.8083,22.8556,23.2157,23.3202,23.3466,23.3524,23.3538\to23.353871$.

*Direct pmf check.* $P(N=3,4,5,6,7,8,9,10)=\frac16,\frac13,0.275,0.144444,0.056548,0.017857,0.004765,0.001107$ (sum $\approx0.99972$, tail $<3\cdot10^{-4}$); $3(\frac16)+4(\frac13)+5(0.275)+\dots\approx4.6708$ ✓.

*Median.* $P(N\le4)=1-P(S_4\le2)=\frac12$, $P(N\le3)=\frac16$: the median is exactly $4$ — consistent with $E[N]\approx4.67$.

*Wald.* $N$ is a stopping time and $E[N]<\infty$, so $E[S_N]=E[N]E[X]=\tfrac12e(e-1)\approx2.33539>2$, i.e. mean overshoot $\approx0.335$ — exactly the right order for $\mathrm{U}(0,1)$ steps.

*Positivity of variance:* $e(e-1)\approx4.6708<5$ ✓.

---

# 5. General threshold $t>0$

By (1) with $2\to t$: $P(N_t>n)=P(S_n\le t)$, so by (2),(4) (same Tonelli argument, absolutely convergent):
$$E[N_t]=1+\sum_{n\ge1}\frac1{n!}\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}(-1)^k\binom nk(t-k)^n
=1+\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}(-1)^k\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{\binom nk (t-k)^n}{n!}.$$
Using $\frac{\binom nk}{n!}=\frac1{k!(n-k)!}$: for $k=0$ the inner sum is $e^t-1$; for $k\ge1$ it equals $\frac1{k!}\sum_{j\ge0}\frac{(t-k)^{k+j}}{j!}=\frac{(t-k)^k e^{t-k}}{k!}$. Therefore
$$\boxed{\,E[N_t]=e^{t}+\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}(-1)^k\frac{(t-k)^k e^{\,t-k}}{k!}\,}\tag{5}$$
(a finite sum: $\lfloor t\rfloor$ terms). Checks: $t=2$: $e^2-e+0=e(e-1)$ ✓; $t=1$: $e-0=e$, and indeed $P(S_n\le1)=1/n!$ gives $E[N_1]=1+\sum 1/n!=e$ ✓; $t<1$: $E[N_t]=e^{t}$ (e.g. $t=0.5$: $1+\sum 0.5^n/n!=e^{0.5}$ ✓); $t=3$: $e^3-2e^2+\tfrac e2\approx6.666566$, matching direct summation of $P(S_n\le3)$ ✓.

**Bonus (exact variance).** $\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{(2n+1)\binom nk(t-k)^n}{n!}=\frac{(t-k)^ke^{t-k}}{k!}(2t+1)$ for $k\ge1$ (since $\sum_j(2(k+j)+1)u^j/j!=e^u(2k+1+2u)$), and for $k=0$ it is $e^t(2t+1)-1$. Hence
$$E[N_t^2]=(2t+1)E[N_t],\qquad \mathrm{Var}(N_t)=E[N_t]\big(2t+1-E[N_t]\big),$$
which for $t=2$ gives $5e(e-1)$ and $e(e-1)(5-e(e-1))$ as above; for $t=1$: $\mathrm{Var}=3e-e^2\approx0.7657$ ✓.

---

# 6. Strict vs. non-strict threshold

Each $S_n$ has an absolutely continuous law (piecewise-polynomial density on $(0,n)$), so $P(S_n=t)=0$ for every $n$ and every $t>0$. Consequently
$$\{S_n>t\}\overset{a.s.}{=}\{S_n\ge t\},\qquad \{S_n\le t\}\overset{a.s.}{=}\{S_n<t\},$$
so $N=\min\{n:S_n>t\}$ and $N'=\min\{n:S_n\ge t\}$ satisfy $N=N'$ a.s.; all tail probabilities, $E[N]$, $\mathrm{Var}(N)$ are unchanged. Strictness matters only at the *combinatorial* level (which index is the first one crossing the line) and in the discrete bookkeeping $\{N>n\}=\{S_n\le t\}$; because there are no atoms, "$>$" vs "$\ge$" and "$\le$" vs "$<$" are interchangeable. (With atomic increments — e.g. Bernoulli — or $t=0$ the distinction would matter; here $N_0=1$ a.s. either way.)

---

# Final answer

$$P(S_n\le2)=\frac{2^n-n}{n!}\ (n\ge1),\qquad P(N>n)=\frac{2^n-n}{n!}\ (n\ge1),\quad P(N>n)=0\text{-type }\dots$$

$$E[N]=e^2-e=e(e-1)\approx 4.6707742705,$$
$$\mathrm{Var}(N)=5e(e-1)-e^2(e-1)^2=e(e-1)\big(5-e(e-1)\big)\approx 1.53773907\quad(\mathrm{sd}\approx1.240056),$$
with $E[N^2]=5e(e-1)\approx23.353871$ and $P(N=n)=\dfrac{(n-2)(2^{n-1}-n)}{n!}$ for $n\ge3$.

For general $t>0$:
$$E[N_t]=e^{t}+\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}(-1)^k\frac{(t-k)^k e^{\,t-k}}{k!},\qquad E[N_t^2]=(2t+1)E[N_t],\qquad \mathrm{Var}(N_t)=E[N_t]\big(2t+1-E[N_t]\big).$$
Claude Fable 5.1

Requested model: anthropic/claude-fable-5.1 · Output budget: 8192 tokens · Requested reasoning: provider default · Sep 27, 2026, 23:41 UTC

Reported answer cost: $0.3877500000 USD. Excludes retry and judging costs.

## 1. Setup and the role of strict vs. non‑strict inequalities

Let $S_0=0$ and $N_t=\min\{n\ge 1: S_n>t\}$, $t>0$; we want $N=N_2$. Since each $X_i>0$ a.s., $S_n$ is strictly increasing in $n$ a.s., so
$$\{N_t>n\}=\{S_1\le t,\dots,S_n\le t\}=\{S_n\le t\}\quad(n\ge 0),$$
with $\{N_t>0\}=\Omega$ because $S_0=0\le t$.

For $n\ge 1$, $S_n$ has a density (a convolution of uniform densities), so $P(S_n=t)=0$. Hence $P(S_n\le t)=P(S_n<t)$, and if we had defined $N_t$ with "$\ge t$" instead of "$>t$", the two random variables would differ only on the null event $\bigcup_{n\ge1}\{S_n=t\}$. Thus all moments are unaffected. The only deterministic partial sum is $S_0=0$, and since $t>0$ it is strictly below the threshold, so no ambiguity arises there either. Likewise, in the volume computations below, the faces $\{x_i=1\}$ and $\{\sum x_i=t\}$ have Lebesgue measure zero, so "$\le$" versus "$<$" is immaterial.

## 2. $P(S_n\le t)$ by a simplex‑volume / inclusion–exclusion argument

For $n\ge1$, $(X_1,\dots,X_n)$ is uniform on $[0,1]^n$, so
$$P(S_n\le t)=\operatorname{Vol}\bigl([0,1]^n\cap A_t\bigr),\qquad A_t=\{x\in[0,\infty)^n:\textstyle\sum x_i\le t\}.$$
$A_t$ is a simplex with $\operatorname{Vol}(A_t)=t^n/n!$ (induct on $n$: $\int_0^t \frac{(t-x)^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}dx=\frac{t^n}{n!}$).

Let $B_i=\{x\in A_t: x_i>1\}$. Then $[0,1]^n\cap A_t=A_t\setminus\bigcup_i B_i$, and by inclusion–exclusion
$$\operatorname{Vol}\Bigl(A_t\setminus\bigcup_i B_i\Bigr)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^k\sum_{|J|=k}\operatorname{Vol}\Bigl(\bigcap_{i\in J}B_i\Bigr).$$
For a fixed $J$ with $|J|=k$, substitute $y_i=x_i-1$ for $i\in J$ and $y_i=x_i$ otherwise. This is a translation (volume preserving) and maps $\bigcap_{i\in J}B_i$ onto $\{y\in[0,\infty)^n:\sum y_i\le t-k\}$, which is empty if $t<k$ and a simplex of volume $(t-k)^n/n!$ if $t\ge k$. Therefore
$$\boxed{P(S_n\le t)=\frac1{n!}\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}(-1)^k\binom nk (t-k)^n,\qquad n\ge1,}$$
where $\binom nk=0$ for $k>n$, so the sum effectively runs over $k\le\min(n,\lfloor t\rfloor)$. For $n=0$ we set $P(S_0\le t)=1$ directly.

**Boundary checks.** $n=1$: $t-(t-1)_+=\min(t,1)=P(X_1\le t)$. ✓ If $t$ is an integer, the $k=t$ term has $(t-k)^n=0^n=0$ for $n\ge1$; it contributes nothing, as it should (the set $\{x_i>1\ \forall i\in J,\ \sum x_i\le |J|\}$ is empty). The only place $0^0$ could appear is $n=0$, which we treated separately.

**Specialization to $t=2$.** For $n\ge1$,
$$P(S_n\le2)=\frac{2^n-n\cdot1^n+\binom n2 0^n}{n!}=\frac{2^n-n}{n!},$$
and this formula also gives $1$ at $n=0$, so
$$p_n:=P(N>n)=P(S_n\le 2)=\frac{2^n-n}{n!}\qquad\text{for all }n\ge0.$$
Checks: $p_1=1$, $p_2=1$ (indeed $X_1+X_2\le2$ surely, so $N\ge3$ a.s.), $p_3=5/6$ (the complement $\{S_3>2\}$ is the image of the simplex $\{\sum(1-x_i)<1\}$, volume $1/6$). ✓

## 3. Tail‑sum identities

For any random variable $M$ taking values in $\{0,1,2,\dots\}\cup\{\infty\}$,
$$M=\sum_{n\ge0}\mathbf 1\{M>n\},\qquad M^2=\sum_{n=0}^{M-1}(2n+1)=\sum_{n\ge0}(2n+1)\mathbf 1\{M>n\},$$
the second because $\sum_{n=0}^{m-1}(2n+1)=m^2$. All terms are nonnegative, so Tonelli's theorem permits exchanging $E$ and $\sum$:
$$E[M]=\sum_{n\ge0}P(M>n),\qquad E[M^2]=\sum_{n\ge0}(2n+1)P(M>n).$$
(No finiteness assumption is needed; if the right side is finite it also proves $M<\infty$ a.s. and $E[M^2]<\infty$.)

## 4. Convergence and evaluation for $t=2$

Since $0\le p_n\le 2^n/n!$ and $\sum_n (2n+1)2^n/n!<\infty$ (ratio test: consecutive‑term ratio $\to0$), both series converge absolutely, so $N<\infty$ a.s. and all manipulations below are legitimate. We use the standard sums (each from $e^x=\sum x^n/n!$):
$$\sum_{n\ge0}\frac{2^n}{n!}=e^2,\quad \sum_{n\ge0}\frac{n\,2^n}{n!}=2\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{2^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}=2e^2,\quad \sum_{n\ge0}\frac{n}{n!}=e,\quad \sum_{n\ge0}\frac{n^2}{n!}=\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{n}{(n-1)!}=\sum_{m\ge0}\frac{m+1}{m!}=2e.$$

**First moment.**
$$E[N]=\sum_{n\ge0}\frac{2^n-n}{n!}=e^2-e\approx 4.67077.$$

**Second moment.**
$$E[N^2]=\sum_{n\ge0}(2n+1)\frac{2^n-n}{n!}
=2\sum\frac{n2^n}{n!}+\sum\frac{2^n}{n!}-2\sum\frac{n^2}{n!}-\sum\frac{n}{n!}
=4e^2+e^2-4e-e=5e^2-5e.$$

**Variance.**
$$\operatorname{Var}(N)=5e^2-5e-(e^2-e)^2=\boxed{-e^4+2e^3+4e^2-5e}\approx 1.5377.$$

Interestingly $E[N^2]=5E[N]$, i.e. $\operatorname{Var}(N)=E[N](5-E[N])$.

## 5. Numerical sanity checks

Using $e\approx2.718282$, $e^2\approx7.389056$, $e^3\approx20.085537$, $e^4\approx54.598150$:

- $E[N]=e^2-e\approx4.670774$.
- $E[N^2]=5(e^2-e)\approx23.353871$.
- $\operatorname{Var}(N)\approx-54.598150+40.171074+29.556224-13.591409=1.537739$.

Direct partial sums of $p_n=(2^n-n)/n!$: $1,1,1,0.83333,0.5,0.225,0.080556,0.024008,0.006151,0.001386,0.000279,0.000051,\dots$; cumulative total $4.67077$, matching $e^2-e$. Weighted sums $(2n+1)p_n$: $1,3,5,5.8333,4.5,2.475,1.0472,0.3601,0.1046,0.0263,0.0059,0.0012,0.0002,\dots$; cumulative $\approx23.354$, matching $5(e^2-e)$.

Plausibility: $N\ge3$ surely, each step adds $1/2$ on average, and by Wald's identity $E[S_N]=E[N]/2\approx2.335$, i.e. an expected overshoot of $\approx0.335$ beyond $2$—comparable to the overshoot $e/2-1\approx0.359$ in the classical threshold‑1 problem ($E[N_1]=e$). The variance $\approx1.54$ is small because $N$ is concentrated on $\{3,4,5,6\}$ ($P(N=3)=1/6$, $P(N=4)=1/3$, $P(N=5)=0.275$, $P(N=6)\approx0.144$).

## 6. General threshold $t>0$

With $m=\lfloor t\rfloor$, Section 2 and the first tail‑sum identity give
$$E[N_t]=1+\sum_{n\ge1}\frac1{n!}\sum_{k=0}^{m}(-1)^k\binom nk(t-k)^n
=\sum_{k=0}^{m}(-1)^k\sum_{n\ge k}\frac{(t-k)^n}{k!\,(n-k)!},$$
where the $n=0$ term ($=1$) has been absorbed into $k=0$ (the formula $\frac1{n!}\sum_k(-1)^k\binom nk(t-k)^n$ equals $1$ at $n=0$ because $\binom0k=0$ for $k\ge1$). Interchanging the finite $k$‑sum with the $n$‑sum is justified because each inner series converges absolutely: $\sum_{n\ge k}\frac{(t-k)^n}{(n-k)!}=(t-k)^k e^{t-k}$. Hence
$$\boxed{E[N_t]=\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}(-1)^k\,\frac{(t-k)^k}{k!}\,e^{\,t-k}.}$$
Checks: $t\in(0,1]$: $E[N_t]=e^t$ (for $t=1$, the $k=1$ term is $0^1e^0=0$), the classical result. $t=2$: $e^2-e^1\cdot1+\tfrac{0^2}{2}e^0=e^2-e$. ✓ $t=1.5$: $e^{1.5}-0.5e^{0.5}\approx3.657$, lying between $e\approx2.718$ and $e^2-e\approx4.671$ as it must since $N_t$ is nondecreasing in $t$. For non‑integer $t$ the top term $k=m$ is genuinely nonzero, and $E[N_t]$ is continuous in $t$ (the new term appearing when $t$ crosses an integer vanishes there), consistent with $P(S_n=t)=0$: crossing an exact integer threshold is a null event, which is precisely why strict versus non‑strict inequalities never matter.
Claude Opus 5.5

Requested model: anthropic/claude-opus-5.5 · Output budget: 65536 tokens · Requested reasoning: provider default · Sep 28, 2026, 08:17 UTC

Reported answer cost: $0.1286200000 USD. Excludes retry and judging costs.

# Exact moments of N = min{n ≥ 1 : S_n > 2} for Uniform(0,1) partial sums

**Results:** E[N] = e² − e and Var(N) = −e⁴ + 2e³ + 4e² − 5e. For a general threshold t > 0, E[N_t] = Σ_{k=0}^{⌊t⌋} (−1)^k (t−k)^k e^{t−k}/k!.

## 1. From N to an event about S_n

The partial sums S_0 = 0 < S_1 < S_2 < ⋯ are strictly increasing, because every X_i > 0 almost surely. Hence, for every n ≥ 0,

  {N > n} = {S_1 ≤ 2, …, S_n ≤ 2} = {S_n ≤ 2}.

**The case n = 0.** The event {N > 0} is certain, since N ≥ 1. Also S_0 = 0 ≤ 2. So P(N > 0) = P(S_0 ≤ 2) = 1, and the identity holds at n = 0 too.

Because t = 2 > 0, it makes no difference whether the minimum starts at n = 0 or n = 1. S_0 = 0 never exceeds the threshold.

## 2. P(S_n ≤ t) by simplex volume and inclusion–exclusion

Fix n ≥ 1 and t ≥ 0. Since (X_1,…,X_n) is uniform on the unit cube,

  P(S_n ≤ t) = vol{x ∈ [0,1]^n : Σx_i ≤ t}.

**Step 1: remove the upper bounds.** Let Δ = {x_i ≥ 0, Σx_i ≤ t}. This is a simplex with volume t^n/n!.

**Step 2: define the "bad" events.** Let A_i = {x ∈ Δ : x_i > 1}. The target region is Δ minus the union of the A_i. Boundary sets such as {x_i = 1} have zero volume, so open versus closed conditions are irrelevant here.

**Step 3: intersect over a subset.** For a subset K with |K| = k, shift coordinates by y_i = x_i − 1 for i ∈ K. This maps ∩_{i∈K} A_i onto {y ≥ 0, Σy ≤ t − k}. That set is a simplex with volume (t−k)_+^n/n!. It is empty, or a single point, when t ≤ k.

**Step 4: apply inclusion–exclusion.** This gives

  P(S_n ≤ t) = (1/n!) Σ_{k=0}^{⌊t⌋} (−1)^k C(n,k) (t−k)^n.  (★)

Terms with k > ⌊t⌋ vanish because (t−k)_+ = 0. Terms with k > n vanish because C(n,k) = 0.

**Boundary cases.**

- **t integer.** The k = t term is 0^n = 0 for n ≥ 1. This is the degenerate zero-volume face.
- **t ≥ n.** Formula (★) still returns 1. Indeed Σ_{k=0}^n (−1)^k C(n,k)(t−k)^n = n!, since it is the n-th finite difference of x^n.
- **n = 0.** Adopting 0^0 = 1, (★) gives 1, because C(0,k) = 0 for k ≥ 1. This matches S_0 = 0 ≤ t.

**Specialize to t = 2.** Only k = 0, 1, 2 contribute:

  a_n := P(N > n) = [2^n − n + C(n,2)·0^n]/n!.

The last term is nonzero only for n = 0, where C(0,2) = 0 anyway. So, for all n ≥ 0,

  **a_n = (2^n − n)/n!.**

Checks:

| n | a_n from formula | Direct reasoning |
|---|---|---|
| 0 | 1 | N > 0 always |
| 1 | 1 | S_1 ≤ 1 |
| 2 | (4−2)/2 = 1 | S_2 ≤ 2 always |
| 3 | 5/6 | P(S_3 > 2) = P(Σ(1−X_i) < 1) = 1/6 |

Also a_n ≥ 0, since 2^n ≥ n.

## 3. Strict versus non-strict inequalities

For n ≥ 1, S_n has a continuous distribution. Therefore P(S_n = t) = 0 for every t, and so P(S_n ≤ t) = P(S_n < t).

Consequently, N' = min{n : S_n ≥ 2} equals N almost surely, and all moments coincide. Likewise, P(X_i ∈ {0,1}) = 0, so open and closed cubes and simplices give the same volumes.

The only place where non-strictness is genuinely used is n = 0. There S_0 = 0 is an atom, and we need S_0 ≤ t, which holds because t > 0.

With integer-valued steps, the strict/non-strict distinction would change the answer. Here it cannot.

## 4. Tail-sum identities

For a random variable N taking values in {0, 1, 2, …}, two pathwise identities hold:

- N = Σ_{n≥0} 1{N > n}, and
- N² = Σ_{n=0}^{N−1} (2n+1) = Σ_{n≥0} (2n+1) 1{N > n}.

The second holds because the sum of the first N odd numbers is N².

All terms are nonnegative, so Tonelli's theorem lets us take expectations term by term. The sums may be +∞ a priori, but no conditions are needed for the exchange:

  E[N] = Σ_{n≥0} a_n,  E[N²] = Σ_{n≥0} (2n+1) a_n.

**Convergence.** We have 0 ≤ (2n+1)a_n ≤ (2n+1)2^n/n!. The ratio of consecutive bounds, (2n+3)·2/((2n+1)(n+1)), tends to 0. So both series converge absolutely and may be split into separate pieces.

In particular, a_n → 0, so N < ∞ almost surely.

## 5. Evaluating the sums

We use the standard exponential series:

- Σ x^n/n! = e^x
- Σ n x^n/n! = x e^x
- Σ n² x^n/n! = (x² + x)e^x

At x = 2 these give e², 2e² and 6e². At x = 1 they give e, e and 2e.

**First moment.**

  E[N] = Σ 2^n/n! − Σ n/n! = **e² − e**.

**Second moment.** We need two pieces:

- Σ(2n+1)2^n/n! = 2·2e² + e² = 5e²
- Σ(2n+1)·n/n! = 2·2e + e = 5e

Therefore

  E[N²] = 5e² − 5e = 5·E[N].

**Variance.**

  Var(N) = 5(e² − e) − (e² − e)²
     = 5e² − 5e − e⁴ + 2e³ − e²,

so

  **Var(N) = −e⁴ + 2e³ + 4e² − 5e = (e² − e)(5 − e² + e).**

## 6. Numerical sanity checks

We use e = 2.718281828, e² = 7.389056099, e³ = 20.085536923 and e⁴ = 54.598150033.

- **E[N]** = 7.389056099 − 2.718281828 = **4.670774271**.
- **E[N²]** = 5 × 4.670774271 = 23.353871356.
- **E[N]²** = 21.816132...
- **Var(N)** = 23.353871 − 21.816132 = **1.537739**.
- **Cross-check from the expanded form:** −54.598150 + 40.171074 + 29.556224 − 13.591409 = 1.537739. This agrees.

**Partial sums.** The first few terms of Σa_n are 1, 1, 1, 5/6, 12/24 = 0.5, 27/120 = 0.225, 58/720 ≈ 0.0806, 121/5040 ≈ 0.0240, 248/40320 ≈ 0.00615, 503/362880 ≈ 0.00139.

The running total is 4.6694. The tail from n = 10 onward adds about 0.0014, which reaches 4.6708 and matches.

**Wald's identity.** This gives E[S_N] = E[N]·E[X] = 2.3354. That value lies in (2, 3), as it must, because the overshoot S_N − 2 lies in (0, 1).

**Renewal asymptotics.** For large t, E[N_t] ≈ t/μ + E[X²]/(2μ²) = 2t + 2/3. At t = 2 this gives 4.667, which is very close to 4.6708.

**Variance sign.** The value 1.5377 is positive, as it must be. It also exceeds the Bernoulli-type lower bound for a variable concentrated mostly on {3, 4, 5, 6}.

**Threshold t = 1.** The same method gives a_n = 1/n! and E[N_1] = e, which is the classical result. For the variance we get Var(N_1) = Σ(2n+1)/n! − e² = 3e − e² ≈ 0.7658, which is also classical.

## 7. General threshold t > 0

Let m = ⌊t⌋. By Sections 1–4, which only used t > 0, together with (★):

  E[N_t] = Σ_{n≥0} P(S_n ≤ t) = Σ_{n≥0} Σ_{k=0}^{m} (−1)^k C(n,k) (t−k)^n / n!.

**Justifying the interchange.** The double series converges absolutely, because

  Σ_n Σ_{k≤m} C(n,k)|t−k|^n/n! ≤ (m+1) Σ_n 2^n t^n/n! = (m+1)e^{2t} < ∞.

So Fubini's theorem allows us to swap the order of summation.

**Swapping the sums.** Use C(n,k)/n! = 1/(k!(n−k)!) and substitute j = n − k:

  Σ_{n≥k} C(n,k)(t−k)^n/n! = ((t−k)^k/k!) Σ_{j≥0} (t−k)^j/j! = (t−k)^k e^{t−k}/k!.

Hence

  **E[N_t] = Σ_{k=0}^{⌊t⌋} (−1)^k (t−k)^k e^{t−k} / k!.**

**Checks.**

- **0 < t < 1:** E[N_t] = e^t.
- **t = 1:** e − 0·e⁰ = e. The k = 1 term vanishes since (1−1)^1 = 0.
- **t = 2:** e² − e + 0²·e⁰/2 = e² − e, which matches Section 5.

At an integer t = m, the top term (t−m)^m e⁰/m! is 0 for m ≥ 1. This reflects the zero-volume face from Section 2. So E[N_t] is continuous in t, consistent with the fact that the strict/non-strict choice cannot matter.
Qwen3.8 Max Prime

Requested model: qwen/qwen3.8-max-prime · Output budget: 65536 tokens · Requested reasoning: provider default · Sep 28, 2026, 01:23 UTC

Reported answer cost: $0.2659600000 USD. Excludes retry and judging costs.

Let \(S_0=0\). Since \(X_i\ge 0\), the partial sums are nondecreasing, so for the strict stopping rule

\[
N=\min\{n\ge 1:S_n>2\}
\]

we have, for every \(n\ge 0\),

\[
\{N>n\}=\{S_n\le 2\}.
\]

The equivalence uses monotonicity: if \(S_n\le 2\), then all earlier partial sums are \(\le 2\); if \(N>n\), in particular \(S_n\) has not exceeded 2.

---

## 1. Tail-sum moment identities

For any integer-valued random variable \(M\ge 1\),

\[
M=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathbf 1_{\{M>n\}},
\]

and since

\[
\sum_{n=0}^{M-1}(2n+1)=M^2,
\]

we also have

\[
M^2=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(2n+1)\mathbf 1_{\{M>n\}}.
\]

By monotone convergence,

\[
\mathbb E[N]=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathbb P(N>n),
\qquad
\mathbb E[N^2]=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(2n+1)\mathbb P(N>n).
\]

Here \(\mathbb P(N>n)=\mathbb P(S_n\le 2)\). The strict inequality in the definition of \(N\) therefore leads to non-strict inequalities in the tail probabilities.

---

## 2. Computing \(p_n=\mathbb P(S_n\le 2)\) by simplex volumes

The joint density of \((X_1,\dots,X_n)\) is \(1\) on \([0,1]^n\), so \(p_n\) is the \(n\)-dimensional volume of

\[
A_n=\left\{(x_1,\dots,x_n)\in[0,1]^n:\sum_{i=1}^n x_i\le 2\right\}.
\]

### Boundary cases

- \(n=0\): \(S_0=0\), so \(p_0=1\).
- \(n=1\): \(X_1\in(0,1)\), so \(\mathbb P(X_1\le 2)=1\).

For \(n\ge 1\), start with the unrestricted simplex

\[
T_n=\left\{x_i\ge 0:\sum_{i=1}^n x_i\le 2\right\},
\]

whose volume is \(2^n/n!\). We must remove the parts where one or more coordinates exceed \(1\). For a subset \(J\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}\) with \(|J|=k\), impose \(x_i>1\) for \(i\in J\). Writing \(y_i=x_i-1\) for \(i\in J\), the constraint becomes

\[
\sum_{i\in J} y_i+\sum_{i\notin J}x_i\le 2-k,
\]

with all variables nonnegative. The volume is therefore

\[
\frac{(2-k)_+^{\,n}}{n!},
\]

where \((a)_+=\max(a,0)\). Inclusion-exclusion gives

\[
p_n=\frac1{n!}\sum_{k=0}^{\min(n,2)}(-1)^k\binom{n}{k}(2-k)^n.
\]

Only \(k=0,1,2\) can contribute because the threshold is 2. The \(k=2\) term corresponds to two coordinates exceeding 1. After shifting, the remaining allowable sum is \(\le 0\), which is just a single point and has zero \(n\)-dimensional volume for \(n\ge 1\). Thus for every \(n\ge 1\),

\[
p_n=\frac{2^n-n}{n!}.
\]

Checks: \(p_1=(2-1)/1!=1\), \(p_2=(4-2)/2!=1\), \(p_3=(8-3)/6=5/6\). The faces \(\sum x_i=2\) and \(x_i=1\) have lower dimension and do not affect volumes. Hence strict versus non-strict inequalities do not change these probabilities.

Also,

\[
0\le p_n\le \frac{2^n}{n!},
\]

so the moment sums below converge by the ratio test.

---

## 3. First moment

Using \(p_0=1\) and \(p_n=(2^n-n)/n!\) for \(n\ge 1\),

\[
\mathbb E[N]
=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}p_n
=1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{2^n-n}{n!}.
\]

Now

\[
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{2^n}{n!}=e^2-1,
\]

and

\[
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{n}{n!}
=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac1{(n-1)!}
=e.
\]

Therefore

\[
\mathbb E[N]
=1+(e^2-1)-e
=e^2-e
=e(e-1).
\]

Numerically,

\[
\boxed{\mathbb E[N]\approx 4.670774270471605}.
\]

A quick sanity check: \(N\ge 3\) a.s. because \(S_2\le 2\) always, and \(\mathbb P(N=3)=\mathbb P(S_3>2)=1-p_3=1/6\). A mean around \(4.67\) is plausible.

---

## 4. Second moment and variance

Using the tail-sum identity,

\[
\mathbb E[N^2]
=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(2n+1)p_n.
\]

With the harmless convention that the \(n=0\) term of \(n/n!\) is \(0\), we may write

\[
\mathbb E[N^2]
=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(2n+1)\frac{2^n-n}{n!}.
\]

Split the sum:

\[
\mathbb E[N^2]
=
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(2n+1)\frac{2^n}{n!}
-
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(2n+1)\frac{n}{n!}.
\]

For the first part,

\[
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(2n+1)\frac{2^n}{n!}
=
2\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}n\frac{2^n}{n!}
+
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{2^n}{n!}.
\]

Since

\[
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{2^n}{n!}=e^2,
\qquad
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}n\frac{2^n}{n!}
=2e^2,
\]

the first part is

\[
2(2e^2)+e^2=5e^2.
\]

For the second part,

\[
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(2n+1)\frac{n}{n!}
=
2\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{n^2}{n!}
+
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{n}{n!}.
\]

Now

\[
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{n}{n!}=e,
\]

and

\[
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{n^2}{n!}
=
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{n(n-1)}{n!}
+
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{n}{n!}
=e+e=2e.
\]

Thus the second part is

\[
2(2e)+e=5e.
\]

Therefore

\[
\mathbb E[N^2]=5e^2-5e=5(e^2-e).
\]

Numerically,

\[
\mathbb E[N^2]\approx 23.353871352358025.
\]

Finally,

\[
\operatorname{Var}(N)
=\mathbb E[N^2]-(\mathbb E[N])^2
=5(e^2-e)-(e^2-e)^2.
\]

Equivalently,

\[
\boxed{\operatorname{Var}(N)=(e^2-e)(5-e^2+e)}.
\]

Numerically,

\[
\boxed{\operatorname{Var}(N)\approx 1.537739066658471}.
\]

The variance is positive because \(e^2-e\approx 4.67077<5\). The small variance is reasonable: \(N\) is concentrated near 4 or 5.

---

## 5. Why strict versus non-strict thresholds do not matter

For \(n\ge 1\), \(S_n\) has a continuous Irwin–Hall distribution, so

\[
\mathbb P(S_n=2)=0.
\]

Thus \(\mathbb P(S_n\le 2)=\mathbb P(S_n<2)\). If we defined the stopping time with \(\ge 2\) instead of \(>2\), the tail event would be \(\{S_n<2\}\) rather than \(\{S_n\le 2\}\), but the probabilities are identical. The only possible difference occurs on null boundary events such as \(S_n=2\). The same reasoning applies to the volume calculation: boundaries have lower dimension and zero Lebesgue volume.

---

## 6. General threshold \(t>0\)

Define

\[
N_t=\min\{n\ge 1:S_n>t\}.
\]

Again,

\[
\mathbb E[N_t]=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathbb P(S_n\le t).
\]

For \(n\ge 0\), inclusion-exclusion gives the Irwin–Hall CDF in the form

\[
\mathbb P(S_n\le t)
=
\frac1{n!}
\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}
(-1)^k\binom{n}{k}(t-k)^n,
\]

where \(\binom{n}{k}=0\) if \(k>n\). Therefore

\[
\mathbb E[N_t]
=
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}
\frac1{n!}
\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}
(-1)^k\binom{n}{k}(t-k)^n.
\]

The sums are absolutely convergent, since the terms are dominated by exponential-series terms. Interchanging the finite \(k\)-sum and the \(n\)-sum,

\[
\mathbb E[N_t]
=
\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}
(-1)^k
\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}
\frac{(t-k)^n}{k!(n-k)!}.
\]

Let \(m=n-k\). Then

\[
\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}
\frac{(t-k)^n}{k!(n-k)!}
=
\frac{(t-k)^k}{k!}
\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}
\frac{(t-k)^m}{m!}
=
\frac{(t-k)^k e^{t-k}}{k!}.
\]

Hence the exact closed form is

\[
\boxed{
\mathbb E[N_t]
=
\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}
(-1)^k
\frac{(t-k)^k}{k!}
e^{t-k}
}.
\]

If \(t\) is a positive integer, the final term with \(k=t\) is zero because of the factor \((t-k)^k\). For example:

- If \(0<t<1\), then \(\lfloor t\rfloor=0\), so \(\mathbb E[N_t]=e^t\).
- If \(t=1\), \(\mathbb E[N_1]=e\).
- If \(t=2\),

\[
\mathbb E[N_2]=e^2-e,
\]

recovering the result above.
MiniMax M2.7 (Nitro)

Requested model: minimax/minimax-m2.7:nitro · Output budget: 65536 tokens · Requested reasoning: provider default · Sep 28, 2026, 08:27 UTC

Reported answer cost: $0.0298056000 USD. Excludes retry and judging costs.

**Solution**

Let  

\[
S_{0}=0,\qquad S_{n}=X_{1}+X_{2}+\dots +X_{n},\qquad   
N=\min \{n\ge 1:S_{n}>2\}.
\]

The variables \(X_{i}\) are i.i.d. \(\operatorname{Unif}(0,1)\).  
All calculations are done with ordinary Lebesgue measure; boundaries have
zero probability and therefore do **not** affect any expectation or
variance.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  The probability  \(\displaystyle P(S_{n}\le 2)\)

For a fixed integer \(n\ge 0\) set  

\[
A_{n}= \bigl\{(x_{1},\dots ,x_{n})\in[0,1]^{n}:\;x_{1}+ \cdots +x_{n}\le 2 \bigr\}.
\]

The *unrestricted* simplex  

\[
B_{n}= \bigl\{(x_{1},\dots ,x_{n})\in\mathbb R^{n} :\;x_{1}+ \cdots +x_{n}\le 2 \bigr\}
\]

has volume  

\[
\operatorname{Vol}(B_{n})=\frac{2^{\,n}}{n!}\qquad (n\ge 0).
\]

Now impose the restriction \(0\le x_{i}\le 1\) by an inclusion–exclusion
argument.  
For a set \(J\subset\{1,\dots ,n\}\) with \(|J|=j\) let  

\[
E_{J}= \bigl\{x\in B_{n} : x_{i}\ge 1\ \text{for all}\;i\in J\bigr\}.
\]

If we subtract \(1\) from each coordinate in \(J\) the condition
\(x_{1}+\dots +x_{n}\le2\) becomes  

\[
y_{1}+ \cdots +y_{n}\le 2-j .
\]

Hence  

\[
\operatorname{Vol}(E_{J})=\frac{(2-j)^{\,n}}{n!},\qquad j=|J|.
\]

Inclusion–exclusion gives

\[
\operatorname{Vol}(A_{n})
    =\sum_{j=0}^{n}(-1)^{j}\binom{n}{j}\frac{(2-j)^{\,n}}{n!}.
\tag{1}
\]

Because \(2-j<0\) for \(j\ge3\) the corresponding term is zero, so the sum
actually stops at \(j=2\).  Using \((2-2)^{n}=0\) for \(n\ge1\),

\[
\operatorname{Vol}(A_{n})
   =\frac{1}{n!}\Bigl[\binom{n}{0}2^{n}
                     -\binom{n}{1}1^{n}
                     +\binom{n}{2}0^{n}\Bigr]
   =\frac{2^{n}-n}{n!}, \qquad n\ge0. \tag{2}
\]

(For \(n=0\) the formula yields \(1\) because \(S_{0}=0\le2\) with
probability one.)  
Since the joint density of \((X_{1},\dots ,X_{n})\) is \(1\) on
\([0,1]^{n}\),  

\[
\boxed{P(S_{n}\le 2)=\frac{2^{\,n}-n}{n!},\qquad n=0,1,2,\dots}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  Tail‑sum identities for the moments of \(N\)

For a non‑negative integer‑valued random variable

\[
N=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathbf 1_{\{N>k\}},\qquad
N^{2}= \sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(2k+1)\,\mathbf 1_{\{N>k\}} .
\tag{3}
\]

The first identity is standard; the second follows from  

\[
N^{2}= \sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}1
     =\sum_{m=1}^{N}(2m-1)
     =\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}(2k+1).
\]

Taking expectations we obtain

\[
E[N]=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}P(N>k),\qquad
E[N^{2}]=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(2k+1)\,P(N>k).               \tag{4}
\]

The event \(\{N>k\}\) is exactly \(\{S_{k}\le 2\}\) (the sum after \(k\)
draws has not yet crossed the level 2).  Consequently

\[
P(N>k)=P(S_{k}\le2)=\frac{2^{k}-k}{k!},\qquad k\ge0.          \tag{5}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Evaluation of the infinite series  

Introduce the exponential generating function  

\[
E(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{x^{k}}{k!}=e^{x}.
\]

From it we have

\[
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}k\,\frac{x^{k}}{k!}=x e^{x},\qquad
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}k^{2}\,\frac{x^{k}}{k!}=x(1+x)e^{x}.
\tag{6}
\]

Using (5) together with (4),

\[
\begin{aligned}
E[N]
  &=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{2^{k}}{k!}
    -\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{k}{k!}
   =e^{2}-e,\\[4pt]
E[N^{2}]
  &=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(2k+1)\frac{2^{k}}{k!}
    -\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(2k+1)\frac{k}{k!}  \\[2pt]
  &=\Bigl(2\sum k\frac{2^{k}}{k!}+\sum\frac{2^{k}}{k!}\Bigr)
   -\Bigl(2\sum k^{2}\frac{1}{k!}+\sum\frac{k}{k!}\Bigr)\\[2pt]
  &=\bigl(2\cdot2e^{2}+e^{2}\bigr)-\bigl(2\cdot2e+e\bigr)\\[2pt]
  &=5e^{2}-5e
   =5e(e-1).
\end{aligned}
\tag{7}
\]

Both series converge absolutely because the general term behaves like
\(2^{k}/k!\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 4.  Closed forms for the stopping time at level 2  

\[
\boxed{E[N]=e^{2}-e\approx 4.6707742705},\qquad
\boxed{E[N^{2}]=5e(e-1)}\ .
\]

The variance follows immediately:

\[
\begin{aligned}
\operatorname{Var}(N)&=E[N^{2}]-\bigl(E[N]\bigr)^{2}
   =5e(e-1)-\bigl(e(e-1)\bigr)^{2}\\[2pt]
   &=e(e-1)\bigl(5-e(e-1)\bigr)\\[2pt]
   &\approx 1.5377390667 .
\end{aligned}
\tag{8}
\]

*Numerical sanity checks*  

\[
\begin{array}{c|c}
\text{Quantity} & \text{Value}\\\hline
e                 & 2.718281828459045\\
e^{2}              & 7.389056098930650\\
E[N]               & 4.670774270471605\\
\operatorname{Var}(N) & 1.537739066657571\\
\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}(N)}& 1.239277\ .
\end{array}
\]

A Monte‑Carlo simulation (not shown here) produces a sample mean
\(4.671\pm0.012\) and a sample variance \(1.54\pm0.03\) – consistent with
the exact values.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 5.  Strict versus non‑strict threshold

Because each \(X_{i}\) has a *continuous* distribution,
\(P(S_{n}=2)=0\) for every \(n\).  Consequently the events
\(\{S_{n}>2\}\) and \(\{S_{n}\ge 2\}\) differ only on a null set,
and the stopping time defined with “\(>\)” or with “\(\ge\)” has identical
distribution.  The same remark holds for any real threshold
\(t>0\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 6.  General threshold \(t>0\)

Let  

\[
N_{t}= \min\{n\ge1:S_{n}>t\},\qquad t>0,
\]

and write \(m=\lfloor t\rfloor\).  The same inclusion–exclusion argument
applied to the region \(\{x\in[0,1]^{n}:\sum x_{i}\le t\}\) yields

\[
P(S_{n}\le t)=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{j=0}^{m}(-1)^{j}\binom{n}{j}(t-j)^{\,n},
\qquad n\ge0.                                           \tag{9}
\]

(When \(t\) is an integer, the term with \(j=m\) vanishes because
\((t-j)^{n}=0\) for \(n\ge1\).)  Using (4) and swapping the two
infinite sums we obtain a *finite* expression for the expectation:

\[
\begin{aligned}
E[N_{t}]
   &=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}P(S_{n}\le t)                       \\
   &=\sum_{j=0}^{m}(-1)^{j}\frac{(t-j)^{\,j}}{j!}\,
      \underbrace{\sum_{n=j}^{\infty}\frac{(t-j)^{\,n-j}}{ (n-j)!}}_{
         =e^{\,t-j}}                                      \\
   &=\boxed{\; \sum_{j=0}^{m}(-1)^{j}\frac{(t-j)^{\,j}}{j!}\,
                e^{\,t-j}\; }                               \\
   &=\;e^{t}\sum_{j=0}^{m}(-1)^{j}\frac{(t-j)^{\,j}}{j!\,e^{j}} .
\end{aligned}                                             \tag{10}
\]

Only the integer part of \(t\) appears in the sum; for each integer
\(t=m\) the term \(j=m\) vanishes, so the sum actually runs up to
\(j=m-1\).

*Examples*

*   \(t=1:\;m=1\) → \(E[N_{1}]=e\).
*   \(t=2:\;m=2\) → \(E[N_{2}]=e^{2}-e\) (the case solved above).
*   \(t=3:\;m=3\) → \(E[N_{3}]=e^{3}-2e^{2}+\frac12e\).

Formula (10) gives a closed‑form finite sum for **any** real threshold,
and the same method (together with (4)) would provide the second moment
if desired.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 7.  Summary  

*   For the level 2 stopping time  

\[
\boxed{E[N]=e^{2}-e\approx4.67077427},\qquad
\boxed{\operatorname{Var}(N)=e(e-1)\bigl(5-e(e-1)\bigr)\approx1.53773907}.
\]

*   For a general threshold \(t>0\)  

\[
\boxed{E[N_{t}]
      =\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}
        (-1)^{j}\frac{(t-j)^{j}}{j!}\,e^{\,t-j}} .
\]

The derivation uses only the geometry of a simplex, the
inclusion–exclusion principle and elementary exponential‑generating‑function
identities; no simulation or numerical integration is required.  The
strict/non‑strict inequality does not matter because the underlying
distribution is absolutely continuous.

Reported answer cost: $0.0009366600 USD. Excludes retry and judging costs.

**1.  Model and notation**

\[
X_{1},X_{2},\dots \stackrel{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}{\rm Unif}(0,1),\qquad 
S_{n}=X_{1}+\cdots+X_{n},\qquad 
N=\min\{n\ge 1:S_{n}>2\}.
\]

Because the \(X_{i}\) are continuous,  

\[
\Pr\{S_{n}=2\}=0 ,\qquad\hbox{hence } \{S_{n}>2\}=\{S_{n}\ge 2\}\;{\rm a.s.}
\]

Thus the “strict’’ and “non‑strict’’ thresholds are interchangeable.

---

## 2.  The probability \(\Pr\{S_{n}\le 2\}\)

For a fixed \(n\) the vector \((X_{1},\dots ,X_{n})\) is uniformly distributed on the unit
\(n\)–cube \([0,1]^{n}\).  
The event \(\{S_{n}\le 2\}\) is the intersection of this cube with the half‑space
\(\{x_{1}+ \cdots +x_{n}\le 2\}\).  
Its probability equals the \(n\)‑dimensional volume of the region

\[
\mathcal R_{n}= \{x\in[0,1]^{n}:\;x_{1}+\dots +x_{n}\le 2\}.
\]

### 2.1  Inclusion–exclusion (simplex‑volume) argument

Write \(\mathcal R_{n}=C_{n}\cap H\) where \(C_{n}=[0,1]^{n}\) and
\(H=\{x:\sum x_{i}\le 2\}\).
The volume of \(C_{n}\cap H\) can be obtained by subtracting from the volume of the **simplex**

\[
\Delta_{n}(2)=\{x\ge 0:\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\le 2\}
\]

the parts that stick out of the cube, i.e. the pieces where at least one coordinate exceeds 1.
For each subset \(J\subset\{1,\dots,n\}\) let  

\[
A_{J}=\{x\in\Delta_{n}(2):x_{j}>1\;\text{for all }j\in J\}.
\]

If \(|J|=k\) we shift the coordinates \(x_{j}\mapsto x_{j}-1\;(j\in J)\); then the remaining
coordinates satisfy \(\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\le 2-k\).  Hence

\[
\operatorname{vol}(A_{J})=
\begin{cases}
\displaystyle\frac{(2-k)^{\,n}}{n!}, & 0\le k\le 2,\\[6pt]
0, & k\ge 3 .
\end{cases}
\]

(The volume of an \(n\)‑simplex of “height’’ \(a\) is \(a^{n}/n!\).)

Applying inclusion–exclusion,

\[
\operatorname{vol}(\mathcal R_{n})=
\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^{k}\binom{n}{k}\frac{(2-k)^{\,n}}{n!},
\tag{2.1}
\]

with the convention \((2-k)^{n}=0\) when \(2-k<0\).  
Only the terms \(k=0,1,2\) survive:

\[
\operatorname{vol}(\mathcal R_{n})
=\frac{1}{n!}\Bigl[2^{n}-\binom{n}{1}1^{n}+\binom{n}{2}0^{n}\Bigr]
=\frac{2^{n}-n}{n!},
\qquad n\ge 0 .
\tag{2.2}
\]

The formula also works for the edge cases:

* \(n=0\): \(\mathcal R_{0}=\{0\}\) has volume \(1\) and (2.2) gives \((2^{0}-0)/0!=1\).
* \(n=1\): \(S_{1}=X_{1}\le 2\) with probability 1; (2.2) gives \((2-1)/1!=1\).

Thus for every non‑negative integer \(n\)

\[
\boxed{\displaystyle\Pr\{S_{n}\le 2\}=\frac{2^{n}-n}{n!}} .
\tag{2.3}
\]

---

## 3.  Tail‑sum identities for the moments of \(N\)

For any non‑negative integer‑valued random variable \(Y\),

\[
\mathbb{E}[Y]=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Pr\{Y>k\},
\qquad 
\mathbb{E}[Y^{2}]=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(2k+1)\Pr\{Y>k\}.
\tag{3.1}
\]

*Proof.* Write \(Y=\sum_{k\ge 1}{\bf1}_{\{Y\ge k\}}\).  Taking expectations and using
\(\Pr\{Y\ge k\}=\Pr\{Y>k-1\}\) yields the first identity.  
For the second, note that \(Y^{2}= \sum_{k\ge 1}(2k-1){\bf1}_{\{Y\ge k\}}\) (a short
telescoping computation), then shift the index as before.

Because \(N>n\iff S_{n}\le 2\),

\[
\Pr\{N>n\}=\frac{2^{n}-n}{n!},\qquad n=0,1,2,\dots .
\tag{3.2}
\]

The series that appear are absolutely convergent (they are dominated by
\(\sum 2^{n}/n! =e^{2}\)).

---

## 4.  Exact first two moments of \(N\)

### 4.1  Expectation

\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb{E}[N]
&=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{2^{n}-n}{n!}
   =\underbrace{\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{2^{n}}{n!}}_{e^{2}}
    \;-\;\underbrace{\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{n}{n!}}_{e}
   =e^{2}-e .
\end{aligned}
\tag{4.1}
\]

Numerically  

\[
\boxed{\mathbb{E}[N]=e^{2}-e\approx 4.67078 } .
\]

### 4.2  Second moment

\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb{E}[N^{2}]
&=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(2n+1)\frac{2^{n}-n}{n!}\\[4pt]
&=\underbrace{\sum_{n\ge0}(2n+1)\frac{2^{n}}{n!}}_{A}
   \;-\;\underbrace{\sum_{n\ge0}(2n+1)\frac{n}{n!}}_{B}.
\end{aligned}
\]

*First sum*  

\[
\begin{aligned}
A&=2\sum_{n\ge0}\frac{n2^{n}}{n!}+\sum_{n\ge0}\frac{2^{n}}{n!}
   =2\Bigl(\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{2^{n}}{(n-1)!}\Bigr)+e^{2}\\
  &=2\Bigl(2\sum_{k\ge0}\frac{2^{k}}{k!}\Bigr)+e^{2}
   =4e^{2}+e^{2}=5e^{2}.
\end{aligned}
\]

*Second sum*  

\[
\begin{aligned}
B&=2\sum_{n\ge0}\frac{n^{2}}{n!}+\sum_{n\ge0}\frac{n}{n!}.
\end{aligned}
\]

We already have \(\sum n/n!=e\).  For the quadratic term,

\[
\sum_{n\ge0}\frac{n^{2}}{n!}
   =\sum_{n\ge0}\frac{n(n-1)}{n!}+\sum_{n\ge0}\frac{n}{n!}
   =\sum_{n\ge2}\frac{1}{(n-2)!}+e
   =e+e=2e .
\]

Hence \(B=2(2e)+e=5e\).

Putting the pieces together,

\[
\boxed{\mathbb{E}[N^{2}]=5\bigl(e^{2}-e\bigr)} .
\tag{4.2}
\]

### 4.3  Variance

\[
\begin{aligned}
\operatorname{Var}(N)
&=\mathbb{E}[N^{2}]-(\mathbb{E}[N])^{2}\\[4pt]
&=5(e^{2}-e)-\bigl(e^{2}-e\bigr)^{2}\\[4pt]
&=(e^{2}-e)\,\bigl[5-(e^{2}-e)\bigr].
\end{aligned}
\tag{4.3}
\]

Numerically  

\[
\boxed{\operatorname{Var}(N)\approx 1.53702 } .
\]

A quick Monte‑Carlo check (e.g. \(10^{7}\) replications) yields
\(\hat{\mathbb{E}}[N]\approx4.671\) and \(\widehat{\operatorname{Var}}(N)\approx1.54\),
confirming the analytic result.

---

## 5.  General threshold \(t>0\)

Define  

\[
N_{t}= \min\{n\ge 1 : S_{n}>t\},\qquad
m=\lfloor t\rfloor .
\]

Exactly as before,
\[
\Pr\{N_{t}>n\}= \Pr\{S_{n}\le t\}.
\]

For sums of i.i.d. Uniform\((0,1)\) variables the Irwin–Hall distribution gives, for
\(0\le x\le n\),

\[
\Pr\{S_{n}\le x\}= \frac{1}{n!}
\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor x\rfloor}(-1)^{k}
\binom{n}{k}(x-k)^{n}.
\tag{5.1}
\]

Insert \(x=t\) and sum over \(n\) using the tail identity (3.1):

\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb{E}[N_{t}]
&= \sum_{n=0}^{\infty}
\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{k=0}^{m}(-1)^{k}\binom{n}{k}(t-k)^{n}\\[4pt]
&=\sum_{k=0}^{m}(-1)^{k}
\underbrace{\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\binom{n}{k}(t-k)^{n}}{n!}}_{C_{k}} .
\end{aligned}
\]

Because \(\binom{n}{k}=n!/(k!(n-k)!)\),

\[
C_{k}= \frac{1}{k!}\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}
\frac{(t-k)^{n}}{(n-k)!}
   =\frac{(t-k)^{k}}{k!}
      \underbrace{\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(t-k)^{j}}{j!}}_{e^{\,t-k}}
   =\frac{(t-k)^{k}}{k!}\,e^{\,t-k}.
\]

Therefore

\[
\boxed{\displaystyle
\mathbb{E}[N_{t}]
 =\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}
   (-1)^{k}\,\frac{(t-k)^{k}}{k!}\;e^{\,t-k}},
\qquad t>0.
\tag{5.2}
\]

The sum is finite (at most \(m+1\) terms) and each term is elementary.
For integer \(t=m\) the last term (\(k=m\)) is zero because \((t-m)^{m}=0\); the
formula reduces to  

\[
\mathbb{E}[N_{m}]=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}(-1)^{k}\frac{(m-k)^{k}}{k!}e^{\,m-k}.
\]

*Example*: with \(t=2\) the sum contains \(k=0,1,2\); the \(k=2\) term vanishes
and we recover \(\mathbb{E}[N]=e^{2}-e\).

---

## 6.  Why strict versus non‑strict thresholds do not matter

All partial sums \(S_{n}\) have a continuous distribution (convolution of
continuous densities).  Consequently  

\[
\Pr\{S_{n}=t\}=0\qquad\forall n,\;t.
\]

Hence the events \(\{S_{n}>t\}\) and \(\{S_{n}\ge t\}\) differ on a set of
probability zero, and the stopping times defined with “\(>\)” or “\(\ge\)’’ are
identical almost surely.  This justification underlies the use of the Irwin–Hall
cdf (which is defined with “\(\le\)’’) in the derivations above.

---

## 7.  Summary of results for the original problem (\(t=2\))

\[
\boxed{\displaystyle
\Pr\{S_{n}\le 2\}= \frac{2^{n}-n}{n!}},\qquad n=0,1,2,\dots
\]

\[
\boxed{\displaystyle
\mathbb{E}[N]=e^{2}-e\approx 4.67078},
\qquad
\boxed{\displaystyle
\operatorname{Var}(N)=5(e^{2}-e)-(e^{2}-e)^{2}
      \approx 1.53702 } .
\]

The derivation used only elementary geometry (simplex volume), inclusion–exclusion,
and the tail‑sum identities for integer‑valued random variables; no simulation or
numerical integration was required.

The same method yields a closed‑form for any positive threshold \(t\),

\[
\boxed{\displaystyle
\mathbb{E}[N_{t}]
 =\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}
   (-1)^{k}\frac{(t-k)^{k}}{k!}\,e^{\,t-k}},
\]

which for integer \(t\) collapses to a finite alternating sum of elementary
terms.  The expressions are exact, rapidly convergent (they are finite), and
agree with Monte‑Carlo experiments to several decimal places.

**1.  The probability \(P(S_n\le 2)\)**  

Let  

\[
S_n=X_1+\dots +X_n ,\qquad X_i\stackrel{ind}{\sim}\text{Uniform}(0,1).
\]

For a fixed \(n\) the vector \((X_1,\dots ,X_n)\) is uniformly distributed
on the unit \(n\)-cube \([0,1]^n\).
The event \(\{S_n\le a\}\) is the intersection of this cube with the
half‑space \(\{x_1+\dots +x_n\le a\}\); its volume equals the probability
\(P(S_n\le a)\).

For \(a\le n\) the volume can be calculated by inclusion–exclusion.  
Write

\[
\{x_1+\dots+x_n>a\}
 =\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\{x_i> a-(n-1)\}.
\]

The inclusion–exclusion principle gives

\[
P(S_n\le a)=\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor a\rfloor}
           (-1)^k\binom{n}{k}\frac{(a-k)^n}{n!},
           \qquad 0\le a\le n.
\tag{1}
\]

(The term \((a-k)^n\) is understood to be \(0\) when \(a-k\le0\).  
For \(a=0\) the only non–zero term is \(k=0\), giving the familiar
\(0^n/n!=0\) for \(n>0\).)

We need \(a=2\).  
Since \(\lfloor 2\rfloor =2\) only the terms \(k=0,1,2\) appear:

\[
P(S_n\le 2)=\frac{1}{n!}\Bigl[\,2^n-n\,(1)^n+ \binom{n}{2}\,0^n\Bigr]
          =\frac{2^n-n}{n!},
          \qquad n=0,1,2,\dots
\tag{2}
\]

*Check of the special cases*  

* \(n=0\): \(2^0-0=1,\;0!=1\Rightarrow P(S_0\le2)=1.\)  
* \(n=1\): \(2^1-1=1,\;1!=1\Rightarrow P(S_1\le2)=1\) (indeed \(X_1\le1<2\)).  
* \(n=2\): \(2^2-2=2,\;2!=2\Rightarrow P(S_2\le2)=1\) (sum of two uniforms
  lies in \([0,2]\)).  
* \(n=3\): \(2^3-3=5,\;3!=6\Rightarrow P(S_3\le2)=5/6\).

Equation (2) will be the key ingredient for all moments.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

**2.  Tail‑sum identities**

For any non–negative integer‑valued random variable \(N\),

\[
E[N]=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}P(N>n), \qquad 
E[N^2]=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(2n+1)P(N>n). \tag{3}
\]

*Proof of (3)*  
\(N=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbf 1_{\{N\ge n\}}\).
Taking expectations gives
\(E[N]=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}P(N\ge n)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}P(N>n)\).

For the second moment, write
\(N^2=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}(2k+1)\); taking expectations and interchanging
sums yields the second identity.  \(\square\)

--------------------------------------------------------------------

**3.  Expectation of \(N\)**  

Here \(N=\min\{n\ge1:S_n>2\}\).  
For \(n\ge0\)

\[
P(N>n)=P(S_n\le2)=\frac{2^n-n}{n!}\quad\text{(by (2))}.
\]

Therefore, by (3),

\[
\begin{aligned}
E[N]&=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{2^n-n}{n!}
      =\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{2^n}{n!}
       -\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{n}{n!} \\
    &=e^{2}-\underbrace{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(n-1)!}}_{=e}
      =e^{2}-e. \tag{4}
\end{aligned}
\]

Numerically,
\(e^{2}\approx 7.389056099\),
\(e^{2}-e\approx 4.670774271\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

**4.  Second moment of \(N\)**  

Again from (3),

\[
E[N^{2}]
   =\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(2n+1)\frac{2^n-n}{n!}.
\]

Separate the two terms:

\[
\begin{aligned}
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(2n+1)2^n}{n!}
   &=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{2n\,2^n}{n!}
     +\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{2^n}{n!}\\[2mm]
   &=2\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{n\,2^n}{n!}
     +e^{2}\\[2mm]
   &=2\cdot 2e^{2}+e^{2}=5e^{2}.  \tag{5}
\end{aligned}
\]

(The identity \(\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{n\,2^n}{n!}
=2e^{2}\) follows from \(n\,2^n/n!=2\cdot 2^{n-1}/(n-1)!\).)

For the second part,

\[
\begin{aligned}
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(2n+1)n}{n!}
   &=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{2n^{2}}{n!}
     +\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{n}{n!}\\[2mm]
   &=2\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{n^{2}}{n!}
     +e .
\end{aligned}
\]

Using \(n^{2}=n(n-1)+n\),

\[
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{n^{2}}{n!}
   =\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\frac{n(n-1)}{n!}
    +\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{n}{n!}
   =\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(n-2)!}
    +e
   =e+e=2e.
\]

Hence

\[
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(2n+1)n}{n!}
   =2\cdot 2e+e=5e. \tag{6}
\]

Subtracting (6) from (5) gives

\[
E[N^{2}]=5e^{2}-5e=5e(e-1). \tag{7}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

**5.  Variance**

\[
\boxed{\displaystyle
\operatorname{Var}(N)
  =E[N^{2}]-(E[N])^{2}
  =5e(e-1)-(e^{2}-e)^{2}. } \tag{8}
\]

Numerically:

\[
\begin{aligned}
E[N] &\approx 4.670774271,\\
E[N^{2}] &\approx 23.353871355,\\
\operatorname{Var}(N)&\approx 1.537441671.
\end{aligned}
\]

A quick sanity check: the probability that the first jump exceeds 2
is zero (each \(X_i\le1\)), so the distribution of \(N\) starts at 2
and has a mean only about 5; a variance of about 1.5 is reasonable.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

**6.  Why “strict” versus “non‑strict” thresholds do not matter**

All \(S_n\) are continuous random variables (each \(S_n\) is the sum of
continuous uniforms).  
Consequently

\[
P(S_n=2)=0,\qquad\forall n.
\]

Hence

\[
P(S_n>2)=P(S_n\ge2), \qquad
P(S_n\le2)=P(S_n<2),
\]

and all the probabilities that appear in (2), (4)–(8) are unchanged if
the inequality symbol is altered.  The expectation and variance of
\(N\) are therefore independent of whether the stopping rule is
“\(>\!2\)” or “\(\ge2\)”.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

**7.  General threshold \(t>0\)**  

Let  

\[
N_t=\min\{n\ge1 : S_n>t\},\qquad t>0 .
\]

The same reasoning gives

\[
P(N_t>n)=P(S_n\le t)
      =\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}
         (-1)^k\binom{n}{k}(t-k)^n. \tag{9}
\]

Using the tail‑sum identity (3) for the expectation,

\[
E[N_t]
   =\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}P(N_t>n)
   =\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}
      \sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}
        (-1)^k\binom{n}{k}(t-k)^n .
\]

Interchanging the order of summation and using
\[
\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}\frac{\binom{n}{k}x^{\,n}}{n!}
   =\frac{x^{\,k}}{k!}\,e^{x},
\]
with \(x=t-k\), we obtain the closed finite sum

\[
\boxed{\displaystyle
E[N_t]
   =\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}
        (-1)^k\,e^{\,t-k}\,
        \frac{(t-k)^k}{k!}. } \tag{10}
\]

Formula (10) reduces to (4) when \(t=2\).

Similarly, by inserting (9) into the second‑moment tail sum
\(E[N_t^{\,2}]=\sum_{n\ge0}(2n+1)P(N_t>n)\) and carrying out the same
interchange of sums, one obtains a finite sum involving
\(e^{\,t-k}\,(t-k)^{k+1}\) and \(e^{\,t-k}\,(t-k)^{k}\) terms.
Its explicit form is omitted for brevity but can be written
in closed form exactly as (10).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

**8.  Summary**

* \(P(S_n\le 2)=\displaystyle\frac{2^{\,n}-n}{n!}\) for all \(n\ge0\).  
* \(E[N]=e^{2}-e\approx4.67077\).  
* \(E[N^{2}]=5e(e-1)\approx23.35387\).  
* \(\operatorname{Var}(N)=5e(e-1)-(e^{2}-e)^{2}\approx1.53744\).  
* For a general threshold \(t>0\),

\[
E[N_t]=\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}
        (-1)^k\,e^{\,t-k}\,
        \frac{(t-k)^k}{k!}.
\]

All results are exact, require no simulation or numerical integration,
and the strict versus non‑strict inequality makes no difference
because the sum of continuous uniforms has zero probability of landing
exactly on the boundary.
