Reported answer cost: $0.0000000000 USD. Excludes retry and judging costs.

## 1. Tail probabilities from simplex volumes

Set \(S_0=0\). Since the increments are nonnegative,

\[
\{N>n\}=\{S_n\le 2\}
\]

exactly: equality with \(2\) has not yet crossed the strict threshold.

For \(n\ge1\), \((X_1,\ldots,X_n)\) has density \(1\) on the unit cube. Consider the unrestricted simplex

\[
D_2=\left\{(x_1,\ldots,x_n):x_i\ge0,\ \sum_{i=1}^n x_i\le2\right\},
\qquad
\operatorname{vol}(D_2)=\frac{2^n}{n!}.
\]

Let \(A_i=\{x\in D_2:x_i>1\}\). If a specified set of \(k\) coordinates exceeds \(1\), shifting those coordinates down by \(1\) gives a simplex with residual sum at most \(2-k\), of volume

\[
\frac{(2-k)_+^n}{n!}.
\]

Thus inclusion-exclusion gives

\[
\Pr(S_n\le2)
=\frac1{n!}\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^k\binom nk(2-k)_+^n
=\frac{2^n-n}{n!},\qquad n\ge1.
\]

The exceptional low-dimensional cases are worth making explicit:

- \(n=0\): there is one point in dimension zero, so \(\Pr(S_0\le2)=1\).
- \(n=1\): the simplex has length \(2\), and the part with \(x_1>1\) has length \(1\), giving probability \(1\).
- \(n=2\): the unit square always lies in \(x_1+x_2\le2\), so the probability is \(1\). The point \((1,1)\) lies on the boundary but has probability zero.
- For \(n\ge3\), the \(k=2\) inclusion-exclusion term has zero \(n\)-dimensional volume because its residual simplex has sum bound \(0\).

Consequently, writing \(p_n=\Pr(N>n)\),

\[
p_0=1,\qquad
p_n=\frac{2^n-n}{n!}\quad(n\ge1).
\]

The displayed formula also algebraically gives \(p_0=1\), although the \(n=0\) simplex argument should be treated separately to avoid any \(0^0\) issue. In particular, \(N\ge3\) almost surely.

The faces \(x_i=1\) and \(\sum x_i=2\) have Lebesgue measure zero. Hence using \(x_i\le1\) instead of \(x_i<1\), or \(S_n<2\) instead of \(S_n\le2\), does not change the probability. Also, replacing the crossing rule \(S_n>2\) by \(S_n\ge2\) would not change \(N\) almost surely, since

\[
\Pr\left(\bigcup_{n\ge1}\{S_n=2\}\right)=0.
\]

## 2. Tail-sum identities and moments

Because \(0\le p_n\le 2^n/n!\), we have \(p_n\to0\), and both

\[
\sum_{n=0}^\infty p_n,
\qquad
\sum_{n=0}^\infty (2n+1)p_n
\]

converge. In particular, \(\Pr(N=\infty)=\lim_{n\to\infty}p_n=0\).

For a positive integer-valued random variable,

\[
N=\sum_{n=0}^\infty \mathbf 1_{\{N>n\}},
\qquad
N^2=\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}(2n+1)
=\sum_{n=0}^\infty (2n+1)\mathbf 1_{\{N>n\}}.
\]

Taking expectations is justified by Tonelli’s theorem, giving

\[
\mathbb E[N]=\sum_{n=0}^\infty p_n,
\qquad
\mathbb E[N^2]=\sum_{n=0}^\infty(2n+1)p_n.
\]

Therefore,

\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb E[N]
&=\sum_{n=0}^\infty\frac{2^n-n}{n!}\\
&=\sum_{n=0}^\infty\frac{2^n}{n!}
-\sum_{n=1}^\infty\frac1{(n-1)!}\\
&=e^2-e.
\end{aligned}
\]

For the second moment, use

\[
\sum_{n=0}^\infty\frac{n2^n}{n!}=2e^2,
\qquad
\sum_{n=0}^\infty\frac{n^2}{n!}=2e,
\]

where the second identity follows from \(n^2=n(n-1)+n\). Hence

\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb E[N^2]
&=\sum_{n=0}^\infty
\frac{(2n+1)(2^n-n)}{n!}\\
&=\left(2\sum_{n\ge0}\frac{n2^n}{n!}
+\sum_{n\ge0}\frac{2^n}{n!}\right)
-\left(2\sum_{n\ge0}\frac{n^2}{n!}
+\sum_{n\ge0}\frac n{n!}\right)\\
&=(4e^2+e^2)-(4e+e)\\
&=5(e^2-e).
\end{aligned}
\]

Thus

\[
\boxed{\mathbb E[N]=e^2-e}
\]

and

\[
\boxed{\operatorname{Var}(N)
=5(e^2-e)-(e^2-e)^2
=e(e-1)\bigl(5-e(e-1)\bigr)}.
\]

Numerically,

\[
\mathbb E[N]\approx4.6707742705,
\qquad
\mathbb E[N^2]\approx23.3538713524,
\]

and

\[
\operatorname{Var}(N)\approx1.5377390667,
\qquad
\operatorname{sd}(N)\approx1.24006.
\]

As a sanity check,

\[
(p_0,\ldots,p_7)
=\left(1,1,1,\frac56,\frac12,\frac9{40},
\frac{29}{360},\frac{121}{5040}\right).
\]

Their sum is approximately \(4.66290\), leaving only about \(0.00788\) for the rapidly decaying remaining tail. Also \(N\ge3\), with

\[
\Pr(N=3)=\frac16,\qquad
\Pr(N=4)=\frac13,\qquad
\Pr(N=5)=\frac{11}{40},
\]

consistent with the mean and standard deviation above.

## 3. General threshold \(t>0\)

Let

\[
N_t=\min\{n\ge1:S_n>t\}.
\]

The same inclusion-exclusion argument gives, for \(n\ge1\),

\[
\Pr(S_n\le t)
=\frac1{n!}\sum_{k=0}^{n}
(-1)^k\binom nk(t-k)_+^n
=\frac1{n!}\sum_{k=0}^{\min(n,\lfloor t\rfloor)}
(-1)^k\binom nk(t-k)^n.
\]

If \(t\) is an integer, the \(k=t\) term is zero because \(n\ge1\). In particular, for \(n=1\), this probability is \(t\) when \(0<t<1\), and \(1\) when \(t\ge1\).

Let \(m=\lfloor t\rfloor\). Since \(\Pr(S_n\le t)\le t^n/n!\), the tail sum converges and is at most \(e^t\). Using \(\binom nk/n!=1/(k!(n-k)!)\),

\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb E[N_t]
&=1+\sum_{n=1}^\infty
\sum_{k=0}^{\min(n,m)}
\frac{(-1)^k(t-k)^n}{k!(n-k)!}\\
&=1+(e^t-1)
+\sum_{k=1}^{m}(-1)^k
\sum_{n=k}^\infty
\frac{(t-k)^n}{k!(n-k)!}\\
&=e^t+\sum_{k=1}^{m}
\frac{(-1)^k}{k!}(t-k)^k e^{t-k}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Thus the compact finite-sum formula is

\[
\boxed{
\mathbb E[N_t]
=\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}
\frac{(-1)^k}{k!}(t-k)^k e^{\,t-k}
}.
\]

At \(t=2\), the \(k=2\) term vanishes and the result reduces to \(e^2-e\), as obtained above. For every \(t>0\), \(S_n\) is continuous for \(n\ge1\), so strict and non-strict threshold conventions agree almost surely; \(n=0\) causes no issue because \(0<t\).
Gemini 3.8 Flash

Requested model: google/gemini-3.8-flash · Output budget: 65536 tokens · Requested reasoning: provider default · Sep 28, 2026, 08:24 UTC

Reported answer cost: $0.0227310000 USD. Excludes retry and judging costs.

### 1. Strict vs. Non-Strict Threshold Events

Let $X_1, X_2, \dots \overset{\text{iid}}{\sim} \text{Uniform}(0,1)$. The vector $(X_1, \dots, X_n)$ has a continuous joint distribution with respect to $n$-dimensional Lebesgue measure $\lambda^n$ on $[0,1]^n$. 

The boundary hyperplanes $\left\{(x_1, \dots, x_n) \in \mathbb{R}^n : \sum_{i=1}^k x_i = t\right\}$ are $(n-1)$-dimensional affine subspaces, each having Lebesgue measure zero. Consequently, for any threshold $t \ge 0$ and any $k \ge 1$:
$$\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{i=1}^k X_i = t\right) = 0$$
Because a countable union of measure-zero events has measure zero, the events $\{S_n \le t\}$ and $\{S_n < t\}$ differ only by a set of probability zero. 

Furthermore, because $X_i > 0$ almost surely, the sequence of partial sums $S_n$ is strictly increasing almost surely. Thus:
$$\{N > n\} = \{S_1 \le 2, S_2 \le 2, \dots, S_n \le 2\} = \{S_n \le 2\} \quad \text{a.s.}$$
Whether $N$ is defined via $S_n > 2$ or $S_n \ge 2$, the random variable $N$ is identical almost surely.

---

### 2. Simplex-Volume and Inclusion-Exclusion for $\mathbb{P}(S_n \le 2)$

For an integer $n \ge 1$, $\mathbb{P}(S_n \le 2) = \text{Vol}_n\left([0, 1]^n \cap \left\{x \in \mathbb{R}^n : \sum_{i=1}^n x_i \le 2\right\}\right)$.

#### Volume of the Standard Simplex
For any $u \ge 0$, the volume of the standard scaled simplex $\Delta_n(u) = \left\{x \in \mathbb{R}_{\ge 0}^n : \sum_{i=1}^n x_i \le u\right\}$ is given by:
$$\text{Vol}_n(\Delta_n(u)) = \frac{u^n}{n!}$$

#### Inclusion-Exclusion Formulation
To restrict the coordinates to $x_i \in [0, 1]$, we subtract the regions where coordinates exceed $1$. Let $E_i = \{x \in \Delta_n(2) : x_i > 1\}$. 

For any index subset $I \subseteq \{1, \dots, n\}$ with $|I| = k$:
$$\bigcap_{i \in I} E_i = \left\{x \in \mathbb{R}_{\ge 0}^n : \sum_{j=1}^n x_j \le 2 \text{ and } x_i > 1 \text{ for all } i \in I\right\}$$
Making the change of variables $y_i = x_i - 1$ for $i \in I$ and $y_j = x_j$ for $j \notin I$, the condition becomes:
$$\sum_{j=1}^n y_j \le 2 - k, \quad y_j \ge 0$$
This is a standard simplex $\Delta_n(2 - k)$, which has volume $\frac{(2 - k)^n}{n!}$ for $k \le 2$, and volume $0$ for $k > 2$.

By the principle of inclusion-exclusion:
$$\mathbb{P}(S_n \le 2) = \frac{1}{n!} \sum_{k=0}^{\min(n, 2)} (-1)^k \binom{n}{k} (2 - k)^n$$

#### Evaluation and Boundary Cases
* **Case $n \ge 2$:**
  $$\mathbb{P}(S_n \le 2) = \frac{1}{n!} \left[ \binom{n}{0} 2^n - \binom{n}{1} (2-1)^n + \binom{n}{2} (2-2)^n \right] = \frac{2^n - n \cdot 1^n + 0}{n!} = \frac{2^n - n}{n!}$$
  *(Note: for $n \ge 1$, $(2-2)^n = 0^n = 0$.)*

* **Case $n = 1$:**
  $S_1 = X_1 \in (0, 1)$, so $\mathbb{P}(S_1 \le 2) = 1$. The formula yields:
  $$\frac{2^1 - 1}{1!} = 1$$

* **Case $n = 0$:**
  By convention, the empty sum is $S_0 = 0 \le 2$, so $\mathbb{P}(S_0 \le 2) = 1$. Evaluating the algebraic formula at $n = 0$:
  $$\frac{2^0 - 0}{0!} = \frac{1 - 0}{1} = 1$$

Thus, the closed form holds uniformly for all integers $n \ge 0$:
$$\mathbb{P}(N > n) = \mathbb{P}(S_n \le 2) = \frac{2^n - n}{n!}, \quad \forall n \ge 0$$

---

### 3. Justification of Tail-Sum Identities

Because $N$ takes values in $\{1, 2, 3, \dots\}$:

1. **First Moment:**
   Using the indicator identity $N = \sum_{n=0}^\infty \mathbf{1}_{\{N > n\}}$:
   $$\mathbb{E}[N] = \mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{n=0}^\infty \mathbf{1}_{\{N > n\}}\right] = \sum_{n=0}^\infty \mathbb{P}(N > n)$$
   Fubini-Tonelli applies directly as the summands are non-negative.

2. **Second Moment:**
   Similarly, $N^2 = \sum_{n=0}^\infty (2n + 1)\mathbf{1}_{\{N > n\}}$, because $\sum_{n=0}^{k-1} (2n + 1) = k^2$. Taking expectations:
   $$\mathbb{E}[N^2] = \sum_{n=0}^\infty (2n + 1) \mathbb{P}(N > n)$$
   Alternatively, using the factorial moment:
   $$N(N-1) = 2 \sum_{n=1}^\infty n \, \mathbf{1}_{\{N > n\}} \implies \mathbb{E}[N(N-1)] = 2 \sum_{n=1}^\infty n \, \mathbb{P}(N > n)$$
   whence $\mathbb{E}[N^2] = \mathbb{E}[N(N-1)] + \mathbb{E}[N]$.

---

### 4. Evaluation of the Series for $\mathbb{E}[N]$ and $\text{Var}(N)$

#### Fundamental Series
Recall the series expansions for $e^x$:
$$\sum_{n=0}^\infty \frac{x^n}{n!} = e^x, \quad \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{x^n}{(n-1)!} = x e^x, \quad \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{n \cdot x^n}{(n-1)!} = (x^2 + x)e^x$$

#### Calculation of $\mathbb{E}[N]$
$$\mathbb{E}[N] = \sum_{n=0}^\infty \frac{2^n - n}{n!} = \sum_{n=0}^\infty \frac{2^n}{n!} - \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{1}{(n-1)!} = e^2 - e$$

#### Calculation of Factorial Moment $\mathbb{E}[N(N-1)]$
$$\sum_{n=1}^\infty n \, \mathbb{P}(N > n) = \sum_{n=1}^\infty n \frac{2^n - n}{n!} = \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{2^n}{(n-1)!} - \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{n}{(n-1)!}$$
Evaluating each component:
$$\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{2^n}{(n-1)!} = 2 \sum_{m=0}^\infty \frac{2^m}{m!} = 2e^2$$
$$\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{n}{(n-1)!} = \sum_{m=0}^\infty \frac{m+1}{m!} = \sum_{m=1}^\infty \frac{1}{(m-1)!} + \sum_{m=0}^\infty \frac{1}{m!} = e + e = 2e$$
Therefore:
$$\sum_{n=1}^\infty n \, \mathbb{P}(N > n) = 2e^2 - 2e$$
$$\mathbb{E}[N(N-1)] = 2(2e^2 - 2e) = 4e^2 - 4e$$

#### Second Moment and Variance
$$\mathbb{E}[N^2] = \mathbb{E}[N(N-1)] + \mathbb{E}[N] = (4e^2 - 4e) + (e^2 - e) = 5e^2 - 5e$$

The variance is:
$$\text{Var}(N) = \mathbb{E}[N^2] - (\mathbb{E}[N])^2 = (5e^2 - 5e) - (e^2 - e)^2$$
Expanding the square:
$$(e^2 - e)^2 = e^4 - 2e^3 + e^2$$
$$\text{Var}(N) = 5e^2 - 5e - e^4 + 2e^3 - e^2 = -e^4 + 2e^3 + 4e^2 - 5e$$
Factoring out $e$:
$$\text{Var}(N) = e(-e^3 + 2e^2 + 4e - 5)$$

---

### 5. Convergence and Numerical Sanity Checks

#### Absolute Convergence
By Stirling's approximation, $\frac{2^n - n}{n!} \sim \frac{2^n}{\sqrt{2\pi n}(n/e)^n}$, ensuring faster-than-exponential convergence with infinite radius of convergence for the series $\sum \frac{2^n - n}{n!} z^n$. All rearranged series converge absolutely.

#### Numerical Sanity Check
Using $e \approx 2.7182818285$:
* $e^2 \approx 7.3890560989$
* $e^3 \approx 20.0855369232$
* $e^4 \approx 54.5981500331$

Expectation:
$$\mathbb{E}[N] = e^2 - e \approx 7.389056 - 2.718282 = 4.670774$$

Since $\mathbb{E}[X_i] = 0.5$, Wald's identity intuitively suggests $N \approx \frac{2}{0.5} = 4$. Due to overshoot, $\mathbb{E}[N]$ must strictly exceed 4, which aligns with $4.6708$.

Variance:
$$\mathbb{E}[N^2] = 5(e^2 - e) \approx 5 \times 4.6707743 = 23.353871$$
$$(\mathbb{E}[N])^2 \approx (4.6707743)^2 = 21.816133$$
$$\text{Var}(N) = 23.353871 - 21.816133 = 1.537738 > 0$$
Using the polynomial expression:
$$\text{Var}(N) = -54.598150 + 2(20.085537) + 4(7.389056) - 5(2.718282) = 1.537738$$
Both calculations agree, and $\text{Var}(N) > 0$.

---

### 6. Generalization to Arbitrary Threshold $t > 0$

Let $N_t = \min\{n \ge 1 : S_n > t\}$ for real $t > 0$. By Irwin-Hall distribution theory:
$$\mathbb{P}(S_n \le t) = \frac{1}{n!} \sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t \rfloor} (-1)^k \binom{n}{k} (t - k)^n$$
for all $n \ge \lfloor t \rfloor$. For $n < \lfloor t \rfloor$, the sum automatically vanishes for terms with $k > n$, leaving the exact value $1$.

Using the tail-sum identity:
$$\mathbb{E}[N_t] = \sum_{n=0}^\infty \mathbb{P}(S_n \le t) = \sum_{n=0}^\infty \frac{1}{n!} \sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t \rfloor} (-1)^k \binom{n}{k} (t - k)^n$$
Interchanging the finite sum over $k$ and the absolutely convergent infinite sum over $n$:
$$\mathbb{E}[N_t] = \sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t \rfloor} \frac{(-1)^k}{k!} \sum_{n=k}^\infty \frac{(t - k)^n}{(n - k)!} = \sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t \rfloor} \frac{(-1)^k}{k!} (t - k)^k \sum_{m=0}^\infty \frac{(t - k)^m}{m!}$$
$$\mathbb{E}[N_t] = \sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t \rfloor} \frac{(-1)^k (t - k)^k}{k!} e^{t - k}$$

#### Verification
* For $t = 1$: $\lfloor 1 \rfloor = 1$:
  $$\mathbb{E}[N_1] = \frac{(1-0)^0}{0!} e^1 - \frac{(1-1)^1}{1!} e^0 = e$$
  *(Recovering the classic Uniform $(0,1)$ sum-to-one result).*
* For $t = 2$: $\lfloor 2 \rfloor = 2$:
  $$\mathbb{E}[N_2] = \frac{2^0}{0!} e^2 - \frac{1^1}{1!} e^1 + \frac{0^2}{2!} e^0 = e^2 - e$$
  *(Recovering our derived result).*
GPT-6 Astra

Requested model: openai/gpt-6-astra · Output budget: 8192 tokens · Requested reasoning: provider default · Sep 27, 2026, 23:40 UTC

Reported answer cost: $0.1608600000 USD. Excludes retry and judging costs.

Write \(S_0=0\). We will use the fact that, because the increments are nonnegative,
\[
\{N>n\}=\{S_n\le 2\},\qquad n\ge0.
\]

## 1. Simplex volume and inclusion–exclusion

For \(n\ge1\), the vector \((X_1,\dots,X_n)\) has density \(1\) on the unit cube. Thus \(\Pr(S_n\le t)\) is the volume of
\[
\{x\in[0,1]^n:x_1+\cdots+x_n\le t\}.
\]
Using the closed cube instead of the open cube changes no probability.

The unrestricted simplex
\[
\{x_i\ge0:x_1+\cdots+x_n\le a\}
\]
has volume \(a^n/n!\) for \(a\ge0\). For example, this follows by induction, integrating its \((n-1)\)-dimensional cross-sections:
\[
V_n(a)=\int_0^a\frac{(a-x)^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}\,dx=\frac{a^n}{n!}.
\]
For \(a<0\) it is empty; for \(a=0\) it has zero \(n\)-dimensional volume.

To enforce the upper bounds \(x_i\le1\), apply inclusion–exclusion to the coordinate violations \(x_i>1\). If a specified set of \(j\) coordinates violates its bound, translate each of those coordinates down by \(1\). The resulting region has volume \((t-j)^n/n!\) when \(t-j\ge0\), and zero otherwise. Strict versus non-strict coordinate inequalities only affect volume-zero boundaries. Consequently, for \(n\ge1\),
\[
\boxed{\displaystyle
\Pr(S_n\le t)=
\frac1{n!}\sum_{j=0}^{\min(n,\lfloor t\rfloor)}
(-1)^j\binom nj(t-j)^n.}
\]
If \(t\) is an integer, the term \(j=t\), when present, is zero because \(n\ge1\).

For \(t=2\), only \(j=0,1\) contribute nonzero volume. Hence
\[
\Pr(S_n\le2)=\frac{2^n-n}{n!},\qquad n\ge1.
\]
The small cases deserve explicit attention:

- **\(n=0\):** \(S_0=0\) deterministically, so \(\Pr(S_0\le2)=1\). The displayed expression also equals \(1\) at \(n=0\), but this is checked separately rather than inferred from a positive-dimensional volume formula.
- **\(n=1\):** the formula gives \(2-1=1\), as it must.
- **\(n=2\):** it gives \((4-2)/2=1\). The \(j=2\) translated simplex is just a point and has area zero.

Thus, including the separately verified \(n=0\) case,
\[
\boxed{\displaystyle \Pr(N>n)=\frac{2^n-n}{n!},\qquad n\ge0.}
\]

For every \(n\ge1\), \(S_n\) has no atoms: conditioning on \(X_2,\dots,X_n\), the event \(S_n=2\) requires \(X_1\) to equal one specified value, which has probability zero. Therefore \(\Pr(S_n<2)=\Pr(S_n\le2)\). Moreover, a countable union shows that almost surely no \(S_n\) equals \(2\), so replacing \(S_n>2\) by \(S_n\ge2\) in the stopping rule does not change \(N\) almost surely.

## 2. Tail sums and convergence

For any positive integer \(m\),
\[
m=\sum_{n=0}^\infty \mathbf1_{\{m>n\}},
\qquad
m^2=\sum_{n=0}^\infty(2n+1)\mathbf1_{\{m>n\}},
\]
the second identity being the sum of the first \(m\) odd integers.

Taking expectations and using monotone convergence for these nonnegative sums gives
\[
\mathbb E[N]=\sum_{n=0}^\infty\Pr(N>n),
\qquad
\mathbb E[N^2]=\sum_{n=0}^\infty(2n+1)\Pr(N>n).
\]
These identities initially hold even with infinite values.

Here the unrestricted-simplex bound gives
\[
0\le\Pr(N>n)\le\frac{2^n}{n!}.
\]
The upper bound tends to zero, proving \(\Pr(N=\infty)=0\). Both moment sums converge because
\[
\sum_{n=0}^\infty(2n+1)\frac{2^n}{n!}<\infty
\]
by the ratio test. All subsequent series manipulations are therefore justified.

## 3. Evaluate the moments

From the exponential series,
\[
\sum_{n=0}^\infty\frac{a^n}{n!}=e^a,
\qquad
\sum_{n=0}^\infty\frac{na^n}{n!}=ae^a,
\]
where the second identity follows by shifting the index. Hence
\[
\boxed{\displaystyle
\mathbb E[N]
=\sum_{n=0}^\infty\frac{2^n-n}{n!}
=e^2-e.}
\]

For the second moment,
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb E[N^2]
&=\sum_{n=0}^\infty(2n+1)\frac{2^n-n}{n!}\\
&=2\sum_{n=0}^\infty\frac{n2^n}{n!}
+\sum_{n=0}^\infty\frac{2^n}{n!}
-2\sum_{n=0}^\infty\frac{n^2}{n!}
-\sum_{n=0}^\infty\frac n{n!}.
\end{aligned}
\]
Using \(n^2=n(n-1)+n\),
\[
\sum_{n=0}^\infty\frac{n^2}{n!}=e+e=2e.
\]
Therefore
\[
\boxed{\displaystyle
\mathbb E[N^2]=4e^2+e^2-4e-e=5(e^2-e).}
\]
It follows that
\[
\boxed{\displaystyle
\operatorname{Var}(N)
=5(e^2-e)-(e^2-e)^2
=4e^2-5e+2e^3-e^4.}
\]

### Numerical sanity checks

The constants are
\[
\mathbb E[N]\approx4.6707742705,\qquad
\mathbb E[N^2]\approx23.3538713524,
\]
\[
\operatorname{Var}(N)\approx1.5377390667.
\]
Since two increments cannot exceed \(2\), \(N\ge3\) almost surely, consistent with this mean. Some exact probabilities are
\[
\Pr(N=3)=1-\frac56=\frac16,\qquad
\Pr(N=4)=\frac56-\frac12=\frac13,
\]
\[
\Pr(N=5)=\frac12-\frac9{40}=\frac{11}{40}.
\]
Thus most of the mass is already at \(3,4,5\), consistent with a mean near \(4.67\) and modest variance.

## 4. General threshold \(t>0\)

Let \(m=\lfloor t\rfloor\). The same tail identity and simplex bound give
\[
\mathbb E[N_t]=1+\sum_{n=1}^\infty\Pr(S_n\le t),
\qquad
\Pr(S_n\le t)\le\frac{t^n}{n!},
\]
so the expectation is finite. Substitute the inclusion–exclusion formula and group by \(j\). There are only finitely many \(j\)'s, and each resulting series is absolutely convergent:
\[
\mathbb E[N_t]
=1+\sum_{n=1}^\infty\frac{t^n}{n!}
+\sum_{j=1}^{m}(-1)^j
\sum_{n=j}^\infty\binom nj\frac{(t-j)^n}{n!}.
\]
For \(j\ge1\),
\[
\begin{aligned}
\sum_{n=j}^\infty\binom nj\frac{(t-j)^n}{n!}
&=\frac{(t-j)^j}{j!}
\sum_{r=0}^\infty\frac{(t-j)^r}{r!}\\
&=\frac{(t-j)^j}{j!}e^{t-j}.
\end{aligned}
\]
Combining the initial \(1\) with \(e^t-1\) yields
\[
\boxed{\displaystyle
\mathbb E[N_t]
=\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}
(-1)^j\frac{(t-j)^j}{j!}\,e^{t-j}.}
\]
The \(j=0\) term is \(e^t\). If \(t\) is an integer, the last term is zero since its exponent \(j=t\) is positive—no \(0^0\) convention is needed.

Finally, for any fixed \(t>0\), \(\Pr(S_n=t)=0\) for every \(n\ge1\), while \(S_0=0\ne t\). Thus strict and non-strict threshold stopping rules again agree almost surely.

Let  
\[
F_n(t)=\mathbb P(S_n\le t),\qquad N=N_2=\min\{n\ge1:S_n>2\}.
\]

## 1. Strict vs non-strict boundaries

For every \(n\ge1\), \(S_n\) has a density, so  
\[
\mathbb P(S_n=t)=0.
\]
Thus \(\mathbb P(S_n\le t)=\mathbb P(S_n<t)\), and replacing the stopping rule \(S_n>t\) by \(S_n\ge t\) changes the stopping time only on the null event that some partial sum equals \(t\) exactly.

The non-strict CDF is nonetheless the right one in the tail identity:  
\(\{N>n\}\) means no strict crossing has occurred by time \(n\), i.e. \(S_n\le 2\). Equality at time \(n\) is allowed.

The same comment applies in the simplex-volume computation: using \(x_i>1\), \(x_i\ge1\), closed vs open polytopes changes only lower-dimensional faces, hence zero \(n\)-dimensional volume.

---

## 2. Exact CDF at threshold \(2\)

Let
\[
\Delta_n(x)=\{x_i\ge0,\ x_1+\cdots+x_n\le x\}.
\]
For \(n\ge1\),
\[
\operatorname{vol}(\Delta_n(x))=\frac{x^n}{n!},
\]
and for \(n=0\), \(\Delta_0(x)\) is a one-point set, with volume \(1\).

We want the volume of
\[
\Delta_n(2)\cap[0,1]^n.
\]
From \(\Delta_n(2)\), remove the events that some coordinate exceeds \(1\). Boundaries \(x_i=1\) do not matter for volume. For a fixed set \(J\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}\) with \(|J|=j\), impose \(x_i\ge1\) for \(i\in J\), then write \(x_i=1+y_i\). The remaining volume is
\[
\operatorname{vol}(\Delta_n(2-j))=\frac{(2-j)^n}{n!}.
\]
For threshold \(2\), only \(j=0,1,2\) can contribute, and the \(j=2\) term has zero \(n\)-dimensional volume for \(n\ge1\). Inclusion–exclusion gives
\[
F_n(2)
=\sum_{j=0}^{2}(-1)^j\binom nj\frac{(2-j)^n}{n!}
=\frac{2^n-n}{n!}.
\]
With binomial conventions, this also covers \(n=0\):
\[
F_0(2)=1,\qquad F_1(2)=1,\qquad F_2(2)=1,
\]
and indeed \(S_1\le2\) and \(S_2\le2\) almost surely.

So for all \(n\ge0\),
\[
\boxed{\mathbb P(S_n\le2)=\frac{2^n-n}{n!}}.
\]

---

## 3. Tail probabilities and moment identities

Because \(S_n\) is nondecreasing,
\[
\{N>n\}=\{S_n\le2\}.
\]
Also \(S_n\to\infty\) a.s. by the strong law of large numbers, since \(\mathbb E X_i=1/2\), so \(N<\infty\) a.s.

Thus, for \(n\ge0\),
\[
\mathbb P(N>n)=\frac{2^n-n}{n!}.
\]
Convergence is immediate from
\[
0\le \frac{2^n-n}{n!}\le \frac{2^n}{n!},
\]
and \(\sum 2^n/n!=e^2\).

For a positive integer-valued \(N\),
\[
N=\sum_{n=0}^\infty \mathbf 1_{\{N>n\}},
\]
and
\[
N^2=\sum_{n=0}^\infty (2n+1)\mathbf 1_{\{N>n\}},
\]
because
\[
\sum_{n=0}^{m-1}(2n+1)=m^2.
\]
Tonelli’s theorem gives
\[
\mathbb E[N]=\sum_{n=0}^\infty \mathbb P(N>n),
\]
and
\[
\mathbb E[N^2]=\sum_{n=0}^\infty (2n+1)\mathbb P(N>n).
\]

---

## 4. Evaluation of the sums

First moment:
\[
\mathbb E[N]
=\sum_{n=0}^\infty \frac{2^n-n}{n!}
=\sum_{n=0}^\infty \frac{2^n}{n!}
-\sum_{n=0}^\infty \frac{n}{n!}.
\]
Now
\[
\sum_{n=0}^\infty \frac{2^n}{n!}=e^2,
\]
and
\[
\sum_{n=0}^\infty \frac{n}{n!}
=\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac1{(n-1)!}=e.
\]
Therefore
\[
\boxed{\mathbb E[N]=e^2-e}.
\]

Second moment:
\[
\mathbb E[N^2]
=\sum_{n=0}^\infty (2n+1)\frac{2^n-n}{n!}
=A-B,
\]
where
\[
A=\sum_{n=0}^\infty (2n+1)\frac{2^n}{n!},
\qquad
B=\sum_{n=0}^\infty (2n+1)\frac{n}{n!}.
\]
For \(A\),
\[
\sum_{n=0}^\infty n\frac{2^n}{n!}
=2\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{2^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}
=2e^2,
\]
so
\[
A=2(2e^2)+e^2=5e^2.
\]
For \(B\),
\[
\sum_{n=0}^\infty \frac{n}{n!}=e,
\]
and
\[
\sum_{n=0}^\infty \frac{n^2}{n!}
=\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{n}{(n-1)!}
=\sum_{m=0}^\infty \frac{m+1}{m!}
=e+e=2e.
\]
Thus
\[
B=2(2e)+e=5e.
\]
Hence
\[
\boxed{\mathbb E[N^2]=5(e^2-e)}.
\]

Therefore
\[
\boxed{\operatorname{Var}(N)
=5(e^2-e)-(e^2-e)^2}.
\]

Numerically,
\[
e^2-e=4.670774270471605,
\]
\[
\mathbb E[N^2]=23.353871352358025,
\]
\[
\operatorname{Var}(N)=1.53773906665847\ldots
\]
A sanity check: since \(S_2\le2\) always, \(N\ge3\), and \(4.67077>3\), consistent.

---

## 5. General threshold \(t>0\)

Let
\[
N_t=\min\{n\ge1:S_n>t\}.
\]
Exactly as before,
\[
\mathbb E[N_t]=\sum_{n=0}^\infty \mathbb P(S_n\le t).
\]

The same inclusion–exclusion simplex computation gives the Irwin–Hall CDF: for \(n\ge1\),
\[
\mathbb P(S_n\le t)
=\frac1{n!}\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}
(-1)^j\binom nj (t-j)^n,
\]
with the usual convention \(\binom nj=0\) for \(j>n\). This also gives the correct value \(1\) for \(n=0\). If \(t\) is an integer, the final term has zero \(n\)-volume contribution for \(n\ge1\), so there is no \(0^0\) ambiguity.

Thus
\[
\mathbb E[N_t]
=\sum_{n=0}^\infty \frac1{n!}
\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}
(-1)^j\binom nj (t-j)^n.
\]
Interchange the finite \(j\)-sum with the absolutely convergent \(n\)-series:
\[
\mathbb E[N_t]
=\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}
(-1)^j
\sum_{n=j}^\infty \binom nj\frac{(t-j)^n}{n!}.
\]
Since
\[
\binom nj\frac1{n!}=\frac1{j!(n-j)!},
\]
we get
\[
\sum_{n=j}^\infty \binom nj\frac{(t-j)^n}{n!}
=\frac{(t-j)^j}{j!}
\sum_{r=0}^\infty \frac{(t-j)^r}{r!}
=\frac{(t-j)^j e^{t-j}}{j!}.
\]
Therefore
\[
\boxed{
\mathbb E[N_t]
=\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}
(-1)^j\frac{(t-j)^j e^{\,t-j}}{j!}
}.
\]

Checks: for \(0<t<1\), this gives \(e^t\). For \(t=1\), it gives \(e\). For \(t=2\), it gives
\[
e^2-e,
\]
matching the calculation above.
Kimi K3

Requested model: moonshotai/kimi-k3 · Output budget: 16384 tokens · Requested reasoning: provider default · Sep 28, 2026, 00:35 UTC

Reported answer cost: $0.1145381458 USD. Excludes retry and judging costs.

Let \(S_0=0\) and write
\[
F_n=\Pr(S_n\le 2),\qquad n\ge 0.
\]

## 1. Simplex-volume derivation of \(F_n\)

For \(n\ge1\), the joint density of \((X_1,\dots,X_n)\) is \(1\) on \([0,1]^n\), so \(\Pr(S_n\le2)\) is the \(n\)-dimensional volume of
\[
R_n=\left\{x_i\in[0,1]:\sum_{i=1}^n x_i\le2\right\}.
\]

The simplex
\[
\left\{x_i\ge0:\sum_{i=1}^n x_i\le a\right\}
\]
has volume \(a^n/n!\), since the standard simplex has volume \(1/n!\) and scaling by \(a\) multiplies \(n\)-dimensional volume by \(a^n\).

Start with the simplex \(\{x_i\ge0,\sum x_i\le2\}\), and impose \(x_i\le1\) by inclusion-exclusion. Let \(A_i=\{x_i>1\}\). For any \(k\) indices, after shifting those coordinates by \(1\), their intersection has residual sum at most \(2-k\). Hence its volume is
\[
\frac{(2-k)^n}{n!},\qquad k=0,1,2,
\]
and is empty for \(k>2\). For \(k=2\), the residual sum is \(0\), so the intersection is a single point and has zero \(n\)-dimensional volume when \(n\ge1\). Therefore
\[
F_n=\frac1{n!}\left[
2^n-n\cdot1^n+\binom n2\,0^n
\right].
\]

For \(n=1\), \(\binom12=0\), so
\[
F_1=2-1=1,
\]
as expected because \(X_1\le1<2\). For every \(n\ge1\), \(0^n=0\), giving
\[
\boxed{F_n=\frac{2^n-n}{n!},\qquad n\ge1.}
\]
In particular \(F_2=1\). Separately,
\[
\boxed{F_0=1,}
\]
because \(S_0=0\). The formula above is not applied at \(n=0\), avoiding any \(0^0\) convention.

## 2. Tail probabilities and moment identities

Because the \(X_i\) are nonnegative, the partial sums are nondecreasing. Thus, for \(n\ge0\),
\[
\{N>n\}=\{S_1\le2,\dots,S_n\le2\}=\{S_n\le2\},
\]
so
\[
\Pr(N>n)=F_n.
\]

For a nonnegative integer-valued random variable,
\[
N=\sum_{n=0}^\infty \mathbf 1_{\{N>n\}}.
\]
Also, if \(N=m\),
\[
\sum_{n=0}^{m-1}(2n+1)=m^2,
\]
and hence
\[
N^2=\sum_{n=0}^\infty(2n+1)\mathbf 1_{\{N>n\}}.
\]
Tonelli’s theorem therefore gives
\[
\mathbb E[N]=\sum_{n=0}^\infty F_n,
\qquad
\mathbb E[N^2]=\sum_{n=0}^\infty(2n+1)F_n.
\]

Both series converge because
\[
0\le F_n\le \frac{2^n}{n!},
\]
and factorial decay dominates both \(2^n\) and the polynomial factor \(2n+1\). In particular, \(F_n\to0\), so \(N<\infty\) almost surely.

## 3. Mean

Using \(F_0=1\),
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb E[N]
&=1+\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{2^n-n}{n!}\\
&=1+\sum_{n=1}^\infty\frac{2^n}{n!}
-\sum_{n=1}^\infty\frac{n}{n!}.
\end{aligned}
\]
Now
\[
\sum_{n=1}^\infty\frac{2^n}{n!}=e^2-1,
\qquad
\sum_{n=1}^\infty\frac{n}{n!}
=\sum_{n=1}^\infty\frac1{(n-1)!}=e.
\]
Consequently
\[
\boxed{\mathbb E[N]=e^2-e.}
\]

Numerically,
\[
\boxed{\mathbb E[N]\approx4.6707742705.}
\]

## 4. Second moment and variance

We have
\[
\mathbb E[N^2]
=1+\sum_{n=1}^\infty(2n+1)\frac{2^n-n}{n!}.
\]
Expanding,
\[
\mathbb E[N^2]
=1+2A-2B+C-D,
\]
where
\[
A=\sum_{n=1}^\infty\frac{n2^n}{n!},\quad
B=\sum_{n=1}^\infty\frac{n^2}{n!},\quad
C=\sum_{n=1}^\infty\frac{2^n}{n!},\quad
D=\sum_{n=1}^\infty\frac n{n!}.
\]
The required power-series identities are
\[
A=2e^2,\qquad B=2e,\qquad C=e^2-1,\qquad D=e.
\]
For example,
\[
B=\sum_{n=1}^\infty\frac{n(n-1)+n}{n!}=e+e=2e.
\]
Thus
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb E[N^2]
&=1+4e^2-4e+(e^2-1)-e\\
&=\boxed{5(e^2-e)}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Writing \(q=e^2-e\),
\[
\boxed{\operatorname{Var}(N)=5q-q^2
=(e^2-e)(5-e^2+e).}
\]
Equivalently,
\[
\boxed{\operatorname{Var}(N)
=-e^4+2e^3+4e^2-5e.}
\]

Numerically,
\[
\mathbb E[N^2]\approx23.3538713524,
\]
\[
(\mathbb E[N])^2\approx21.8161322857,
\]
and therefore
\[
\boxed{\operatorname{Var}(N)\approx1.5377390667.}
\]
The standard deviation is approximately \(1.24006\).

As a sanity check,
\[
F_0=F_1=F_2=1,\qquad
F_3=\frac56,\qquad F_4=\frac12,\qquad F_5=\frac9{40}.
\]
Thus \(N\ge3\) almost surely, while the rapidly decreasing remaining tails produce a mean near \(4.67\). Also,
\[
23.3539=\mathbb E[N^2]>(\mathbb E[N])^2\approx21.8161,
\]
as required.

## 5. General threshold \(t>0\)

Let \(m=\lfloor t\rfloor\). The same inclusion-exclusion argument gives the Irwin-Hall CDF
\[
\Pr(S_n\le t)
=\frac1{n!}\sum_{k=0}^{\min(n,m)}
(-1)^k\binom nk(t-k)^n.
\]
Using \(\binom nk=0\) for \(k>n\), the upper limit may be replaced by \(m\). Hence
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb E[N_t]
&=\sum_{n=0}^\infty\Pr(S_n\le t)\\
&=\sum_{n=0}^\infty\frac1{n!}
\sum_{k=0}^m(-1)^k\binom nk(t-k)^n.
\end{aligned}
\]
Since \(m\) is finite, the sums may be interchanged:
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb E[N_t]
&=\sum_{k=0}^m\frac{(-1)^k}{k!}
\sum_{n=k}^\infty\frac{(t-k)^n}{(n-k)!}\\
&=\boxed{
\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}
\frac{(-1)^k}{k!}(t-k)^k e^{\,t-k}
}.
\end{aligned}
\]
Each inner series is an absolutely convergent exponential series.

For \(0<t<1\), this reduces to \(\mathbb E[N_t]=e^t\). If \(t\) is a positive integer, the final \(k=t\) term vanishes because \(0^t=0\). At \(t=2\),
\[
\mathbb E[N_2]=e^2-e,
\]
recovering the main result.

## 6. Strict versus non-strict thresholds

For every fixed \(n\ge1\), \(S_n\) has a continuous density, so
\[
\Pr(S_n=t)=0.
\]
Therefore
\[
\Pr(S_n\le t)=\Pr(S_n<t).
\]
Moreover, replacing the definition \(N_t=\min\{n:S_n>t\}\) by
\[
\min\{n:S_n\ge t\}
\]
changes the stopping time only if some partial sum is exactly \(t\). The event
\[
\bigcup_{n=1}^\infty\{S_n=t\}
\]
is a countable union of probability-zero events and hence has probability zero. Thus strict and non-strict versions have the same distribution, mean, and variance. Boundary choices in the simplex-volume calculation likewise affect only zero-volume sets.
Hy3

Requested model: tencent/hy3 · Output budget: 65536 tokens · Requested reasoning: provider default · Sep 28, 2026, 08:27 UTC

Reported answer cost: $0.0156619320 USD. Excludes retry and judging costs.

Let \(X_i\sim\text{Uniform}(0,1)\) i.i.d., \(S_n=X_1+\cdots+X_n\) (\(S_0=0\)), and  
\(N=\min\{n\ge 1:S_n>2\}\).

## 1. Tail probability \(P(S_n\le 2)\) by volumes

For \(n\ge 1\), the joint density on \([0,1]^n\) is 1, so \(P(S_n\le 2)\) equals the volume of  
\[
A_n=\{x\in[0,1]^n:\sum_{i=1}^n x_i\le 2\}.
\]

Consider the unrestricted simplex \(B_n=\{x\in[0,\infty)^n:\sum x_i\le 2\}\). Its volume is \(2^n/n!\).  
Let \(E_i=\{x\in B_n:x_i>1\}\). By symmetry,
\[
\operatorname{vol}(E_i)=\operatorname{vol}\{y_i\ge0,\;y_i+\sum_{j\ne i}x_j\le 1\}=\frac{1^n}{n!}=\frac1{n!}.
\]
Any intersection \(E_i\cap E_j\) for \(i\ne j\) is empty (or has volume 0), because then \(x_i+x_j>2\), leaving allowance \(2-2=0\). Thus by inclusion–exclusion,
\[
\operatorname{vol}(A_n)=\frac{2^n}{n!}-n\cdot\frac1{n!}=\frac{2^n-n}{n!},\qquad n\ge 1.
\]
Checks: \(n=1\) gives \(1\); \(n=2\) gives \(1\); \(n=3\) gives \(5/6\).

Boundaries such as \(x_i=1\) or \(\sum x_i=2\) have Lebesgue measure zero, so using \(<\) or \(\le\) in the volume makes no difference.  
For \(n=0\), \(S_0=0\), so \(P(S_0\le 2)=1\).

Hence, with \(p_n=P(N>n)\),
\[
p_0=1,\qquad p_n=P(S_n\le 2)=\frac{2^n-n}{n!}\quad(n\ge 1).
\]

## 2. Tail-sum identities and convergence

Since \(S_n\) is nondecreasing,
\[
\{N>n\}=\{S_1\le2,\dots,S_n\le2\}=\{S_n\le2\}
\]
exactly. Because the distribution of \(S_n\) is continuous, \(P(S_n=2)=0\); thus replacing \(>\) by \(\ge\) in the definition of \(N\) changes nothing a.s.

For any integer-valued \(N\ge 1\),
\[
N=\sum_{n=0}^\infty \mathbf 1_{\{N>n\}},\qquad
N^2=\sum_{n=0}^\infty (2n+1)\mathbf 1_{\{N>n\}}.
\]
Taking expectations,
\[
E[N]=\sum_{n=0}^\infty p_n,\qquad
E[N^2]=\sum_{n=0}^\infty (2n+1)p_n.
\]
Convergence is immediate: \(p_n\le 2^n/n!\), so \(\sum p_n\le e^2<\infty\); also \((2n+1)p_n=O(2^n/(n-1)!)\), summable.

## 3. Mean and variance for threshold \(2\)

### Mean
\[
E[N]=1+\sum_{n=1}^\infty\frac{2^n-n}{n!}
=1+\Bigl(\sum_{n=1}^\infty\frac{2^n}{n!}\Bigr)-\Bigl(\sum_{n=1}^\infty\frac{n}{n!}\Bigr)
=1+(e^2-1)-e.
\]
Thus
\[
\boxed{E[N]=e^2-e\approx 4.670774}.
\]

### Second moment
\[
E[N^2]=1+\sum_{n=1}^\infty\frac{(2n+1)(2^n-n)}{n!}
=1+S_1-S_2,
\]
where
\[
S_1=\sum_{n=1}^\infty\frac{(2n+1)2^n}{n!}
=2\sum_{n=1}^\infty\frac{n2^n}{n!}+\sum_{n=1}^\infty\frac{2^n}{n!}
=2(2e^2)+(e^2-1)=5e^2-1,
\]
using \(\sum n a^n/n!=ae^a\). Also,
\[
S_2=\sum_{n=1}^\infty\frac{(2n+1)n}{n!}
=\sum_{n=1}^\infty\frac{2n^2+n}{n!}.
\]
With \(m=n-1\),
\[
S_2=\sum_{m=0}^\infty\frac{2(m+1)+1}{m!}
=2\sum_{m=0}^\infty\frac{m}{m!}+3\sum_{m=0}^\infty\frac1{m!}
=2e+3e=5e.
\]
Hence
\[
E[N^2]=1+(5e^2-1)-5e=5e^2-5e=5(e^2-e).
\]
Therefore
\[
\operatorname{Var}(N)=E[N^2]-E[N]^2
=(e^2-e)\bigl(5-(e^2-e)\bigr)
=\boxed{-e^4+2e^3+4e^2-5e\approx 1.537739}.
\]

## 4. Numerical sanity checks

- Since \(X_i<1\) a.s., \(S_1<1\) and \(S_2<2\) a.s., so \(N\ge 3\). Indeed \(p_2=1\), so \(P(N\le2)=0\).
- \(p_3=5/6\Rightarrow P(N=3)=1/6\).
- \(p_4=1/2\Rightarrow P(N=4)=p_3-p_4=1/3\).
- \(p_5=27/120=0.225\Rightarrow P(N=5)=0.275\).
Summing \(p_n\): \(1+1+1+0.8333+0.5+0.225+0.0806+\cdots=4.6708\), matching \(E[N]\).
The renewal asymptotic for Uniform\((0,1)\) interarrivals gives \(E[N_t]\approx 2t+2/3\); at \(t=2\) this is \(4.6667\), very close to \(4.6708\).

## 5. General threshold \(t>0\)

Let \(N_t=\min\{n\ge1:S_n>t\}\) and \(m=\lfloor t\rfloor\). By the same inclusion–exclusion argument, for \(n\ge0\),
\[
P(S_n\le t)=\frac1{n!}\sum_{k=0}^{\min(n,m)}(-1)^k\binom{n}{k}(t-k)^n,
\]
where terms with \(t<k\) are zero (and \(0^0=1\) for \(n=k=0\)). This is the Irwin–Hall CDF.

Now
\[
E[N_t]=\sum_{n=0}^\infty P(S_n\le t)
=\sum_{n=0}^\infty\frac1{n!}\sum_{k=0}^{m}(-1)^k\binom{n}{k}(t-k)^n.
\]
The series is absolutely convergent, so swap sums. For fixed \(k\),
\[
\sum_{n=k}^\infty \binom{n}{k}\frac{(t-k)^n}{n!}
=e^{\,t-k}\frac{(t-k)^k}{k!}.
\]
Thus the finite sum
\[
\boxed{E[N_t]=\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}(-1)^k\,e^{\,t-k}\,\frac{(t-k)^k}{k!}}.
\]

Examples:
- \(t=2\): \(e^2-e\) (as above).
- \(t=1\): \(e\).
- \(t=3\): \(e^3-2e^2+\frac{e}{2}\approx6.6665\).
- \(0<t<1\): \(E[N_t]=e^t\).

Again, strict versus non-strict inequality in \(S_n>t\) is irrelevant because \(P(S_n=t)=0\) for all \(n\ge1\).
GLM 5.3 Flash

Requested model: z-ai/glm-5.3-flash · Output budget: 65536 tokens · Requested reasoning: provider default · Sep 28, 2026, 08:25 UTC

Reported answer cost: $0.0115306100 USD. Excludes retry and judging costs.

## 1. Tail probabilities for threshold \(2\)

Let \(S_0=0\), and define

\[
p_n=\mathbb P(N>n),\qquad n=0,1,2,\dots
\]

Because \(S_n\) is nondecreasing in \(n\),

\[
\{N>n\}=\{S_1\le 2,\dots,S_n\le 2\}=\{S_n\le 2\}.
\]

Thus \(p_n=\mathbb P(S_n\le 2)\). Also \(N<\infty\) a.s. since \(S_n/n\to \mathbb E[X_1]=1/2\) a.s.

For a nonnegative integer-valued random variable \(N\),

\[
\mathbb E[N]=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathbb P(N>n),
\]

and, since \(\sum_{n=0}^{k-1}(2n+1)=k^2\),

\[
\mathbb E[N^2]=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(2n+1)\mathbb P(N>n).
\]

These are justified by monotone convergence.

---

## 2. Computing \(\mathbb P(S_n\le 2)\) by simplex volume

Let

\[
A_n=\{x\in [0,1]^n:x_1+\cdots+x_n\le 2\}.
\]

Since the density of \((X_1,\dots,X_n)\) is \(1\) on \([0,1]^n\),

\[
\mathbb P(S_n\le 2)=\operatorname{vol}_n(A_n).
\]

For \(n=0\), \(S_0=0\), so \(p_0=1\).

For \(n\ge 1\), start with the unconstrained simplex

\[
\Delta_n(2)=\{x_i\ge 0:x_1+\cdots+x_n\le 2\},
\]

whose volume is

\[
\operatorname{vol}_n(\Delta_n(2))=\frac{2^n}{n!}.
\]

We must remove the parts where some \(x_i>1\). Let

\[
B_i=\{x\in \Delta_n(2):x_i>1\}.
\]

If \(x_i>1\) and \(x_j>1\) for \(i\ne j\), then \(x_i+x_j>2\), impossible under \(x_1+\cdots+x_n\le 2\). Hence the \(B_i\) are pairwise disjoint. For fixed \(i\), put \(y_i=x_i-1\). Then

\[
y_i+\sum_{j\ne i}x_j\le 1,
\]

with all variables nonnegative, so

\[
\operatorname{vol}_n(B_i)=\frac{1}{n!}.
\]

Therefore, for \(n\ge 1\),

\[
p_n=\frac{2^n}{n!}-n\frac{1}{n!}
=\frac{2^n-n}{n!}.
\]

This formula also gives \(p_0=1\) if interpreted separately. It gives \(p_1=1\), consistent with \(X_1\le 1<2\), and \(p_2=1\), consistent with \(X_1+X_2\le 2\). Boundary choices such as \(x_i>1\) versus \(x_i\ge 1\) do not affect volume.

So

\[
\boxed{p_n=\mathbb P(N>n)=\frac{2^n-n}{n!}},\qquad n=0,1,2,\dots
\]

Also,

\[
0\le p_n\le \frac{2^n}{n!},
\]

so \(\sum p_n\) and \(\sum n p_n\) converge absolutely.

---

## 3. First moment

Using the tail-sum identity,

\[
\mathbb E[N]
=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}p_n
=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{2^n-n}{n!}.
\]

Split the sum:

\[
\mathbb E[N]
=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{2^n}{n!}
-\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{n}{n!}.
\]

Now

\[
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{2^n}{n!}=e^2,
\]

and

\[
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{n}{n!}
=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(n-1)!}
=e.
\]

Hence

\[
\boxed{\mathbb E[N]=e^2-e}.
\]

Numerically,

\[
\mathbb E[N]\approx 4.6707742705.
\]

---

## 4. Second moment and variance

Again using the tail identity,

\[
\mathbb E[N^2]
=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(2n+1)\frac{2^n-n}{n!}.
\]

Split:

\[
\mathbb E[N^2]
=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(2n+1)\frac{2^n}{n!}
-\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(2n+1)\frac{n}{n!}.
\]

First,

\[
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(2n+1)\frac{2^n}{n!}
=2\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}n\frac{2^n}{n!}
+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{2^n}{n!}.
\]

Since

\[
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n\frac{2^n}{n!}
=2\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{2^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}
=2e^2,
\]

we get

\[
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(2n+1)\frac{2^n}{n!}
=4e^2+e^2=5e^2.
\]

Similarly,

\[
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(2n+1)\frac{n}{n!}
=2\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{n^2}{n!}
+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{n}{n!}.
\]

Now

\[
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{n^2}{n!}
=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{n}{(n-1)!}
=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{k+1}{k!}
=e+e=2e.
\]

Therefore,

\[
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(2n+1)\frac{n}{n!}
=2(2e)+e=5e.
\]

Thus

\[
\mathbb E[N^2]=5e^2-5e=5(e^2-e).
\]

Since \(\mathbb E[N]=e^2-e\),

\[
\boxed{\operatorname{Var}(N)
=5(e^2-e)-(e^2-e)^2}.
\]

Numerically,

\[
\mathbb E[N^2]\approx 23.3538713524,
\]

\[
\operatorname{Var}(N)\approx 1.5377390667,
\]

so the standard deviation is approximately \(1.24006\).

A quick sanity check: the first tail probabilities are

\[
p_0=p_1=p_2=1,\quad
p_3=\frac56,\quad
p_4=\frac12,\quad
p_5=\frac9{40},
\]

\[
p_6=\frac{29}{360},\quad
p_7=\frac{121}{5040},\quad
p_8=\frac{31}{5040}.
\]

Summing through \(n=8\) gives

\[
\sum_{n=0}^{8}p_n\approx 4.669047619,
\]

already close to \(e^2-e\approx 4.670774270\). Also \(N\ge 3\) a.s., consistent with the mean exceeding \(4\).

---

## 5. Strict versus non-strict thresholds

The definition of \(N\) uses the strict event \(S_n>2\), but the tail event is non-strict:

\[
\{N>n\}=\{S_n\le 2\}.
\]

If instead one defined

\[
\widetilde N=\min\{n:S_n\ge 2\},
\]

then

\[
\{\widetilde N>n\}=\{S_n<2\}.
\]

But \(S_n\) has no atoms: for \(n\ge 1\), conditioning on \(X_2,\dots,X_n\), the event \(S_n=2\) requires \(X_1\) to equal one exact value, which has probability \(0\). Hence

\[
\mathbb P(S_n<2)=\mathbb P(S_n\le 2).
\]

Therefore \(N\) and \(\widetilde N\) agree a.s., and the moments are unchanged. Boundary issues in the volume argument similarly have zero Lebesgue measure.

---

## 6. General threshold \(t>0\)

Now let

\[
N_t=\min\{n\ge 1:S_n>t\},
\]

and set \(m=\lfloor t\rfloor\). As before,

\[
\mathbb P(N_t>n)=\mathbb P(S_n\le t).
\]

By the same inclusion-exclusion argument, for \(n\ge 0\),

\[
\mathbb P(S_n\le t)
=\frac1{n!}\sum_{k=0}^{m}(-1)^k\binom{n}{k}(t-k)^n,
\]

with the convention that \(\binom{n}{k}=0\) for \(k>n\). This is the Irwin-Hall CDF formula.

Thus

\[
\mathbb E[N_t]
=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathbb P(S_n\le t)
=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac1{n!}
\sum_{k=0}^{m}(-1)^k\binom{n}{k}(t-k)^n.
\]

Interchange the finite \(k\)-sum with the convergent \(n\)-sum:

\[
\mathbb E[N_t]
=\sum_{k=0}^{m}(-1)^k
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\binom{n}{k}\frac{(t-k)^n}{n!}.
\]

For \(a=t-k\ge 0\),

\[
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\binom{n}{k}\frac{a^n}{n!}
=\frac{a^k}{k!}e^a.
\]

Therefore,

\[
\boxed{
\mathbb E[N_t]
=\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}
(-1)^k\frac{(t-k)^k}{k!}e^{t-k}
}.
\]

For \(t=2\), this reduces to

\[
\mathbb E[N_2]=e^2-e.
\]

Convergence is guaranteed because

\[
\mathbb P(S_n\le t)\le \frac{t^n}{n!},
\]

and \(\sum_{n\ge 0}t^n/n!<\infty\).
Nemotron 3 Ultra (free)

Requested model: nvidia/nemotron-3-ultra-550b-a55b:free · Output budget: 65536 tokens · Requested reasoning: provider default · Sep 28, 2026, 08:24 UTC

Reported answer cost: $0.0000000000 USD. Excludes retry and judging costs.

**1. Definitions and Reduction to Tail Probabilities**

Let \(X_1,X_2,\dots\) be i.i.d. \(\operatorname{Uniform}(0,1)\) random variables and \(S_n = \sum_{i=1}^n X_i\). Define  
\[
N = \min\{ n \ge 1 : S_n > 2\}.
\]
Because each \(X_i > 0\) almost surely, \(S_n\) is strictly increasing a.s. Hence  
\[
\{N > n\} = \{S_1 \le 2, \dots, S_n \le 2\} = \{S_n \le 2\} \qquad (n\ge 1),
\]
and \(\{N > 0\}\) is the whole space (probability 1). For a non‑negative integer‑valued variable,
\[
\mathbb{E}[N] = \sum_{n=0}^\infty \mathbb{P}(N > n),\qquad 
\mathbb{E}[N^2] = \sum_{n=0}^\infty (2n+1)\,\mathbb{P}(N > n).
\]
These identities follow from Fubini’s theorem (or summation by parts) and are valid because the sums converge absolutely.

**2. Volume of the Simplex: Computing \(\mathbb{P}(S_n \le 2)\)**

The vector \((X_1,\dots,X_n)\) is uniformly distributed on the unit cube \([0,1]^n\). The probability \(\mathbb{P}(S_n \le 2)\) equals the volume of the intersection of the cube with the half‑space \(\sum_{i=1}^n x_i \le 2\).

Using inclusion–exclusion, start with the simplex \(\{x_i \ge 0,\; \sum x_i \le 2\}\) whose volume is \(2^n/n!\). For each subset of \(k\) coordinates force them to exceed 1; the remaining sum is \(\le 2-k\). This gives the Irwin–Hall formula:
\[
\mathbb{P}(S_n \le 2) = \frac{1}{n!}\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor 2\rfloor} (-1)^k \binom{n}{k} (2-k)^n.
\]
For \(n\ge 1\) we have \(\lfloor 2\rfloor = 2\). The term for \(k=2\) is \(\binom{n}{2}\cdot 0^n = 0\). Therefore
\[
\mathbb{P}(S_n \le 2) = \frac{2^n - n}{n!},\qquad n\ge 1.
\]
For \(n=0\) we set \(S_0=0\) and \(\mathbb{P}(S_0\le 2)=1\); the formula \(\frac{2^0-0}{0!}=1\) also holds if we interpret \(0^0=1\).

*Boundary cases:* \(n=1\) gives \(2^1-1=1\), correct because \(X_1\le 1<2\). \(n=2\) gives \(4-2=2\), and \(2/2!=1\), correct because \(S_2\le 2\) always. For \(n\ge 3\) the probability is \(<1\), as expected.

**Why strict vs. non‑strict inequality does not matter:**  
For every \(n\ge 1\), \(S_n\) has a continuous density (it is a convolution of continuous densities). Thus \(\mathbb{P}(S_n = 2) = 0\). Consequently \(\mathbb{P}(S_n < 2) = \mathbb{P}(S_n \le 2)\) and \(\mathbb{P}(S_n > 2) = \mathbb{P}(S_n \ge 2)\) a.s. The definition of \(N\) using \(>\) or \(\ge\) leads to the same distribution.

**3. Expectation \(\mathbb{E}[N]\)**

\[
\mathbb{E}[N] = \sum_{n=0}^\infty \mathbb{P}(S_n \le 2)
= 1 + \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{2^n - n}{n!}
= \sum_{n=0}^\infty \frac{2^n}{n!} - \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{n}{n!}.
\]
Both series converge absolutely (their terms are bounded by \(2^n/n!\) and \(n/n!\) respectively). We evaluate:
\[
\sum_{n=0}^\infty \frac{2^n}{n!} = e^2,\qquad
\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{n}{n!} = \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{1}{(n-1)!} = e.
\]
Hence
\[
\boxed{\mathbb{E}[N] = e^2 - e \approx 4.670774.}
\]

**4. Second Moment and Variance**

\[
\mathbb{E}[N^2] = \sum_{n=0}^\infty (2n+1)\,\mathbb{P}(S_n \le 2)
= \sum_{n=0}^\infty (2n+1)\frac{2^n - n}{n!}.
\]
Split into two sums:
\[
S_a = \sum_{n=0}^\infty (2n+1)\frac{2^n}{n!},\qquad
S_b = \sum_{n=0}^\infty (2n+1)\frac{n}{n!}.
\]
For \(S_a\):
\[
S_a = 2\sum_{n=0}^\infty n\frac{2^n}{n!} + \sum_{n=0}^\infty \frac{2^n}{n!}
= 2\left(2\sum_{m=0}^\infty \frac{2^m}{m!}\right) + e^2
= 4e^2 + e^2 = 5e^2.
\]
For \(S_b\) (note the \(n=0\) term is zero):
\[
S_b = \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{2n^2+n}{n!}
= 2\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{n}{(n-1)!} + \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{1}{(n-1)!}
= 2\sum_{m=0}^\infty \frac{m+1}{m!} + e
= 2\left(\sum_{m=1}^\infty \frac{1}{(m-1)!} + e\right) + e
= 2(e + e) + e = 5e.
\]
Thus
\[
\mathbb{E}[N^2] = S_a - S_b = 5e^2 - 5e = 5e(e-1).
\]
Finally,
\[
\operatorname{Var}(N) = \mathbb{E}[N^2] - (\mathbb{E}[N])^2
= 5e(e-1) - \bigl(e^2 - e\bigr)^2
= e(e-1)\bigl[5 - e(e-1)\bigr].
\]
Numerically,
\[
e \approx 2.71828,\quad e^2 \approx 7.38906,\quad e(e-1) \approx 4.67077,
\]
\[
\operatorname{Var}(N) \approx 4.67077 \times (5 - 4.67077) \approx 1.5378.
\]
(Equivalently, \(-e^4 + 2e^3 + 4e^2 - 5e \approx 1.5378\).)

**5. Generalization to Arbitrary Threshold \(t>0\)**

Let \(N_t = \min\{ n \ge 1 : S_n > t\}\). As before, \(\{N_t > n\} = \{S_n \le t\}\) a.s. The Irwin–Hall CDF gives for \(n\ge 1\)
\[
\mathbb{P}(S_n \le t) = \frac{1}{n!}\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor} (-1)^k \binom{n}{k} (t-k)^n,
\]
and \(\mathbb{P}(S_0 \le t)=1\). Then
\[
\mathbb{E}[N_t] = \sum_{n=0}^\infty \mathbb{P}(S_n \le t)
= 1 + \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{1}{n!}\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor} (-1)^k \binom{n}{k} (t-k)^n.
\]
Interchanging the finite sum over \(k\) with the absolutely convergent series over \(n\) (bounded by \(\sum t^n/n! = e^t\)) and using \(\binom{n}{k}/n! = 1/(k!\,(n-k)!)\):
\[
\mathbb{E}[N_t] = 1 + \sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor} (-1)^k \sum_{n=k}^\infty \frac{(t-k)^n}{k!\,(n-k)!}.
\]
For \(k=0\) the inner sum is \(\sum_{n=1}^\infty t^n/n! = e^t - 1\). For \(k\ge 1\) let \(m=n-k\):
\[
\sum_{n=k}^\infty \frac{(t-k)^n}{k!\,(n-k)!}
= \frac{(t-k)^k}{k!} \sum_{m=0}^\infty \frac{(t-k)^m}{m!}
= \frac{(t-k)^k}{k!}\, e^{t-k}.
\]
Adding the \(k=0\) term \(e^t\) (which equals \(1 + (e^t-1)\)) we obtain the compact formula:
\[
\boxed{\mathbb{E}[N_t] = \sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor} (-1)^k \frac{(t-k)^k}{k!}\, e^{t-k}.}
\]
*Checks:*  
- \(t=1\): \(\lfloor1\rfloor=1\), sum \(= e^1 - 0 = e\).  
- \(t=2\): sum \(= e^2 - 1^1/1!\, e^1 + 0 = e^2 - e\).  
- \(0 < t < 1\): \(\lfloor t\rfloor=0\), sum \(= e^t\), which matches the known result that \(\mathbb{P}(S_n \le t)=t^n/n!\) for \(t\le 1\).

All steps are exact, use only elementary probability, inclusion–exclusion, and series manipulation. The final expressions for \(\mathbb{E}[N]\) and \(\operatorname{Var}(N)\) are closed forms in terms of \(e\).
Ling 3.0 Flash

Requested model: inclusionai/ling-3.0-flash · Output budget: 32768 tokens · Requested reasoning: provider default · Sep 28, 2026, 08:25 UTC

Reported answer cost: $0.0008661030 USD. Excludes retry and judging costs.

We are given independent random variables \(X_1,X_2,\dots\sim\text{Uniform}(0,1)\), their partial sums \(S_n = \sum_{i=1}^n X_i\) (with \(S_0=0\)), and the stopping time  
\[
N = \min\{n\ge 1 : S_n > 2\}.
\]
We derive closed forms for \(\mathbb{E}[N]\) and \(\operatorname{Var}(N)\) using inclusion–exclusion on simplex volumes, tail‑sum identities, and then generalise to an arbitrary threshold \(t>0\).

---

### 1.  The probability \(\mathbb{P}(S_n \le 2)\) by inclusion–exclusion

For a fixed \(n\), the vector \((X_1,\dots,X_n)\) is uniformly distributed on the unit cube \([0,1]^n\).  Hence \(\mathbb{P}(S_n \le t)\) equals the volume of the region  
\[
A_n(t) = \bigl\{x\in[0,1]^n : x_1+\cdots+x_n \le t\bigr\}.
\]
Let \(B_n(t) = \{x\ge 0 : \sum x_i \le t\}\) be the unrestricted simplex; its volume is \(t^n/n!\).  To restrict each coordinate to \([0,1]\), we remove the parts where some \(x_i>1\).  By the inclusion–exclusion principle, for \(t\ge 0\),
\[
\operatorname{Vol}\bigl(A_n(t)\bigr) = \frac{1}{n!}\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^k\binom{n}{k}\bigl((t-k)_+\bigr)^n,
\]
where \((u)_+ = \max(u,0)\).  The sum effectively stops at \(k = \lfloor t\rfloor\).

Take \(t=2\).  Then \(\lfloor t\rfloor = 2\), so
\[
\mathbb{P}(S_n\le 2) = \frac{1}{n!}\Bigl(2^n - \binom{n}{1}1^n + \binom{n}{2}0^n\Bigr).
\]
For \(n=0\) we interpret the empty sum as giving probability \(1\).  For \(n\ge 1\), the term with \(k=2\) vanishes because \(0^n=0\).  Thus for all \(n\ge 0\),
\[
p_n \;:=\; \mathbb{P}(S_n\le 2) \;=\; \frac{2^n - n}{n!}.
\]
(Check: \(p_0=1\), \(p_1=1\), \(p_2=1\), \(p_3=5/6\), \(p_4=1/2\), matching the Irwin–Hall CDF.)

---

### 2.  Strict vs. non‑strict threshold

Because the \(X_i\) are continuous, \(S_n\) has a density; therefore \(\mathbb{P}(S_n = 2) = 0\) for every \(n\).  Consequently \(\{S_n > 2\}\) and \(\{S_n \ge 2\}\) differ only by a null set, so the strict inequality in the definition of \(N\) is probabilistically equivalent to a non‑strict one.  No adjustment is needed.

---

### 3.  Tail‑sum identities for the moments

Since \(S_n\) is strictly increasing, the event \(N > n\) is exactly \(\{S_n \le 2\}\) (with \(S_0=0\le 2\)).  For any non‑negative integer‑valued random variable,
\[
\mathbb{E}[N] = \sum_{n=0}^\infty \mathbb{P}(N > n),\qquad
\mathbb{E}[N^2] = \sum_{n=0}^\infty (2n+1)\,\mathbb{P}(N > n).
\]
Hence
\[
\mathbb{E}[N] = \sum_{n=0}^\infty p_n,\qquad
\mathbb{E}[N^2] = \sum_{n=0}^\infty (2n+1)p_n.
\]
The terms \(p_n \sim 2^n/n!\) decay super‑exponentially, guaranteeing absolute convergence of all sums.

---

### 4.  Evaluation of \(\mathbb{E}[N]\) and \(\mathbb{E}[N^2]\)

Split the expectation:
\[
\mathbb{E}[N] = \sum_{n=0}^\infty \frac{2^n}{n!} - \sum_{n=0}^\infty \frac{n}{n!}
= e^2 - e.
\]
For the second moment we first need \(\sum n p_n\):
\[
\sum_{n=0}^\infty n\,\frac{2^n - n}{n!}
= \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{2^n}{(n-1)!} - \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{n}{(n-1)!}
= 2\sum_{m=0}^\infty \frac{2^m}{m!} - \sum_{m=0}^\infty \frac{m+1}{m!}
= 2e^2 - (e+e) = 2e^2 - 2e.
\]
Then
\[
\mathbb{E}[N^2] = 2(2e^2 - 2e) + (e^2 - e) = 5e^2 - 5e.
\]
The variance is
\[
\operatorname{Var}(N) = \mathbb{E}[N^2] - \bigl(\mathbb{E}[N]\bigr)^2
= 5e^2 - 5e - (e^2 - e)^2
= e(e-1)\bigl(5 + e - e^2\bigr).
\]

**Numerical sanity check:**  \(e \approx 2.71828\), so \(e^2 \approx 7.38906\).  
\(\mathbb{E}[N] \approx 4.6708\), \(\operatorname{Var}(N) \approx 1.5377\).  The mean is slightly above the naive estimate \(2/0.5 = 4\), and the variance is moderate, consistent with the known behaviour of uniform sums.

---

### 5.  Generalisation to an arbitrary threshold \(t>0\)

Define \(N_t = \min\{n\ge 1 : S_n > t\}\).  By the same tail‑sum argument,
\[
\mathbb{E}[N_t] = \sum_{n=0}^\infty \mathbb{P}(S_n \le t)
= \sum_{n=0}^\infty \frac{1}{n!}\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor} (-1)^k\binom{n}{k}(t-k)^n.
\]
Swap the order of summation (absolute convergence justifies this):
\[
\mathbb{E}[N_t] = \sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor} (-1)^k
\sum_{n=k}^\infty \frac{1}{n!}\binom{n}{k}(t-k)^n.
\]
The inner sum simplifies:
\[
\sum_{n=k}^\infty \frac{1}{n!}\frac{n!}{k!\,(n-k)!}(t-k)^n
= \frac{1}{k!}\sum_{m=0}^\infty \frac{(t-k)^{m+k}}{m!}
= \frac{(t-k)^k}{k!}\,e^{t-k}.
\]
Therefore
\[
\boxed{\mathbb{E}[N_t] = \sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor} (-1)^k\,\frac{(t-k)^k}{k!}\,e^{t-k}}.
\]
This finite sum involves \(\lfloor t\rfloor\) and exponentials.  For \(t=2\) it gives \(e^2 - e\); for \(t=1\) it gives \(e\), recovering the classical results.

---

### 6.  Why strict vs. non‑strict does not matter here

As noted in §2, the uniform distribution is continuous, so \(S_n\) has no atoms.  For every \(n\) and every real \(t\), \(\mathbb{P}(S_n = t) = 0\).  Thus the events \(\{S_n > t\}\) and \(\{S_n \ge t\}\) are almost surely identical, and the stopping time \(N\) (and its moments) is unaffected by the choice of inequality.  This is a special feature of continuous distributions; for discrete sums the distinction would be essential.
GPT-6 Sol

Requested model: openai/gpt-6-sol · Output budget: 65536 tokens · Requested reasoning: provider default · Sep 28, 2026, 08:17 UTC

Reported answer cost: $0.0279820000 USD. Excludes retry and judging costs.

Let \(F_n(t)=\Pr(S_n\le t)\), with \(S_0=0\). The empty sum is deterministic, so \(F_0(2)=1\). Also \(F_1(2)=1\), since \(X_1<1\), and \(F_2(2)=1\), since \(X_1+X_2<2\).

For \(n\ge1\), \(F_n(2)\) is the volume of
\[
\{(x_1,\ldots,x_n)\in[0,1]^n:x_1+\cdots+x_n\le2\}.
\]
Without the upper bounds \(x_i\le1\), the simplex has volume \(2^n/n!\). For each chosen set of \(j\) coordinates constrained to exceed \(1\), translate those coordinates down by \(1\). The remaining simplex has volume \((2-j)^n/n!\) if \(j<2\), and zero volume if \(j\ge2\). Inclusion–exclusion therefore gives, for every \(n\ge1\),
\[
F_n(2)=\frac1{n!}\sum_{j=0}^{n}(-1)^j\binom nj(2-j)_+^n
      =\frac{2^n-n}{n!}.                                      \tag{1}
\]
In particular, (1) gives \(F_1(2)=1\) and \(F_2(2)=1\). The \(n=0\) case was handled separately; applying the volume formula there would introduce an unnecessary \(0^0\) convention. Simplex boundaries have zero volume, so using \(<\) rather than \(\le\) in the volume calculation changes nothing.

Because the increments are positive, \(N>n\) exactly when \(S_n\le2\). Equation (1) also bounds \(F_n(2)\le2^n/n!\), so \(\Pr(N=\infty)=\lim_nF_n(2)=0\), and the moment sums below converge. For each positive integer \(N\),
\[
N=\sum_{n=0}^\infty\mathbf1_{\{N>n\}},
\qquad
N^2=\sum_{n=0}^\infty(2n+1)\mathbf1_{\{N>n\}}.
\]
Taking expectations is justified by Tonelli’s theorem. Using \(F_0(2)=1\) and (1),
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb E[N]
&=1+\sum_{n=1}^\infty\frac{2^n-n}{n!}\\
&=1+(e^2-1)-e
=\boxed{e^2-e}.
\end{aligned}
\]
Likewise, the exponential-series identities
\[
\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{n\,2^n}{n!}=2e^2,\qquad
\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{n}{n!}=e,\qquad
\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{n^2}{n!}=2e
\]
give
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb E[N^2]
&=1+\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{(2n+1)(2^n-n)}{n!}\\
&=1+\bigl(4e^2+e^2-1\bigr)-(4e+e)\\
&=5(e^2-e).
\end{aligned}
\]
Thus
\[
\boxed{\operatorname{Var}(N)
=5(e^2-e)-(e^2-e)^2
=(e^2-e)(5-e^2+e)}.
\]
Numerically, \(\mathbb E[N]\approx4.67077427\) and \(\operatorname{Var}(N)\approx1.53773907\). As a check on the scale, \(N\ge3\) surely, while (1) gives \(\Pr(N=3)=1-F_3(2)=1/6\).

For an arbitrary \(t>0\), put \(m=\lfloor t\rfloor\). The same simplex argument gives, for \(n\ge1\),
\[
F_n(t)=\frac1{n!}\sum_{k=0}^{\min(n,m)}
 (-1)^k\binom nk(t-k)^n,\qquad F_0(t)=1.
\]
The simplex bound \(F_n(t)\le t^n/n!\) ensures convergence. Summing over \(n\) and using
\[
\sum_{n=k}^\infty\frac{\binom nk a^n}{n!}
=\frac{a^k e^a}{k!}
\]
yields the finite expression
\[
\boxed{\displaystyle
\mathbb E[N_t]
=\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}
 (-1)^k\frac{(t-k)^k}{k!}e^{\,t-k}}.
\]
The \(k=0\) factor is \(1\). If \(t\) is an integer, its final term has \(k=t>0\) and is zero.

Finally, for every \(n\ge1\), \(S_n\) has a continuous distribution, so \(\Pr(S_n=t)=0\). Strict and non-strict threshold events therefore have the same probability; the non-strict event \(S_n\le t\) is nevertheless the exact event corresponding to \(N_t>n\).

Reported answer cost: $0.0022066000 USD. Excludes retry and judging costs.

Let \(S_0=0\). For \(n\ge1\), the event \(S_n\le t\) is the part of the simplex
\[
\{x_i\ge0:\ x_1+\cdots+x_n\le t\}
\]
that lies inside the unit cube. The full simplex has volume \(t^n/n!\). To impose \(x_i\le1\), use inclusion-exclusion on the events \(x_i>1\): after shifting \(k\) selected coordinates down by \(1\), the remaining simplex has volume \((t-k)_+^n/n!\), where \(u_+=\max(u,0)\). Thus
\[
\boxed{\displaystyle
\Pr(S_n\le t)=\frac1{n!}\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^k\binom nk(t-k)_+^n,\qquad n\ge1.}
\]
Equivalently, only \(k\le\lfloor t\rfloor\) contribute, with terms \(k>n\) omitted.

The case \(n=0\) must be treated separately: \(S_0=0\), so \(\Pr(S_0\le t)=1\) for \(t>0\). For \(n=1,t=2\), the formula gives \(2-(2-1)=1\), as it should. At integer \(t\), a term with \(k=t\) is zero for \(n\ge1\); there is no \(0^0\) term in this formula.

For \(t=2\), inclusion-exclusion gives, for every \(n\ge1\),
\[
p_n:=\Pr(S_n\le2)
=\frac{2^n-n}{n!}.
\]
Indeed, the \(k=0\) and \(k=1\) terms are \(2^n\) and \(-n\); the \(k=2\) term vanishes, and terms \(k>2\) have zero positive part. In particular \(p_1=p_2=1\). Also \(p_0=1\).

Because the increments are nonnegative, \(N>n\) exactly when \(S_n\le2\). For any positive integer-valued \(N\), the pointwise identities
\[
N=\sum_{n\ge0}\mathbf 1_{\{N>n\}},\qquad
N^2=\sum_{n\ge0}(2n+1)\mathbf 1_{\{N>n\}}
\]
hold. Taking expectations (by monotone convergence) gives the tail-sum formulas. The sums below converge: their terms are bounded by a polynomial in \(n\) times \(2^n/n!\), which is summable.

Therefore
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb E[N]
&=1+\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{2^n-n}{n!}\\
&=1+(e^2-1)-e
=\boxed{e^2-e}
\approx \boxed{4.6707742705}.
\end{aligned}
\]
For the second moment,
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb E[N^2]
&=1+\sum_{n\ge1}(2n+1)\frac{2^n-n}{n!}\\
&=1+\bigl(5e^2-1\bigr)-5e
=\boxed{5(e^2-e)}
\approx \boxed{23.3538713524},
\end{aligned}
\]
where
\[
\sum_{n\ge1}(2n+1)\frac{2^n}{n!}=5e^2-1,\qquad
\sum_{n\ge1}(2n+1)\frac{n}{n!}=5e.
\]
Hence
\[
\boxed{\operatorname{Var}(N)=5(e^2-e)-(e^2-e)^2
\approx 1.5377390667.}
\]
As a quick check, the tail probabilities begin \(p_0=p_1=p_2=1\), \(p_3=5/6\), and \(p_4=1/2\), consistent with the computed mean.

For strict versus non-strict thresholds, \(N>n\) uses \(S_n\le2\) because crossing is defined by \(S_n>2\). For \(n\ge1\), the event \(S_n=2\) has probability zero: it lies on a hyperplane of volume zero. Thus \(<\) and \(\le\) give the same probability here. At \(n=0\), \(S_0=0\ne2\).

More generally, for \(t>0\), \(N_t>n\) exactly when \(S_n\le t\). Summing the simplex formula over \(n\ge0\), with the \(n=0\) term handled separately, yields
\[
\boxed{\displaystyle
\mathbb E[N_t]
=e^t+\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}
\frac{(-1)^k e^{\,t-k}(t-k)^k}{k!}.}
\]
To see the summation, the \(k=0\) contribution for \(n\ge1\) is \(e^t-1\); for \(k\ge1\), writing \(n=k+j\) gives
\[
\sum_{n\ge k}\binom nk\frac{(t-k)^n}{n!}
=\frac{(t-k)^k}{k!}e^{t-k}.
\]
Only finitely many \(k\) contribute, and each exponential series converges. If \(t\) is an integer, the last displayed summand is zero.
Solar Pro 4

Requested model: upstage/solar-pro4 · Output budget: 65536 tokens · Requested reasoning: provider default · Sep 28, 2026, 08:26 UTC

Reported answer cost: $0.0014466600 USD. Excludes retry and judging costs.

We consider \(X_1,X_2,\dots \overset{\text{iid}}{\sim} \text{Uniform}(0,1)\) and \(S_n = \sum_{k=1}^n X_k\) with \(S_0=0\).  
Define \(N = \min\{n\ge 1: S_n > 2\}\). Because each \(X_k > 0\) a.s., the increments are strictly positive, so the walk is strictly increasing; this will make strict vs non-strict threshold events coincide almost surely except at \(t\) equal to an integer when using the indicator boundary, but for \(t=2\) and continuous uniforms the boundary has measure zero.

---

### 1. Distribution of \(S_n\) and \(P(S_n\le 2)\)

The sum of \(n\) independent \(\text{Uniform}(0,1)\) has the Irwin–Hall density
\[
f_{S_n}(x) = \frac{1}{(n-1)!} \sum_{j=0}^{n} (-1)^{j}\binom{n}{j} (x-j)_{+}^{\,n-1},\quad x\ge 0,
\]
where \((y)_+=\max(y,0)\).  
Equivalently, \(P(S_n\le x)\) is the volume of the simplex \(\{y_i\ge 0,\,\sum y_i \le x\}\) clipped to the unit cube, obtained via inclusion–exclusion over the constraints \(y_i\le 1\):
\[
P(S_n\le x)=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor x\rfloor} (-1)^j \binom{n}{j} (x-j)^n_+.
\]
For \(x=2\) we restrict \(j\in\{0,1,2\}\). Carefully handling small \(n\):

* \(n=0\): \(S_0=0\) so \(P(S_0\le 2)=1\) — used later in sums.
* \(n=1\): \(P(S_1\le 2)=P(X_1\le 2)=1\) because \(X_1\le 1<2\).
* \(n\ge 2\): the formula gives a polynomial.
  - \(n=2\): \(P(S_2\le 2)=\frac{1}{2!}[1\cdot 2^2 - 2\cdot 1^2 + 1\cdot 0^2] = 1\). Indeed \(S_2\le 2\) always since \(X_i\le1\).
  - \(n\ge 3\): the \(j=2\) term \((2-2)^n=0\) vanishes, so
    \[
    P(S_n\le 2)=\frac{1}{n!}\Big[2^n - n\,1^n\Big] = \frac{2^n-n}{n!},\qquad n\ge 3.
    \]
This holds for \(n=2\) as well? Check: \(\frac{2^2-2}{2!}=1\) — yes. For \(n=1\): \(\frac{2-1}{1!}=1\) — yes. So a unified expression for \(n\ge1\) is
\[
P(S_n\le 2)=\frac{2^n-n}{n!},\quad n\ge1,\quad P(S_0\le 2)=1.
\]
For \(n\ge 2^n\) would give negative? Irrelevant; for \(n\ge3\) we have \(2^n\ge 8 > n\) so it’s positive. For large \(n\), \(P(S_n\le 2)\sim 2^n/n!\) rapidly decays, hence all tail sums converge.

---

### 2. Tail‑sum identities for moments (strict vs non‑strict)

\(N\) is a nonnegative integer r.v. (with \(N\ge1\) a.s.). For any integer‑valued \(T\ge 0\) we have the standard identities
\[
E[T]=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty} P(T>k),\qquad 
E[T^2]=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty} (2k+1) P(T>k).
\]
Here \(T=N\). The event \(\{N>k\}\) means the process has **not** exceeded the threshold by index \(k\), i.e. \(S_k\le 2\). Because \(S_k\) has a continuous distribution, the boundary event \(S_k=2\) has probability zero, so \(P(S_k\le 2)=P(S_k<2)=P(N>k)\). Therefore strict vs non‑strict threshold does **not** affect the probabilities here — we may safely use the CDF.

Thus for \(t=2\):
\[
E[N]=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty} P(S_k\le 2),\qquad
E[N^2]=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty} (2k+1) P(S_k\le 2).
\]
Note \(k=0\): \(S_0=0\le 2\) so \(P(N>0)=1\); the sums start at \(k=0\).

---

### 3. Compute \(E[N]\)

Split \(k=0,1,2\) and use the formula for \(k\ge3\):
\[
E[N] = 1 + 1 + 1 + \sum_{k=3}^{\infty} \frac{2^k-k}{k!}.
\]
Since the formula also works for \(k=1,2\) we can rewrite:
\[
E[N] = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \frac{2^k-k}{k!} - \text{(adjust }k=0\text{ term)}.
\]
At \(k=0\): \(\frac{2^0-0}{0!}=1\) matches. At \(k=1\): \((2-1)/1!=1\) matches. So indeed
\[
E[N] = \sum_{k=0}^\infty \frac{2^k}{k!} - \sum_{k=0}^\infty \frac{k}{k!}.
\]
We have \(\sum_{k=0}^\infty \frac{2^k}{k!} = e^2\).  
For the second sum: \(\frac{k}{k!} = \frac{1}{(k-1)!}\) for \(k\ge1\), and \(k=0\) term is 0. Hence
\[
\sum_{k=0}^\infty \frac{k}{k!} = \sum_{k=1}^\infty \frac{1}{(k-1)!} = e.
\]
Therefore
\[
\boxed{E[N]=e^2 - e.}
\]
Numerically: \(e^2\approx 7.389056\), \(e\approx 2.7182818\), so \(E[N]\approx 4.670774\).  
This makes intuitive sense: each \(X_i\) has mean \(0.5\), so to exceed 2 we expect about \(4\) steps; the overshoot and randomness push it slightly higher.

---

### 4. Compute \(\operatorname{Var}(N)\)

We need \(E[N^2]\). Use
\[
E[N^2] = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} (2k+1) P(S_k\le 2).
\]
Again treat \(k\) via the unified polynomial; for \(k=0\): \((2\cdot0+1)\cdot1 =1\). Formula \(\frac{2^k-k}{k!}\) also gives 1 for \(k=0\), so we can sum from \(k=0\) with the expression (checking \(k=1\): \((3)(1)=3\), formula gives \((2-1)/1!=1\) — wait discrepancy: for \(k=1\), \(P(S_1\le2)=1\), but \((2^1-1)/1! =1\), yes ok.) So
\[
E[N^2] = \sum_{k=0}^\infty (2k+1)\frac{2^k-k}{k!}.
\]
Expand:
\[
= 2\sum_{k=0}^\infty \frac{k(2^k-k)}{k!} + \sum_{k=0}^\infty \frac{2^k-k}{k!}.
\]
The second sum is \(e^2-e\). The first sum:
\[
\sum_{k=0}^\infty \frac{k 2^k}{k!} - \sum_{k=0}^\infty \frac{k^2}{k!}.
\]
* First part: for \(k\ge0\), \(\frac{k 2^k}{k!} = \frac{2^k}{(k-1)!}\) (with \(k=0\) term 0). Shift index \(m=k-1\):
  \[
  \sum_{k=1}^\infty \frac{2^k}{(k-1)!} = 2\sum_{m=0}^\infty \frac{2^m}{m!} = 2e^2.
  \]
* Second part: \(\frac{k^2}{k!} = \frac{k}{(k-1)!}\) for \(k\ge1\). Write \(k = (k-1)+1\):
  \[
  \sum_{k=1}^\infty \frac{k}{(k-1)!} = \sum_{k=1}^\infty \frac{k-1}{(k-1)!} + \sum_{k=1}^\infty \frac{1}{(k-1)!}.
  \]
  The first piece: \(\sum_{k=2}^\infty \frac{1}{(k-2)!} = e\). The second piece: \(e\). So total = \(2e\).
Thus
\[
\sum_{k=0}^\infty \frac{k(2^k-k)}{k!} = 2e^2 - 2e.
\]
Multiply by \(2\):
\[
2(2e^2 - 2e) = 4e^2 - 4e.
\]
Add the remaining sum \(e^2 - e\):
\[
E[N^2] = (4e^2 - 4e) + (e^2 - e) = 5e^2 - 5e.
\]
So
\[
\boxed{\operatorname{Var}(N) = E[N^2] - (E[N])^2 = 5e^2 - 5e - (e^2 - e)^2.}
\]
Expand \((e^2-e)^2 = e^4 - 2e^3 + e^2\). Then
\[
\operatorname{Var}(N) = 5e^2 - 5e - e^4 + 2e^3 - e^2 = -e^4 + 2e^3 + 4e^2 - 5e.
\]
Numerically: \(e\approx 2.7182818\), \(e^2\approx7.389056\), \(e^3\approx20.085537\), \(e^4\approx54.598150\).  
Then \(\operatorname{Var}(N)\approx -54.598150 + 40.171074 + 29.556224 - 13.591409 \approx 1.537739\).  
Sanity: E[N]≈4.67, variance≈1.54, coefficient of variation ≈0.27, reasonable for a renewal count near threshold 2.

---

### 5. Generalization for threshold \(t>0\)

Let \(N_t = \min\{n\ge 1: S_n > t\}\). By the same strict‑monotonic argument and continuous distribution, \(P(N_t > k) = P(S_k \le t) = P(S_k < t)\) a.s. Hence
\[
E[N_t] = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} P(S_k \le t).
\]
Using the Irwin–Hall CDF:
\[
P(S_k \le t) = \frac{1}{k!} \sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor} (-1)^j \binom{k}{j} (t-j)^k_+,
\]
where we interpret \(\binom{k}{j}=0\) if \(j>k\), and the \(k=0\) case handled separately. Including \(k=0\) with \(P(S_0\le t)=1\), and for \(k\ge1\) the sum over \(j\) stops at \(\min(\lfloor t\rfloor, k)\) automatically because for \(j>k\) the binomial coefficient is zero. Thus
\[
\boxed{E[N_t] = 1 + \sum_{k=1}^{\infty} \frac{1}{k!} \sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor} (-1)^j \binom{k}{j} (t-j)^k.}
\]
For fixed \(t\), the sum is finite in \(j\) and infinite in \(k\); convergence is guaranteed because the terms are \(\sim (t-j)^k/k!\) and \(\sum_k (t-j)^k/k! = e^{t-j}\), giving a finite total. Rearranging:
\[
E[N_t] = 1 + \sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor} (-1)^j \sum_{k=1}^{\infty} \frac{1}{k!}\binom{k}{j} (t-j)^k.
\]
Since \(\frac{1}{k!}\binom{k}{j} = \frac{1}{j!(k-j)!}\) for \(k\ge j\) and zero otherwise,
\[
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty} \frac{1}{k!}\binom{k}{j}(t-j)^k = \frac{1}{j!} \sum_{k=j}^{\infty} \frac{(t-j)^k}{(k-j)!} = \frac{(t-j)^j}{j!} e^{t-j}.
\]
Thus a closed form involving a finite sum:
\[
\boxed{E[N_t] = 1 + \sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor} (-1)^j \frac{(t-j)^j}{j!}\, e^{t-j}.}
\]
For \(t=2\): \(\lfloor t\rfloor=2\).  
\(j=0\): \( (2)^0/0!\, e^{2} = e^2\).  
\(j=1\): \((-1)^1 (1)^1/1!\, e^{1} = -e\).  
\(j=2\): \((-1)^2 (0)^2/2!\, e^{0} = 0\).  
Add the leading \(1\): \(1 + e^2 - e\), which matches our earlier \(E[N]=e^2-e+?\) Wait earlier \(E[N]=e^2-e\). Indeed the leading 1 is already embedded? Let’s check: the formula yields \(1+e^2-e\). But we computed \(E[N]=e^2-e\). Discrepancy of 1. Let’s re-evaluate: For t=2, \(E[N]=\sum_{k=0}^\infty P(S_k\le 2)\). \(k=0\) term =1. The sum over \(k\ge1\) gave \(e^2 - e - 1\)? Let’s recompute carefully: earlier we had \(E[N] = 1 + 1 + 1 + \sum_{k=3}^\infty \frac{2^k-k}{k!}\). Evaluate full sum: \(\sum_{k=0}^\infty \frac{2^k-k}{k!} = e^2 - e\). But that sum *includes* \(k=0\) term = 1, \(k=1\) term = 1, \(k=2\) term = 1. So total sum from \(k=0\) is \(e^2-e\). Indeed \(E[N] = e^2-e\). In the generalized formula, \(1 + \sum_{k=1}^\infty = 1 + (e^2 - e - 1) = e^2 - e\) because \(\sum_{k=1}^\infty = (e^2-e) - 1\). Let’s check: \(\sum_{k=0}^\infty \frac{2^k-k}{k!} = e^2 - e\). Subtract \(k=0\) term (which is 1) gives \(e^2 - e -1\). So the formula with the leading 1 is correct: \(1 + (e^2-e-1) = e^2-e\). Good — our generalized expression is correct.

So \(E[N_t]\) is expressed as a finite alternating sum over \(j\) up to \(\lfloor t\rfloor\), involving exponentials. For integer \(t=m\),
\[
E[N_m] = 1 + \sum_{j=0}^{m} (-1)^j \frac{(m-j)^j}{j!} e^{m-j}.
\]
The second moment \(E[N_t^2]\) can be written similarly:
\[
E[N_t^2] = \sum_{k=0}^\infty (2k+1) P(S_k\le t),
\]
yielding a finite‑\(j\) sum with terms \((2k+1)\frac{(t-j)^k}{j!(k-j)!}\); sum over \(k\) gives contributions proportional to \((t-j)^j e^{t-j}\) and \((t-j)^{j+1} e^{t-j}\). This is a finite sum for any fixed \(t\).

---

### 6. Remarks on strict vs non‑strict and convergence

- **Strictness**: Because \(S_k\) is continuous, \(P(S_k = t)=0\) for all \(k\). Hence \(\{N_t > k\} = \{S_k \le t\}\) has the same probability as \(\{S_k < t\}\). The threshold strictness does not affect the distribution of \(N_t\) or its moments.
- **Convergence**: For any fixed \(t\), \(P(S_k\le t) \sim \frac{t^k}{k!}\) as \(k\to\infty\) (since the inclusion–exclusion sum is dominated by the \(j=0\) exponential series). The series for \(E[N_t]\) converges like \(\sum t^k/k! = e^t\). For \(E[N_t^2]\), \((2k+1) t^k/k!\) also converges. Thus all quantities are finite and well‑defined.

**Numerical sanity (t=2)**:
- \(E[N] \approx 4.670774\),
- \(\operatorname{Var}(N) \approx 1.53774\),
- Standard deviation ≈ 1.240.  
A quick simulation mental check: Uniform(0,1) mean 0.5, so to reach >2 we expect around 4–5 steps; the computed mean 4.67 and modest variance are plausible. The closed‑form constants \(e^2-e\) and \(-e^4+2e^3+4e^2-5e\) provide exact rational combinations of exponentials.

---
Gemini 3.1 Pro Preview

Requested model: google/gemini-3.1-pro-preview · Output budget: 16384 tokens · Requested reasoning: provider default · Sep 28, 2026, 00:26 UTC

Reported answer cost: $0.1820640000 USD. Excludes retry and judging costs.

### 1. Strict vs. Non-Strict Threshold Events
Let $S_n = X_1 + \dots + X_n$ for $n \ge 1$, and define $S_0 = 0$. We evaluate the stopping time $N = \min\{n \ge 1 : S_n > 2\}$. 
Whether the threshold condition is strict ($S_n > 2$) or non-strict ($S_n \ge 2$) has **no effect** on the probabilities or the expectations. For any $n \ge 1$, $S_n$ is the sum of independent continuous Uniform(0,1) random variables, meaning its distribution is absolutely continuous and holds no point masses. Consequently, the probability of hitting the boundary exactly is zero: $\mathbb{P}(S_n = 2) = 0$. For $n=0$, $S_0 = 0 < 2$ strictly, so it holds under either definition. Thus, we can freely interchange $\mathbb{P}(S_n \le 2)$ and $\mathbb{P}(S_n < 2)$.

### 2. Simplex-Volume Argument for $\mathbb{P}(S_n \le 2)$
To find $\mathbb{P}(S_n \le x)$ for $x=2$, we interpret the probability geometrically. The joint distribution of $(X_1, \dots, X_n)$ is uniform over the unit hypercube $[0,1]^n$, which has a volume of 1. Thus, $\mathbb{P}(S_n \le x)$ is exactly the $n$-dimensional volume of the region $H = \{\mathbf{x} \in [0,1]^n : \sum_{i=1}^n x_i \le x\}$.

We find this volume using the Principle of Inclusion-Exclusion. 
First, consider the unconstrained standard simplex $\Delta_x = \{\mathbf{x} \in \mathbb{R}_{\ge 0}^n : \sum x_i \le x\}$. It is well known that the volume of $\Delta_x$ is $\frac{x^n}{n!}$.
To restrict this to the hypercube, we must subtract the regions where the upper bound $x_i \le 1$ is violated. Let $A_i = \{\mathbf{x} \in \Delta_x : x_i > 1\}$. Using the translation $y_i = x_i - 1$, $A_i$ bijectively maps to $\Delta_{x-1}$. Thus, the volume of $A_i$ is $\frac{(x-1)^n}{n!}$ (if $x \ge 1$, and 0 otherwise). 

By symmetry, the intersection of $k$ such violation events restricts $k$ variables to be $> 1$, translating to a simplex of size $x - k$. Its volume is $\frac{(x-k)^n}{n!}$ if $x \ge k$. Because there are $\binom{n}{k}$ such intersections of $k$ variables, inclusion-exclusion yields the Irwin-Hall distribution:
$$ \mathbb{P}(S_n \le x) = \sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor x \rfloor} (-1)^k \binom{n}{k} \frac{(x-k)^n}{n!} $$
For $x = 2$, the sum operates over $k \in \{0, 1, 2\}$:
$$ \mathbb{P}(S_n \le 2) = \frac{1}{n!} \left[ \binom{n}{0} 2^n - \binom{n}{1} 1^n + \binom{n}{2} 0^n \right] $$
We define $0^0 = 1$ and $0^n = 0$ for $n \ge 1$. 
- For $n \ge 1$, the $k=2$ term is $\binom{n}{2} \cdot 0 = 0$.
- For $n = 0$, $\binom{0}{2} = 0$, so the term is also 0.
Thus, the $k=2$ term vanishes globally, simplifying to:
$$ \mathbb{P}(S_n \le 2) = \frac{2^n - n}{n!} $$
**Checking boundary cases:** 
- For $n=0$: $\mathbb{P}(S_0 \le 2) = (2^0 - 0)/0! = 1$ (since $S_0=0 \le 2$ almost surely).
- For $n=1$: $\mathbb{P}(S_1 \le 2) = (2^1 - 1)/1! = 1$ (since $X_1 \le 1 < 2$ almost surely).
The formula gracefully handles all non-negative integers $n$.

### 3. Tail-Sum Identities & Convergence
For any random variable $N$ taking values in $\{1, 2, 3, \dots\}$, the expected value and second moment can be rewritten using tail probabilities.
Using $n = \sum_{k=0}^{n-1} 1$ and $n^2 = \sum_{k=0}^{n-1} (2k+1)$, we have:
$$ \mathbb{E}[N] = \sum_{n=1}^\infty \sum_{k=0}^{n-1} 1 \cdot \mathbb{P}(N=n), \quad \mathbb{E}[N^2] = \sum_{n=1}^\infty \sum_{k=0}^{n-1} (2k+1) \cdot \mathbb{P}(N=n) $$
Because all terms are non-negative, Tonelli’s Theorem permits exchanging the sums:
$$ \mathbb{E}[N] = \sum_{k=0}^\infty \sum_{n=k+1}^\infty \mathbb{P}(N=n) = \sum_{k=0}^\infty \mathbb{P}(N > k) $$
$$ \mathbb{E}[N^2] = \sum_{k=0}^\infty (2k+1) \sum_{n=k+1}^\infty \mathbb{P}(N=n) = \sum_{k=0}^\infty (2k+1) \mathbb{P}(N > k) $$
The event $\{N > n\}$ occurs if and only if the sum hasn't crossed the threshold by step $n$, meaning $S_n \le 2$. Thus, $\mathbb{P}(N > n) = \mathbb{P}(S_n \le 2)$.

**Convergence:** The sequences for moments require summing $a_n = \mathbb{P}(S_n \le 2) = \frac{2^n-n}{n!}$. By the Ratio Test on the dominant term $\frac{2^n}{n!}$, we observe $a_{n+1}/a_n \approx \frac{2}{n+1} \to 0$ as $n \to \infty$. The factorial decay thoroughly ensures absolute convergence, validating series expansion and sum partitioning in the next steps.

### 4. Evaluating Infinite Sums for $\mathbb{E}[N]$ and $\text{Var}(N)$
By the identity for $\mathbb{E}[N]$,
$$ \mathbb{E}[N] = \sum_{n=0}^\infty \frac{2^n - n}{n!} = \sum_{n=0}^\infty \frac{2^n}{n!} - \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{n}{n!} = e^2 - \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{1}{(n-1)!} = e^2 - e $$
Next, we expand $\mathbb{E}[N^2]$ into two absolutely convergent series:
$$ \mathbb{E}[N^2] = \sum_{n=0}^\infty (2n+1) \frac{2^n}{n!} - \sum_{n=0}^\infty (2n+1) \frac{n}{n!} $$
Let's evaluate the left sum (incorporating $e^x = \sum \frac{x^n}{n!}$):
$$ \sum_{n=0}^\infty \frac{2n 2^n}{n!} + \sum_{n=0}^\infty \frac{2^n}{n!} = 4 \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{2^{n-1}}{(n-1)!} + e^2 = 4e^2 + e^2 = 5e^2 $$
Let's evaluate the right sum:
$$ \sum_{n=0}^\infty \frac{2n^2}{n!} + \sum_{n=0}^\infty \frac{n}{n!} = 2\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{n}{(n-1)!} + e = 2\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(n-1)+1}{(n-1)!} + e $$
$$ = 2 \left( \sum_{n=2}^\infty \frac{1}{(n-2)!} + \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{1}{(n-1)!} \right) + e = 2(e + e) + e = 5e $$
Therefore, $\mathbb{E}[N^2] = 5e^2 - 5e$.

The variance is $\text{Var}(N) = \mathbb{E}[N^2] - \mathbb{E}[N]^2$:
$$ \text{Var}(N) = 5e^2 - 5e - (e^2 - e)^2 = 5e^2 - 5e - (e^4 - 2e^3 + e^2) $$
$$ \text{Var}(N) = -e^4 + 2e^3 + 4e^2 - 5e $$

**Numerical Sanity Check:**
Approximating $e \approx 2.7182818$:
- $\mathbb{E}[N] = e^2 - e \approx 7.389056 - 2.718282 = \mathbf{4.670774}$
- $\mathbb{E}[N^2] = 5e^2 - 5e \approx 36.945280 - 13.591409 = 23.353871$
- $\text{Var}(N) = 23.353871 - (4.670774)^2 \approx 23.353871 - 21.816132 = \mathbf{1.537739}$
The expected stopping time takes roughly 4.67 draws, heavily anchored bounded by $N \ge 3$, proving the numbers intuitive and consistent.

### 5. Generalizing $\mathbb{E}[N_t]$ for Arbitrary Threshold $t > 0$
We seek an exact expression for $\mathbb{E}[N_t] = \sum_{n=0}^\infty \mathbb{P}(S_n \le t)$. Substituting the generalized Irwin-Hall distribution:
$$ \mathbb{E}[N_t] = \sum_{n=0}^\infty \sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t \rfloor} (-1)^k \binom{n}{k} \frac{(t-k)^n}{n!} $$
Since the summation over $k$ is finite and the series converges absolutely, we swap the order of integration/summation. Furthermore, $\binom{n}{k} = 0$ for $n < k$, which shifts our effective bounds for $n$:
$$ \mathbb{E}[N_t] = \sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t \rfloor} (-1)^k \sum_{n=k}^\infty \binom{n}{k} \frac{(t-k)^n}{n!} $$
Using the identity $\binom{n}{k} \frac{1}{n!} = \frac{1}{k!(n-k)!}$ and changing variables via $m = n - k$:
$$ \sum_{n=k}^\infty \frac{(t-k)^n}{k!(n-k)!} = \frac{1}{k!} \sum_{m=0}^\infty \frac{(t-k)^{m+k}}{m!} = \frac{(t-k)^k}{k!} \sum_{m=0}^\infty \frac{(t-k)^m}{m!} = \frac{(t-k)^k}{k!} e^{t-k} $$
Substituting this back yields the final compact generalization:
$$ \mathbb{E}[N_t] = \sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t \rfloor} (-1)^k \frac{(t-k)^k}{k!} e^{t-k} $$
Notice that if $t=2$, this gives $(-1)^0 \frac{2^0}{0!}e^2 + (-1)^1 \frac{1^1}{1!}e^1 + (-1)^2 \frac{0^2}{2!}e^0 = e^2 - e$, independently confirming our earlier derivation.

Reported answer cost: $0.0117579500 USD. Excludes retry and judging costs.

Let \(S_0=0\). For \(n\ge 1\) and \(x\ge0\), the standard simplex-volume inclusion-exclusion formula is
\[
P(S_n\le x)=\frac1{n!}\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor x\rfloor}(-1)^k\binom nk (x-k)^n,
\]
with \(\binom nk=0\) if \(k>n\). This comes from the volume \(x^n/n!\) of \(\{u_i\ge0,\sum u_i\le x\}\), subtracting the \(k\)-fold intersections where \(k\) coordinates exceed \(1\).

## 1. Distribution of \(S_n\) at threshold \(2\)

Let
\[
p_n=P(S_n\le 2)=P(N>n).
\]
For \(n=0\), \(S_0=0\), so \(p_0=1\). For \(n\ge1\), using \(x=2\),
\[
p_n=\frac1{n!}\left[2^n-n(1)^n+\binom n2 0^n\right].
\]
For \(n\ge1\), the last term is \(0\) (and for \(n=1\) it is also absent because \(\binom12=0\)). Hence
\[
p_n=\frac{2^n-n}{n!}\qquad(n\ge1).
\]
This also gives \(p_1=1\), \(p_2=1\), matching the boundary facts \(S_1<1<2\) and \(S_2<2\) a.s.

## 2. Tail-sum identities

Since \(N\) is a positive integer,
\[
E[N]=\sum_{n=0}^\infty P(N>n),
\]
and
\[
E[N^2]=\sum_{n=0}^\infty (2n+1)P(N>n),
\]
because \(N^2=1+3+\cdots+(2N-1)\).

Because \(S_n\) is increasing, \(N>n\) iff \(S_n\le2\). Thus \(P(N>n)=p_n\). Strict versus non-strict matters not here: for \(n\ge1\), \(S_n\) is continuous, so \(P(S_n=2)=0\); for \(n=0\), \(S_0=0<2\).

Therefore
\[
E[N]=\sum_{n=0}^\infty p_n.
\]
So
\[
E[N]=1+\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{2^n-n}{n!}
=1+\left(e^2-1\right)-e
=e^2-e.
\]
Thus
\[
\boxed{E[N]=e^2-e\approx4.670774270}.
\]

For the second moment,
\[
E[N^2]=1+\sum_{n=1}^\infty (2n+1)\frac{2^n-n}{n!}.
\]
Compute separately:
\[
\sum_{n=1}^\infty (2n+1)\frac{2^n}{n!}
=2\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{n2^n}{n!}+\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{2^n}{n!}
=2(2e^2)+(e^2-1)=5e^2-1.
\]
Also, with \(m=n-1\),
\[
\sum_{n=1}^\infty (2n+1)\frac{n}{n!}
=\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{2n+1}{(n-1)!}
=\sum_{m=0}^\infty \frac{2m+3}{m!}
=2e+3e=5e.
\]
Hence
\[
E[N^2]=1+(5e^2-1)-5e=5e^2-5e=5(e^2-e).
\]
Therefore
\[
\operatorname{Var}(N)=E[N^2]-(E[N])^2
=5(e^2-e)-(e^2-e)^2.
\]
So
\[
\boxed{\operatorname{Var}(N)=(e^2-e)\bigl(5-e^2+e\bigr)}
\]
and numerically
\[
\operatorname{Var}(N)\approx1.537739067.
\]

Sanity check: \(E[N]=4.670774270\), \(E[N^2]=23.353871352\), so the variance is positive and small, as expected since \(N\ge3\) and crossing near time \(3\)–\(5\) dominates.

## 3. General threshold \(t>0\)

Let
\[
N_t=\min\{n\ge1:S_n>t\},\qquad m=\lfloor t\rfloor.
\]
By the same tail argument,
\[
E[N_t]=\sum_{n=0}^\infty P(S_n\le t).
\]
For \(n\ge1\), using the inclusion-exclusion formula,
\[
P(S_n\le t)=\frac1{n!}\sum_{k=0}^m(-1)^k\binom nk(t-k)^n.
\]
Thus
\[
E[N_t]=1+\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac1{n!}\sum_{k=0}^m(-1)^k\binom nk(t-k)^n.
\]
Interchange the finite \(k\)-sum with the \(n\)-sum:
\[
E[N_t]=1+\sum_{k=0}^m \frac{(-1)^k}{k!}
\sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{(t-k)^n}{(n-k)!},
\]
with the convention that terms with \(n<k\) vanish. For \(k=0\), the inner sum is \(e^t-1\). For \(k\ge1\), it is \((t-k)^k e^{t-k}\). Hence
\[
E[N_t]=1+(e^t-1)+\sum_{k=1}^m(-1)^k\frac{(t-k)^k e^{t-k}}{k!}.
\]
Therefore
\[
\boxed{
E[N_t]=\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}
(-1)^k\frac{(t-k)^k e^{t-k}}{k!}.
}
\]
If \(t\) is an integer and \(k=t\), the term is \(0\) for \(t>0\). For \(t<1\), this gives \(E[N_t]=e^t\).

For \(t=2\), this reduces to
\[
E[N_2]=e^2-e,
\]
as before.

Strict versus non-strict again makes no difference: for every \(n\ge1\), \(S_n\) has a continuous distribution, so \(P(S_n=t)=0\). Thus using \(S_n>t\) or \(S_n\ge t\) in the definition of \(N_t\) gives the same moments.
